Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G1.1, G1.2, G1.3, G1.4, G1.7, G1.8, G1.10, G1.11). Report states client name is 'John'; Fact Find page 1 lists 'Alan'. Report states household expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find page 10 calculates detailed total monthly expenditure as £1,718.00. Report states 'typically earning around £300 per week net' as HGV driver income; Fact Find page 7 shows 'Net Basic Monthly Income £1,200.00' which converts to approximately £276.92 per week, a difference of approximately £23.08 per week. 11 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G1.1] Essential facts (personal details) stated in the report are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Client One: John Sullivan, age 69, married to Kim, age 65. Both in good health. Three grown-up children, all financially independent. John works full-time as HGV driver earning typically £300 per week net, with additional £20 per week from Sea Cadets volunteer role. Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net. John in receipt of full State Pension £230 per week; Kim will receive full State Pension around £230 per week at State Pension age. Household expenditure approximately £1,700 per month. Cash savings around £6,000. Combined pension arrangements approximately £110,000. John expects to retire within next two years; Kim intends to continue working until age 66.

    File:     Fact Find (Test 1) dated 19/09/2025 shows: Client One name listed as 'Alan' on page 1, not 'John'. Kim listed as spouse with date of birth 07/05/1960, age 65. Employment listed as HGV Driver with CDH Recruitment. Net Basic Monthly Income £1,200.00 (page 7). State Pension Monthly £230.00 (page 9). Additional income: State Pension £230.00 monthly listed. Total monthly income £1,430.00. Expenditure details show Rent £600, Council Tax £200, Electricity £120, Water £60, Food & Personal Care £300, Car/Travelling £150, Building Insurance £25, Entertainment £100 = £1,718.00 monthly total (page 10). Cash assets not explicitly stated in extracted passages. Household retirement planning objective states 'needs household income of £1,700 net per month' and 'probably retire in the next 2 years' (page 9). Client Agreement Privacy document [P3] names party as 'Sullivan' without first name. Driving Licence Ref SULLI506076JA9SL seen 08/12/2025 [P5].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states household expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find page 10 calculates detailed total monthly expenditure as £1,718.00

      - Report states 'typically earning around £300 per week net' as HGV driver income; Fact Find page 7 shows 'Net Basic Monthly Income £1,200.00' which converts to approximately £276.92 per week, a difference of approximately £23.08 per week

    The first name discrepancy between 'John' in the suitability report and 'Alan' in the Fact Find is material. The Fact Find page 1 clearly labels 'Client One: Alan' while the suitability report consistently refers to 'John'. The driving licence reference and client agreement identify 'Sullivan' but do not establish the given name. This is a potential identity mismatch that undermines confidence in whether the report describes the correct client.

   

    The household expenditure figures differ: report states 'approximately £1,700' while Fact Find page 10 calculates £1,718.00 as the detailed total. This is within a reasonable rounding tolerance (1% difference) and the report's use of 'approximately' suggests awareness of this range. This falls within low severity rounding.

   

    The HGV driver income stated as 'typically £300 per week net' in the report differs from the Fact Find Basic Monthly Income of £1,200.00 (£276.92 per week). The word 'typically' on page 3 of the report suggests awareness of variability. However, Fact Find page 7 shows this as 'Net Basic Monthly Income' not variable income, and page 14 cashflow section treats it as £300 per week assumption. The report's use of 'typically' and the deterministic cashflow assumption create an internal inconsistency documented in the extraction (expectedButAbsent record).

   

    The pension value discrepancy is significant: report asserts 'approximately £110,000' but the sum of five individual arrangements extracted from the report itself is £116,998.47. Page 9 of the suitability report states transfer value as £110,185, but the individual plan values sum to £116,998.47. This £6,813.47 shortfall is unexplained in the evidence and was flagged in the extraction as an internalInconsistency. The derived figures confirm this arithmetic contradiction: the sum does not reconcile.

   

    The guard regarding spouse details applies: Kim (age 65) is a spouse not the advised client, so her details should not be treated as a mismatch on the primary client. Her details are consistent across documents.

   

    The identity of the primary client ('John' vs 'Alan') cannot be resolved from the passages provided and represents a material concern that affects the entire assessment of personal detail consistency.

    The comparison cannot be performed. The Fact Find (category B) records income and expenditure but does not contain a tax status section or record of the client's allowance position. Without a document that records the client's actual tax position—such as a tax summary, provider documentation, or fact find entry—this assertion remains unsupported by the evidence pack. Fact Find [P1] and [P4] show income table with employment status 'Employed', occupation 'HGV Driver', employer 'CDH Recruitment', net basic monthly income £1,200.00, state pension monthly £230.00, total monthly £1,430.00. [P24] cashflow shows JS (age 69/65 pairs through 2025-2056) retiring 2027 at age 71, KS retiring 2026 at age 66. [P17] confirms Kim contributes '£1,100 per month from her employment' and 'will also receive a full State Pension'.

      - Report infers John's retirement date as 2027-10-14 (age 71 in 2027); planning document [P7] and [P23] confirm retirement age 71 in 2027, which is consistent

      - Report states John earns '£300 per week net' as HGV driver; Fact Find [P4] and [P5] state net basic monthly income £1,200.00, which equals £276.92 per week (£1,200.00 / 4.33), not £300 per week

      - Report states John receives '£20 per week' from Sea Cadets; [P8] corroborates this value exactly

      - Report states Kim receives '£1,100 per month' employment income; [P17] corroborates this value exactly

      - Report states Kim will receive 'state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67'; [P17] confirms she 'will also receive a full State Pension' but does not specify the amount or age eligibility; [P3] states 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67', corroborating the amount and age

    The primary employment details for John (HGV driver, employed) and Kim (employed) are present and largely consistent across documents. However, critical numerical discrepancies exist regarding income figures: (1) The report asserts John earns '£300 per week net' but the Fact Find records £1,200.00 net basic monthly income, which converts to approximately £276.92 per week, a difference of £23.08 or 7.7%. (2) The report states John receives 'state pension income of approximately £230 per week' but the Fact Find shows £230.00 monthly, implying either the report misquoted the frequency or made a unit conversion error. £230 monthly equals £53.08 weekly, not £230 weekly. This is a material discrepancy. The word 'typically' applied to the £300 per week income on page 3 of the report suggests variability, but the cashflow model on page 14 treats it deterministically without acknowledgement of this variance. The employer name discrepancy (report says 'agency', Fact Find says 'CDH Recruitment') is minor — 'agency' is a descriptor; CDH Recruitment is the actual employer name. Retirement dates are correctly inferred and corroborated by planning documents. Kim's income of £1,100 per month is fully corroborated. The state pension amounts and ages for both are corroborated but the report contains a frequency error regarding John's state pension amount.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Report asserts: HGV driver employment income £300/week net (monthly equivalent £1,300); Sea Cadets volunteer income £20/week net (monthly equivalent £86.67); Kim employment income £1,100/month net; State Pension £230/week net for client (monthly equivalent £1,000); State Pension £230/week for Kim from 2026 (monthly equivalent £1,000); Household expenditure £1,700/month; Total monthly income £4,486.67; Monthly surplus £2,786.67; Total assets £6,000; Total liabilities £0.

    File:     Evidence shows: HGV driver income £300/week net [P23]; Sea Cadets income £20/week net [P23]; Kim employment income £1,100/month net [P23]; State Pension £230/month from fact-find [P8]; State Pension £11,973 p/a (approximately £998/month) [P23]; Household expenditure £1,718/month [P1]; Total net monthly income £1,430 from fact-find [P1]; Fact-find shows monthly disposable income £-288 (deficit) [P1]; Cash savings £6,000 [P1]; No liabilities [P1]; Suitability report states annual expenditure approximately £1,700 [P7] and state pension income of £11,973 per annum [P7].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report monthly equivalent for £300/week calculated as £1,300 but fact-find shows employed basic income £1,200/month [P8], creating a £100 discrepancy

      - Report calculates £230/week state pension as £1,000/month equivalent, but fact-find shows £230/month [P8] and planning document shows £11,973 p/a (£998/month) [P23], not £1,000/month

      - Report states household expenditure £1,700/month but fact-find calculated total household expenditure is £1,718/month [P1]

      - Report total monthly income £4,486.67 is not corroborated; fact-find shows total net monthly income £1,430 [P1]

      - Report calculates monthly surplus £2,786.67 from stated figures, but fact-find shows monthly disposable income £-288 (deficit) [P1], a £3,074.67 contradiction

      - Report does not incorporate Kim's state pension in current income figures despite stating she will receive £230/week when she reaches state pension age in 2026, but the planning documents show Kim's state pension is already included in projections [P9]

    Multiple material discrepancies exist between the report's income and expenditure assertions and the supporting evidence. The most significant is the total monthly income: the report states £4,486.67 but the fact-find demonstrates total net monthly income of £1,430, a £3,056.67 difference. The report's calculation of £300/week as £1,300/month is correct per the guard (52 weeks ÷ 12 months = 4.333), but the fact-find employment income column shows £1,200/month [P8], creating ambiguity about whether the £300/week income was actually achieved or the fact-find figure is incomplete. The state pension conversion shows the report treating £230/week as £1,000/month, yet the fact-find lists £230/month [P8] and the planning documents show £11,973 p/a, which calculates to approximately £998/month—neither supporting the £1,000 figure. The household expenditure discrepancy between £1,700 stated in the report and £1,718 calculated in fact-find is minor (1.0%) but material when the report relies on specific figures. Most critically, the report asserts a surplus of £2,786.67 monthly, but the fact-find explicitly shows total monthly disposable income as £-288 (a deficit). This is not reconcilable by rounding or frequency conversion and suggests the report either used incomplete income data, excluded the actual household expenditure, or made a fundamental calculation error. The consolidated household expenditure guard permits comparing totals only rather than line-by-line, so the £1,718 vs £1,700 difference is observable but the £1,718 figure appears to be the properly calculated total from the detailed breakdown [P1]. The report's income figures lack supporting evidence in the fact-find, which shows significantly lower employment income (£1,200 vs £1,300 claimed) and does not reconcile to the report's stated total.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The extraction identified six internal inconsistencies: (1) People's Pension value £6,601.55 is consistent but recommendation to 'Retain' contradicts objective OBJ3 to consolidate pensions; (2) Risk rating shows questionnaire result of 4 vs agreed/confirmed rating of 5 with unclear sequence; (3) Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% (0.21% + 0.22%), but text claims new plan 0.06% more expensive, contradicting the calculation; (4) Fund allocations show same funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal Map 50%) but with slight naming variance between pages 2 and 12; (5) Employment income stated as 'typically £300 per week net' on page 3 but assumed as deterministic '£300 per week' in cashflow on page 14; (6) Individual existing arrangement values sum to £116,998.47 but stated total transfer value is £110,185, a discrepancy of £6,813.47.

    File:     This check compares the report against itself and does not rely on external evidence. The internal inconsistencies are drawn from the extraction report's own analysis of the canonical model extracted from the suitability report. No supporting document passages are searched or cited; the comparison is internal to the report's stated figures, narratives and calculations.

    Discrepancies:

      - Aviva charge contradiction: page 5 states 0.50% existing; page 11 recommendation table shows 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total; page 6 text states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the arithmetic showing new charge is 0.06% cheaper (0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% reduction)

      - Transfer value arithmetic: sum of five existing arrangements (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002.00) = £116,998.47 but page 9 states total transfer value as £110,185, creating unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47

      - Employment income qualifier: page 3 describes income as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' suggesting variability, but page 14 cashflow assumptions section lists 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' without the qualifier 'typically', treating it as deterministic input

      - Risk rating sequence ambiguity: page 4 contains two statements—'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and later 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'—creating unclear causal sequence between questionnaire result (4), adviser assessment/agreement, and client confirmation

      - Fund name consistency: page 2 recommendation summary lists 'CT Universal Map Balanced' and 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' while page 12 lists 'CT Universal MAP' and 'HSBC' with allocations 50% each; the fuller names on page 2 appear to be the same funds but naming inconsistency exists across pages

      - Retention vs consolidation logic: recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan - £6,601.55' but objective OBJ3 directs consolidation of pensions; the retention of this one plan while consolidating/switching four others is not reconciled

    All six identified discrepancies are internal contradictions within the report itself and can be assessed without external evidence. Severity assessment: (1) The Aviva charge contradiction is material—page 6 asserts the new plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, but the fee table on page 11 and the arithmetic show the opposite (0.44% new vs 0.50% old = 0.06% cheaper). This is a high-severity self-contradiction on a figure that could drive the recommendation. (2) The transfer value shortfall of £6,813.47 is material and unexplained; it appears the report may have excluded one or more arrangements from the total without stating why, or there is an arithmetic error. This is high severity as transfer value is a driver of the recommendation. (3) The employment income qualifier ('typically' vs deterministic) is a moderate concern; the cashflow model treats £300/week as fixed but the narrative suggests it varies, which affects reliability of the financial projection. (4) The risk rating sequence is ambiguous but does not constitute a numerical contradiction—both statements conclude to risk rating 5, so the final position is clear even if the path to it is unclear; this is low-to-moderate concern affecting the documentation of consent. (5) Fund naming inconsistency (Universal Map vs MAP; full name vs abbreviation) appears to refer to the same funds and is a low-severity presentation issue. (6) The retention of People's Pension contradicts the stated consolidation objective, but the extraction notes this is a logical/strategic inconsistency rather than a data mismatch on the figure itself—still material and moderate severity. The core numerical contradictions are the Aviva charge statement (high severity) and the transfer value shortfall (high severity).

The guard on frequency conversion is satisfied (the 0.4% gap between 52/12 and 4.33×12 is arithmetic, not inconsistency), but this is a substantive discrepancy between what the report extracts as the income figure (£1,300) and what the Fact Find itself records (£1,200). The report also includes Kim's £1,100/month employment income and future State Pension £230/week in its five-stream total, but the Fact Find income section totals only the primary household income at £1,430. The report states expenditure as £1,700 but Fact Find explicitly shows £1,718.00 as calculated total. The word 'typically' on page 3 regarding the £300/week income suggests variability, but the cashflow model treats it as deterministic. The conversion methodology is arithmetically sound, but the foundational income figures differ between report extraction and Fact Find source, creating a reconciliation failure at the base level.

Outcome: Potential Concern

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts three objectives with explicit priority ranks: (1) 'Ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime' (priority 1); (2) 'Retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month' (priority 2); (3) 'Consolidate and streamline pension arrangements' (priority 3). All three are marked as client-stated.

    File:     Evidence corroborates all three objectives. Fact Find [P11] records: 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' Suitability Report [P4] restates: 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime. You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month. You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' Financial planning documents [P7] show timeline with 'Retire (John)' at 2027 (2 years from fact find date 2025) and '[Retire (Kim)' at 2026.

    All three stated objectives are directly evidenced in the fact find and suitability report itself. The priority order (lifestyle affordability, retirement income target, pension consolidation) aligns with the substance of client discussions: the client expressed primary concern about organizing pensions to support a defined retirement goal (£1,700 net monthly within 2 years), which inherently prioritizes achieving that income target and ensuring ongoing affordability. The consolidation objective is stated as a supporting aim (to 'help him get his head around what he has'), reflecting lower priority. Wording differences between report and fact find are paraphrasing only and do not signal inconsistency. The 2-year retirement horizon is consistently stated across all documents (fact find completed 19/09/2025, timeline shows retirement in 2027). The household income target of £1,700 net per month is stated identically in fact find [P11] and suitability report [P4]. Lifestyle affordability is discussed in context of the couple's current expenditure (fact find [P6] shows total monthly household expenditure of £1,718), against which the retirement income target sits.

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) a retirement target date of 2027-10-14 (October 2027); (2) a term of 2 years to retirement; (3) three time horizon values: LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm; and (4) an intended retirement age of 66.

      - Report asserts retirement age 66, but P18 (Zurich illustration dated 24 September 2025) shows retirement date 7 June 2031 at age 75, a difference of 9 years.

    Multiple retirement horizons exist in the file, and the comparison is compromised by the presence of two clients (designated as JS and KS in P3, with ages 66 and 71 in the retirement dates). The suitability report is client-first and asserts a single retirement target of October 2027 with age 66 and a 2-year term. However: (1) P3 shows one client ('JS') retiring at age 66 in 2026, and another client ('KS') retiring at age 71 in 2027. The October 2027 date could align with KS's 2027 retirement date, but the report claims age 66, which aligns with JS's age but the wrong year (2026 vs 2027). (2) P1 pension switch analysis uses age 75 with a 5.5-year term, which contradicts the 2-year assertion. The guard states that switch analysis routinely shows a different horizon and should be collected before judging; here the spread is significant (2 years vs 5.5 years). (3) P18 Zurich illustration dated 24 September 2025 explicitly shows 'Retirement date 7 June 2031 (age 75)', which yields approximately 5.75 years to retirement from the illustration date, not 2 years. (4) P2 Zurich policy states both 'Selected retirement age 65' and 'Planned Retirement Age for this plan is 75', creating ambiguity about contractual vs intended retirement age (the guard notes that selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date). The 2-year term in the report cannot be reconciled with the 5-year-plus terms shown in multiple illustrations and projections. The time horizons in the report (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) are stated without explicit linkage to objectives, making them generic assertions lacking specificity. No passage corroborates that these three distinct time horizons are associated with three distinct client objectives; the canonical model extracted these as separate values but provides no evidence they serve different goals.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts an emergency access need: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8). The report states the client holds £6,000 in liquid capital as an emergency fund and confirms 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' (page 3). The report also states 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' (page 8).

    File:     The evidence shows £6,000 in liquid assets (savings account) held separately from pension investments across multiple documents [P1, P4, P5]. The fact-find [P3] records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 against net monthly income of £1,430.00, creating a monthly deficit of £288.00. The financial planning model [P8, P13] shows employment income of £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly) for John and £13,200 p/a (£1,100 monthly) for Kim until retirement in 2026-2027. The planning projections [P13, P17] demonstrate liquid assets remain available throughout the baseline scenario. The report discusses flexi-access drawdown (FAD) as a means to access pension funds with tax-free lump sum options and flexible income withdrawals [P2], and confirms pension access takes 10-15 working days [P2 implies this is standard for pension drawdown]. No evidence records ad-hoc access requirements beyond the general emergency access discussion.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client 'confirmed' sufficient liquid capital for emergencies but does not quantify what amount the client considered sufficient or on what basis (days of expenditure, specific events, or advisor assessment)

      - Report references 'instance access funds' (page 8) as comfort around liquid cash but this appears to be a typographical error; no clear definition of what 'instance access' means in the client's context

      - The £6,000 emergency fund represents approximately 3.5 months of the household monthly deficit (-£288), but the report does not explain the adequacy rationale against any identified emergency scenario

      - Report asserts pension drawdown access takes 10-15 working days but does not explain whether this timeframe was acceptable to the client or whether it meets any stated ad-hoc access need

      - No evidence of discussion with the client regarding foreseeable access needs beyond emergency (e.g., planned major expenditure, health-related access, support for family members)

      - Report describes flexi-access drawdown as offering 'flexible and tax-efficient' withdrawal but does not link this feature to any specific client objective or access timeline

    The comparison can be performed. The report addresses emergency access by confirming £6,000 in liquid reserves and stating the client confirmed this is sufficient. Evidence corroborates the £6,000 amount held in liquid assets [P1, P4, P5] and the 10-15 working day pension drawdown timeframe is a standard industry fact stated in the report [P2] rather than a client-specific assertion. However, several material gaps exist: (1) The sufficiency of £6,000 is asserted as 'confirmed' by the client but no evidence shows the client's reasoning or what emergency scenarios they considered. The fact-find [P3] records a current monthly shortfall of -£288, making it unclear whether £6,000 was sized relative to this deficit, specific life events, or a rule of thumb. (2) The report does not establish whether the client identified any planned or ad-hoc access requirements during the advice process beyond general emergency access. The planning projections [P13, P17] show stable liquid assets throughout retirement, but no evidence shows discussion with the client about anticipated withdrawals or life-event-triggered access (e.g., health costs, family support). (3) The report states the client is 'comfortable with the concept' of having 'liquid cash' for 'instance access' [P2], but this phrasing is ambiguous and does not clarify what specific access scenarios were discussed. (4) The recommendation to consolidate five pension arrangements into one Aviva pension via flexi-access drawdown is presented as offering 'flexibility' but is not explicitly tied to any stated client access need or objective. The guards note: 'No access needs identified' is a valid evidenced answer; absence is not failure to ask. However, the report does assert that emergency access was confirmed sufficient, placing the burden on supporting that claim with evidence of what was discussed. The canonical model shows no quote or detail about the basis for £6,000 sufficiency. The financial planning model shows asset projections but does not document client conversations about access timing or triggers. On balance, the access requirement has been partly addressed (emergency fund confirmed) but lacks sufficient evidence of client-specific reasoning or discussion of planned/ad-hoc needs.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Client retirement expected within two years (2027-10-14) with loss of HGV employment income (£1,300/month) and Sea Cadets income (£86.67/month), total impact -£1,386.67/month net; (2) Kim's retirement at age 66 (2026-03-26) with loss of employment income -£1,100/month net; (3) Kim's State Pension commencement at age 66 (2026-03-26) providing +£1,000/month net (described as 'around £230 per week'); (4) Inheritance Tax regime change for pensions effective 2027-04-06, with no quantified financial impact stated but noted as affecting death benefits and drawdown funds.

    File:     The case file evidence supports the following: (1) From P1 (Suitability Report), client states 'You expect to retire within the next two years' and objective is 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month.' From P4 (planning tool), retirement ages marked as John 71 in 2027 and Kim 66 in 2026. From P23 (Fact Find), Basic Income from 'Employed, CDH Recruitment' shows £0.00to £1,200.00 monthly, and State Pension £0.00 to £230.00 monthly; Income Changes section states 'No' to foreseeable changes. (2) From P4, timeline explicitly shows 'Retire (Kim)' in 2026 and 'Retire (John)' in 2027. (3) From P4, Kim's state pension age marked as 66. From P1, objective states 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'. (4) From P1, the report quotes: 'From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT' - this is a legislative change affecting all pension holders, not a client-specific foreseeable change. (5) From P8 and P14 (Baseline scenario income statement first year), Employment Income shown as £16,640 for one party and £13,200 combined from another source, totalling £29,840; State Pension £11,973 combined.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client retirement expected 2027-10-14; planning tool (P4) shows retirement 'Retire (John)' in 2027 without specific date, consistent in year but specific date not corroborated.

      - Report derives client employment income loss as £1,300/month HGV driving; Fact Find (P23) shows employment income of £1,200/month from 'Employed, CDH Recruitment', a £100/month discrepancy.

      - Report states client has Sea Cadets income of £86.67/month; no evidence of this income source appears in Fact Find (P23) or income statements (P8, P14).

      - Report states Kim's State Pension will be 'around £230 per week' (approximately £1,000/month); Fact Find (P23) shows State Pension £230.00 monthly, not weekly, creating ambiguity about whether report's conversion is accurate.

      - Fact Find (P23) Income Changes section states 'No' to foreseeable changes, contradicting the report's assertion of multiple significant foreseeable changes (two retirements, income losses).

      - Report quantifies Kim's income loss as £1,100/month; Fact Find shows Kim's employment income as part of combined household income but does not isolate Kim's specific employment income amount.

      - Report derives total household employment income impact from retirements as -£1,386.67/month (£1,300 + £86.67); Fact Find sources do not separately itemise these components to verify the derivation.

      - IHT regime change cited as foreseeable change; this is a legislative change affecting all UK pension holders effective 2027-04-06, not a client-specific foreseeable change unique to this client's circumstances.

    The report identifies four foreseeable life changes. Three are client-specific: two retirements (client and spouse) and associated income changes, plus commencement of State Pension for spouse. The fourth—IHT regime change for pensions—is a legislative change affecting all pension holders, not a foreseeable change specific to this client's circumstances. Per the guard at 'How to compare': Legislative changes affecting everyone are not client-specific foreseeable changes, so this item does not constitute a finding on this requirement. On the three substantive client-specific changes: (1) Retirement timing is corroborated: report says 'within two years', P4 planning tool shows retirement in 2027 (consistent with document dated 2025), and P1 objective confirms 'retire in 2 years'. (2) Kim's retirement at age 66 in 2026 is corroborated by P4 timeline and P1 report text. (3) Kim's State Pension at age 66 is corroborated by P4 (state pension age 66) and P1 (around £230/week). However, material discrepancies exist on income amounts: Report claims HGV employment income of £1,300/month, but P23 Fact Find shows employment income £1,200/month—a £100 discrepancy. Report asserts Sea Cadets income of £86.67/month; no evidence supports this source in any document (P23 Fact Find lists no such income). Report converts Kim's State Pension to 'around £230 per week', but P23 shows £230 monthly, not weekly—the report's conversion implies approximately £1,000/month net whereas £230/month is what the evidence states. The Fact Find (P23) Income Changes section answers 'No' to foreseeable income changes, creating direct tension with the report's assertion of two retirements and material income losses. The planning tools (P2, P3, P4, P9, P11, P12, P14, P20, P21) incorporate retirement events for both John (2027) and Kim (2026) in the timeline and cashflow projections, showing these changes are modelled, but the underlying income sources and amounts contain discrepancies with the Fact Find. The report's financial impact figures cannot be fully verified against source documents due to these component-level discrepancies. No vulnerability overlay applies; no vulnerability drivers or characteristics recorded in the evidence materially affect the assessment of whether foreseeable changes are stated and evidenced.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report links six recommendations to stated objectives: (1) Switch Aviva pension to OBJ3; (2) Switch Scottish Widows to OBJ3; (3) Switch Zurich pension to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (4) Switch Standard Life pension to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (5) Retain People's Pension to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (6) Recommended new Aviva Pension Portfolio to OBJ1 and OBJ2. The recommended plan is a Personal Pension with £110,185 transfer value, selected retirement age 71, and projections to age 75. The client is aged 69, expects to retire within two years, and has access to flexi-access drawdown from age 55 (currently accessible). Objectives are stated as having time horizons: OBJ1 'LongTerm', OBJ2 'MediumTerm', OBJ3 'MediumTerm'.

    File:     The case file shows the client is aged 69, married to Kim aged 65. Client 'typically earning around £300 per week net' with additional income from volunteer role. Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net. Client expects to retire 'within the next two years' (age ~71), while Kim 'intends to continue working until she reaches 66' (age 66 in 2026). Household expenditure approximately £1,700 per month. Client holds savings of around £6,000 and 'several pension arrangements with a combined value of approximately £110,000'. Client 'has confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies'. The pension switch report projects values at age 75 under three growth scenarios. The Key Features document states normal minimum pension age is currently 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028. Flexi-access drawdown is available from age 55. The financial planning documents (intelliflo) model expenditure and cashflow to age 100 and show 'Required Withdrawal (Pensions)' beginning around age 75-76.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client 'expects to retire within the next two years' (implying age ~71) but then recommends a Personal Pension with 'selected retirement age 71' and projections to age 75, creating ambiguity about whether the retirement date (71) is the access date or a distinct planning horizon

      - Report links three recommendations (Zurich, Standard Life, and People's Pension retention) to OBJ2 and/or OBJ3 with 'MediumTerm' time horizons, but does not explicitly state what these objectives are or whether a medium-term horizon aligns with a retirement in two years followed by drawdown to age 75+

      - Report recommends consolidating five existing pensions into one new Aviva Pension Portfolio but retains People's Pension (£6,601.55) separately; the rationale for retention versus consolidation is not explicit in the linked objectives

      - Report asserts £110,185 transfer value into new solution but derived figures show sum of all five arrangements is £116,998.47, creating a £6,813.47 unexplained discrepancy that affects whether the recommendation delivers the stated transfer amount

      - Report does not explicitly address whether the client's stated two-year retirement timeline and need for emergency liquidity (£6,000 saved) reconcile with a medium-term pension investment strategy or whether access requirements are met by the recommended wrapper and withdrawal mechanism

    The report asserts that six recommendations are linked to three objectives with stated time horizons (OBJ1 LongTerm, OBJ2 MediumTerm, OBJ3 MediumTerm). The case file establishes that the client is aged 69, expects to retire within two years (age ~71), and has a medium-term life expectancy. The recommended solution is a Personal Pension with access from age 55 (already accessible at 69) offering flexi-access drawdown. A critical ambiguity arises: the report states objectives have a 'MediumTerm' time horizon, and the client intends to retire within two years and begin drawing benefits at age 71 (four years from now). This is not inherently inconsistent, as 'medium-term' could span 4-6 years, but the report does not explicitly reconcile the stated time horizons with the retirement date or with the age 75 projection point used in the pension switch analysis. The report recommends retaining the People's Pension (OBJ2 and OBJ3) while switching four other arrangements into the new Aviva plan, but provides no objective-specific rationale for this bifurcation. The canonical extract shows linked objectives by recommendation but does not quote or evidence why each objective requires each action. The guard instructs that boilerplate linkages do not suffice; a specific objective must be named. The report does state 'I have recommended a Personal Pension for the following reasons' [P2] but the passage does not name the specific objectives served. The access mechanism is a Personal Pension with flexi-access drawdown; this carries no restriction at age 69 and is suitable for drawdown from age 71. However, the report does not explicitly state what liquidity or ad-hoc access needs the client has disclosed, nor does it address the £6,000 emergency fund held separately. The derived figures reveal a material discrepancy: the sum of all five existing arrangements is £116,998.47 but the recommended transfer value is £110,185, a shortfall of £6,813.47. The extraction noted this internal inconsistency. This means either some arrangements are not being transferred (not stated in the recommendations) or the transfer value calculation is understated. This undermines whether the recommendations will deliver the full existing portfolio value to the new solution. The financial planning documents show the client's expenditure modelled deterministically at £24,001 annually in year 1, with required pension withdrawals beginning around age 75-76 in the proposed scenario, aligning with the report's projection to age 75. However, the report does not explicitly link the medium-term objective to this drawdown timeline or explain how the recommended allocation (CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%) serves objectives at a specific horizon. A guard states that the access mechanism, not wrapper name, should be checked: a pension for a 69-year-old carries no access restriction, and flexi-access drawdown is accessible on demand. This is met. However, the guard also requires that boilerplate linkages be rejected; the extraction found that recommendations are linked by objective ID but the suitability report does not quote specific reasoning that ties each objective to each recommendation beyond stating 'I have recommended a Personal Pension'. The specific objectives themselves are not evidenced in the case file passages provided; only the generic time horizons appear in the canonical model. Without access to the stated objectives and their specific milestones, the comparison cannot fully verify whether each recommendation is appropriate for its linked objective. The report asserts a Personal Pension is suitable but the case file does not contain a detailed needs analysis recording the client's specific goals, priorities, or access requirements beyond retirement timing and emergency liquidity.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states three client objectives: (1) 'Ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime' (page 3, 'Your Objectives' section); (2) 'Retire within two years with household income of £1,700 net per month' (page 3); (3) 'Consolidate and streamline pension arrangements' (page 3). All three are marked as client-stated in the canonical model. The report also discloses planning assumptions including general expenditure inflation at 2%, savings inflation at 2%, state pensions inflation at 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions inflation at 5%, mid-growth rate for critical yield analysis at 2.94%, and surplus income percentage saved at 0%.

    File:     The Fact Find (P3) records under 'Retirement Planning' that the client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The Fact Find also records the client 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has.' File notes (P9) confirm the client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and state the client is 'not reliant upon your pension funds.' The Fact Find (P14) shows total net monthly income of £1,430.00 against total monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, with a disposable income of minus £288.00. The Fact Find (P5) records 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No'. The Risk Profile (P16) contains no additional notes on investment target or agreed risk level.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts objective to 'ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime' as client-stated, but the Fact Find (P3, P5) and file notes record no such statement; instead they record the client expects no changes in expenditure and has negative current disposable income of £288.00 per month against stated retirement income target of £1,700 net per month.

      - The Fact Find (P14) records total net monthly income of £1,430.00 with total monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, meaning current household income is £288.00 below current expenditure. The report's objective to 'ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime' is inconsistent with this recorded financial position and no evidence shows how this gap is to be bridged through the recommended pension strategy.

      - The report states retirement horizon is 'within two years' (aligned with Fact Find P3 'next 2 years') but the file notes (P9) record the client 'wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period,' which contradicts a two-year horizon.

    The first objective ('ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime') is stated in the report with provenance to page 3 'Your Objectives' section, but the evidence pack contains no client statement supporting this objective. The Fact Find records the client's stated objectives as retirement within two years at £1,700 net monthly income and pension consolidation, but not lifetime affordability of current lifestyle. The File notes confirm these two objectives explicitly. The first objective appears to be an adviser inference or planning assumption, not a client statement. Under the guard 'An objective the adviser inferred and the client then confirmed at a meeting is stated, not unsupported — read the file notes before calling it unevidenced,' the file notes do not record client confirmation of this first objective. The second objective (retire in two years at £1,700/month) is directly evidenced in the Fact Find (P3). The third objective (consolidate pensions) is directly evidenced in the Fact Find (P3). The planning assumptions (inflation rates and surplus income treatment) are disclosed in the report and supported by the 'Cashflow Assumptions' section on page 19 of the report itself. However, a material inconsistency exists: the file notes (P9) state the client wishes 'for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period,' but the stated retirement horizon is two years. This temporal contradiction between the two-year retirement goal and the ten-year investment growth horizon is not reconciled in the evidence. Additionally, the current negative disposable income of £288.00 per month creates a tension with the stated objective to 'ensure continued ability to afford lifestyle through lifetime' that is not addressed.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.4, G3.6, G3.7, G3.10). Report states household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; [P14] Fact Find shows Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure of £1,718.00. Report asserts 'Medium' capacity for loss rating; no passage explicitly uses the term 'Medium' or identifies a formal CFL rating scale or classification system. CFL assessment appears to be derived from income and expenditure analysis rather than a separate CFL questionnaire or formal assessment methodology. 8 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts: assessed code 4 (Cautious Balanced per questionnaire); agreed code 5 (Balanced); label Balanced; scale 1-10. Page 3 of the suitability report states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' It also states 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'.

    File:     P1 (Risk profile report) shows questionnaire result: '4 Cautious Balanced' as natural risk level. P1 also shows agreed risk level: '5 Balanced'. P3 (Suitability Report) confirms 'You completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire' with result 4, and states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' P3 also confirms the agreed rating is used for recommendations: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor'. P6 (Risk profile report) shows agreed risk level 5 Balanced with full descriptor. P12 (SIPP research report) states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' P4 confirms 'Agreed risk level: 5 Balanced'. The scale is consistently identified as 1-10 in P1, P2, and related passages.

    The report's assessed code (4), agreed code (5), label (Balanced), and scale (1-10) are all corroborated by the evidence. P1 confirms the questionnaire returned a score of 4 on the 1-10 scale, categorised as 'Cautious Balanced'. P1 and P6 both document an agreed risk level of 5, labelled 'Balanced', on the same 1-10 scale. P3 explicitly records the override: the questionnaire result (4) did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite, so adviser and client agreed to increase to rating 5. This override is evidenced, explained, and the client's confirmation is recorded ('You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5' and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'). The asset allocation recommended (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%, both rated risk level 5 per P17 and P12) is mapped to the agreed risk level 5. No contradiction exists between the questionnaire result and the agreed rating — the override is properly documented. The scale used throughout is consistent (1-10). All figures in the report match the evidence: assessed code 4 matches [P1], agreed code 5 matches [P1], [P6], [P12], [P4], and label 'Balanced' is confirmed across multiple passages.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts a 'Medium' capacity for loss rating.       - Report states household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; [P14] Fact Find shows Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure of £1,718.00

      - Report asserts 'Medium' capacity for loss rating; no passage explicitly uses the term 'Medium' or identifies a formal CFL rating scale or classification system

      - CFL assessment appears to be derived from income and expenditure analysis rather than a separate CFL questionnaire or formal assessment methodology

    The income and expenditure components cited in the CFL assessment are all corroborated by the evidence. The client's financial position is strong: employment income plus spouse's income totals approximately £4,400 net monthly (£300 + £20 + £1,100 per week converted), State Pension income is secure and documented, household expenditure is approximately £1,700 (or £1,718 per Fact Find), and liquid assets of £6,000 exist with no liabilities. This financial position substantiates a finding that the client has capacity to absorb investment losses without detriment to day-to-day living. However, a material gap exists: no passage defines or evidences what 'Medium' capacity for loss means, nor is a CFL questionnaire or formal assessment framework retrieved. The guard states 'A CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning is not evidenced merely because the words appear.' The financial reasoning is present (income exceeds expenditure, savings held, no liabilities), but the classification itself ('Medium') is not grounded in a named or identified scale in the evidence. The extraction metadata indicates the CFL provenance quote was 'PresentButUnparseable', suggesting the original document contained CFL language that could not be extracted reliably. The report extraction also records an ambiguity: 'Page 4 describes capacity for loss as medium but justification refers to income comfortably meeting expenditure with savings and pension funds as basis. Unclear whether medium is a formal classification or descriptive.' This ambiguity is not resolved in the evidence pack. The factual basis for CFL (income, expenditure, savings, liabilities) is supported; the rating label ('Medium') and its scale are not.

The objectives recorded as LongTerm, MediumTerm, and MediumTerm do not reconcile clearly with a stated 10-year term when the risk profile justification cites 2 years. The discrepancy between the 10-year canonical extraction and the 2-year evidence is not explained or reconciled in any document.   [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a risk override from rating 4 (questionnaire result) to rating 5 (agreed and confirmed). The justification is 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite.' Client acknowledgement includes two statements: (1) 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.' (2) 'You have confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period and feel a balanced approach to this investment would achieve this goal.' The override is documented as direction 'Increased' from code 4 to code 5.

    File:     The Risk Profile Report (category D) confirms the natural risk level as 4 'Cautious Balanced' from the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. It shows an agreed risk level of 5 'Balanced' and states: 'In the discussion we had, you were shown a projection, based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon. We then discussed this projection and altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved.' The suitability report (category I) restates the override: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' File notes (category C) contain an ATR wording note stating 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' However, the same file notes also contain a conflicting statement: 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' — which contradicts the agreed rating of 5 stated elsewhere.

    Discrepancies:

      - File notes state client agreed to 'Risk rating of 6' while all other sources (Risk Profile Report, Suitability Report) confirm agreed rating of 5

      - Suitability Report states client 'wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and is aware 'this is higher than your agreed risk rating', creating ambiguity about what the prior agreed rating was before the increase — the phrasing suggests 5 is higher than a previously agreed baseline, but questionnaire was 4 and final agreed is 5

      - File notes ATR wording includes text 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' which contradicts both the questionnaire result (4) and the consistent agreed rating of 5 found in Risk Profile Report and main Suitability Report

    The requirement is to verify that where an agreed risk rating differs from the questionnaire result, the override is justified and the client has acknowledged it. The comparison can be performed as all material elements are present in the evidence. The report asserts an override from 4 to 5 with justification and client acknowledgement. The Risk Profile Report corroborates the process: natural risk level 4, agreed risk level 5, reached through discussion and adjustment of factors. The justification 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' appears identically in the Suitability Report and file notes, showing consistency in how the override was communicated. Client acknowledgements are documented in the Suitability Report, including confirmation of non-reliance on pension funds and desire for growth. However, a material discrepancy exists in the file notes: the ATR wording section contains the phrase 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6', which contradicts the agreed rating of 5 stated everywhere else. This creates an internal inconsistency within the case file itself. The Risk Profile Report and main Suitability Report are internally consistent: questionnaire 4, agreed 5, justification provided, client acknowledgement recorded. The file notes discrepancy suggests either a drafting error in the file notes or an unexplained deviation between documented communications. The override direction, from 4 to 5, is a single-level increase that sits within the risk scale and is not extreme. The justification provided is general ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') rather than specific to the client's circumstances, but it is stated. The client acknowledgements are substantive and relate both to the risk increase and to financial capacity (non-reliance on pension funds, 10-year investment horizon, desire for growth). The core elements of G3.6 are satisfied: override is documented, justified, and client acknowledgement is recorded. The discrepancy regarding the '6' rating in file notes is a concern that requires clarification but does not negate the presence of proper override documentation and justification in the primary suitability report and supporting risk profile assessment.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts an agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced), a target investment strategy risk rating of 5, and a capacity for loss rated as 'Medium'. The recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), each individually risk-rated as 5. The report states the client agreed to increase risk rating from 4 (questionnaire result) to 5, and confirms the client's wish to proceed with this higher rating, understanding that funds may go up and down. The report asserts capacity for loss is 'Medium' based on income comfortably meeting expenditure, with savings and pension funds as a buffer.

    File:     The evidence corroborates an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) stated consistently across multiple documents [P1, P2, P3, P9]. The SIPP research report [P1, P15] confirms 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The risk profile document [P3, P9] shows natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from the questionnaire, with agreed level of 5 (Balanced) after discussion. The suitability report [P5] documents the client's understanding and agreement: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and acknowledges 'you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk.' The two recommended funds [P1, P14] are identified as both carrying risk rating 5. Capacity for loss is described in [P5] as 'Medium' based on state pension (£230/week) plus employment income (£300/week HGV driver) plus spouse contribution (£1,100/month) with savings and pension as buffer, stating 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' Investment term is 10 years until age 75.

    Discrepancies:

      - The questionnaire result (natural risk level 4) differs from the agreed risk level (5), creating an upward override. The override is explained in the documents as justified by adviser assessment and client confirmation, but the extraction metadata notes ambiguity about whether this was adviser-assessed or client-chosen.

      - The canonical model shows capacity for loss rated as 'Medium' but the extraction metadata flags this as ambiguous: 'Unclear whether "medium" is a formal classification or descriptive.' The evidence describes capacity for loss descriptively (income meeting expenditure with buffers) rather than assigning a formal classification level.

      - Existing arrangements carry varying risk ratings on provider scales (9, 4, 2, 7, 4) which are explicitly marked as non-comparable to the client risk scale ('isComparableToClientScale': false). These cannot be directly compared to the target strategy rating of 5 on the client's 1-5 scale.

    The core requirement is whether the recommended investment strategy (target rating 5) exceeds the client's risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss (Medium). The comparison can be performed: (1) Agreed risk rating equals target strategy rating: both are 5 on the same scale, documented consistently across [P1, P2, P3, P5, P9, P15]. (2) The upward override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5 is explained and evidenced: [P5] states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and documents client confirmation 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'. The client acknowledged this is higher risk and understood the implications. (3) Capacity for loss (Medium) is supported by financial position: [P5] details state pension plus employment income (total ~£320/week net for client plus £1,100/month spouse contribution) comfortably exceeding expenditure, with savings and pension buffers available, stating explicitly 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' (4) The recommended funds align with the agreed rating: [P1, P15] confirm both funds carry risk rating 5 and [P15] states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The two existing concerns do not materially affect this determination: the 'Medium' classification ambiguity does not undermine the underlying financial position assessment, which is clearly evidenced; the provider scale incomparability applies to existing arrangements only and does not affect the new strategy assessment, as the new strategy is mapped to the client risk scale. No evidence contradicts that strategy rating (5) exceeds the agreed rating (5) or the capacity for loss (Medium). The strategy does not exceed the client's risk tolerance; it aligns with it. The override itself is properly documented with justification and client acknowledgement.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: (1) Attitude to Risk questionnaire indicated risk rating 4, but adviser assessment and client agreement established risk rating 5 ('Balanced') on a 1-10 scale, with override justified by questionnaire not fully reflecting risk appetite and client confirmation of willingness to take higher risk; (2) Capacity for loss rated as 'Medium', based on state pension £230/week, employment income £300/week plus £20/week, spouse contribution £1,100/month plus state pension £230/week at retirement, combined income comfortably meets expenditure of £1,700/month, £6,000 savings, no liabilities, and confirmed underperformance would not impact day-to-day living; (3) Investment term is 10 years (referenced in client acknowledgement); (4) Recommended strategy is Aviva Risk 5 Portfolio with HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50%, both risk-rated at 5, designed to align with agreed risk rating of 5 and provide diversified multi-asset approach; (5) Strategy risk rating equals agreed rating of 5 on same 1-10 scale.

    File:     Evidence corroborates: (1) Risk profile report [P1, P7] confirms agreed risk level is 5 'Balanced' with description matching report: 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person...you may be inclined to look for a combination of investments with differing levels of risk'; (2) Fact Find [P17, P24] documents financial position: savings £6,000, no liabilities stated; (3) Risk profile report [P7] shows investment period of 2 years in projections table, not 10 years; (4) SIPP research report [P5, P15] confirms recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C, each 50%, with agreed risk level 5 and note 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level' [P15]; (5) Risk profile report [P1] describes methodology: natural risk level from questionnaire used as starting point, discussed with projections, factors altered until agreed balance achieved, resulting in agreed risk level for investment; (6) File notes [P6] show alternative fund options reviewed: Aviva current risk 2, Zurich current risk 7, Standard Life current risk 4, People's Pension rated risk 9 by Defaqto; (7) Capacity for loss assessment basis partially documented in risk profile but income details incomplete in evidence pack (state pension, employment income, spouse income and exact amounts not explicitly verified in retrieved passages); (8) MiFID II cost disclosure [P5, P18] shows combined fund ongoing costs 0.24%, transaction costs 0.13%, total 0.37% for the two funds.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states investment term is 10 years (from client acknowledgement: 'best growth potential over a 10-year period'), but Risk Profile Report [P7] projections table shows Investment Period as 2 years

      - Report describes capacity for loss rationale citing state pension £230/week, employment £300/week, spouse £1,100/month and spouse state pension £230/week, but retrieved passages do not contain explicit quantified verification of all these income amounts in supporting documents

      - Report claims override from risk rating 4 to 5 justified by 'questionnaire didn't fully reflect risk appetite', but Risk Profile Report [P1] states methodology involves starting with questionnaire score and then discussing/altering factors 'until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved', which describes standard process rather than evidence that questionnaire was inadequate

    The core elements of the risk, capacity for loss and strategy assessment are corroborated: agreed risk rating is consistently stated as 5 across report, Risk Profile Report, and SIPP research; the two recommended funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) are confirmed in SIPP research and carry individual risk ratings of 5; the portfolio risk mapping matches the agreed rating of 5. The override from questionnaire rating 4 to agreed rating 5 is documented with client acknowledgement statements present. However, two material inconsistencies require resolution: (1) Investment term discrepancy — the report's client acknowledgement references a 10-year period for growth, but the Risk Profile Report's own projections table specifies a 2-year Investment Period, creating ambiguity about whether the term used in the suitability assessment matches the projections underlying the capacity for loss and risk rating determination. This is a substantive inconsistency because capacity for loss and risk appropriateness depend on time horizon. (2) Capacity for loss justification — the report itemizes specific income sources (state pension £230/week, employment £300/week plus £20/week, spouse £1,100/month plus £230/week state pension at retirement), but the retrieved evidence [P1, P7] does not contain explicit verification of these exact figures in those sources; the Fact Find [P17, P24] documents £6,000 savings and no liabilities but does not include the detailed income breakdown. The report's statement that combined income 'comfortably meets' expenditure of £1,700/month is supported in principle (stated in report's own quotes from page 4 Capacity for loss section) but lacks independent corroboration of the income figures used in that assessment from the retrieved passages. (3) The override justification ('questionnaire didn't fully reflect risk appetite') appears to restate the outcome rather than provide substantive evidence of why the questionnaire was inadequate, though the client acknowledgement ('you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and confirmation 'not reliant upon pension funds') does provide client-side justification. The guard on standard process applies here: the Risk Profile Report describes a normal methodology of starting with questionnaire and adjusting through discussion, not evidence of questionnaire inadequacy per se. Tolerance for loss is stated qualitatively (client aware funds rise and fall, aware of downside risk) but no quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is documented, which is consistent with the extraction note that this is absent; this is a gap but not a contradiction. No vulnerability indicators in the evidence materially affect this assessment. The compatibility of the recommended funds with the agreed risk rating and capacity for loss appears sound based on retrieved evidence, but the investment term inconsistency and incomplete independent verification of income basis for capacity for loss determination constitute material clarifications needed.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client's stated knowledge level is 'Moderate'.

    File:     The Risk profile report (P1, P20) records the client's responses to the Investor Experience questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. When asked 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' the client responded 'No understanding / knowledge'. The questionnaire also shows 'No understanding / knowledge' to questions about stocks and shares, Unit Trusts, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning. The client answered 'Yes, by myself' only to Current Accounts/Savings/Cash ISA and 'Yes, by myself' to checking valuations annually. File notes (P18) state: 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The Fact Find (P8) shows the client is 65 years old, employed as an HGV driver earning £1,200 net monthly plus £230 state pension, with total monthly expenditure of £1,718 and negative disposable income of £-288.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states knowledge level is 'Moderate'; Risk profile questionnaire (P1, P20) records client response as 'No understanding / knowledge' to the direct question on investing experience and understanding.

      - Report asserts 'Moderate' knowledge; file notes (P18) explicitly state 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'.

      - Report categorises knowledge as 'Moderate'; questionnaire shows 'No understanding / knowledge' responses to stocks and shares, Unit Trusts, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning products.

    The comparison can be performed. The questionnaire responses in the Risk profile report (P1, P20) are direct, assessed answers to explicit questions about investment understanding, not self-deprecating remarks. The client selected 'No understanding / knowledge' when asked to describe their experience and understanding of investing generally. This is reinforced by identical responses across multiple specific product questions and by the file notes (P18) which characterise the experience as 'limited to workplace pension arrangements only'. The report's assertion of 'Moderate' knowledge is contradicted by the evidence. The questionnaire data appears to predate or occur at the same meeting (18/09/2025) as the fact find completion (19/09/2025), so there is no temporal guard explaining a change. The discrepancy is material: the report states a knowledge level that is one category higher than what the file documents. The guards note that self-assessed comfort is distinct from assessed knowledge, and that questionnaire wording is the evidence—'No understanding / knowledge' recorded against a direct question is an assessed answer, not a self-assessment of comfort. The evidence shows assessed knowledge of 'No understanding / knowledge', not 'Moderate'. No evidence in the file supports an upgrading of this assessment to 'Moderate' by the time of advice.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the client has: (1) previously taken out cash savings products; (2) NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by himself; (3) held group pensions for some time.

    File:     The Risk Profile questionnaire [P1] shows: (1) 'Yes, by myself' to investing in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings within last 5 years; (2) 'No' to stocks and shares within last 5 years; (3) 'No' to pension planning and income in retirement products within last 5 years; (4) 'No understanding / knowledge' to questions about how stocks and shares work and risks; (5) 'No understanding / knowledge' to pension planning and income in retirement. The Fact Find [P24] records employment as HGV Driver. Existing arrangements [P5, P6, P7, P8, P11, P22] show five pension plans held: People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' which aligns with P1 showing 'No' to stocks and shares in last 5 years, but P1 also shows 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, a material knowledge gap the report does not address.

      - The report asserts client 'held your group pensions for some time' but P1 Risk Profile shows 'No' response to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' This contradicts the claim of pension holding experience.

      - The report makes no mention of the client's recorded 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning despite recommending pension consolidation and transfer to a complex multi-fund personal pension.

    The report's assertion that the client has 'held group pensions for some time' is contradicted by P1, which records 'No' to pension product investment in the last 5 years. However, the existing arrangements evidence [P5, P6, P7, P8, P11, P22] confirms five pension plans currently exist with substantial values, evidencing past holding. The tension arises from the questionnaire answer 'No' to pension products in last 5 years: either the plans predate 5 years (consistent with holding 'for some time'), or the questionnaire response is inaccurate. The more material finding is that P1 explicitly records the client's response as 'No understanding / knowledge' to the questions 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' This is a direct, contemporaneous record of the client's self-assessed knowledge state and contradicts the report's implicit reliance on pension experience to justify suitability. The report recommends consolidation of five pensions into a new personal pension with two funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%), a material restructuring. The recommendation depends on the client understanding pension structures and retirement income mechanics. The evidence [P1] shows the client recorded 'No understanding / knowledge' of exactly these topics. The guard permits distinguishing passive workplace pension holding from self-directed experience; however, the rule also provides that 'plans held do not prove understanding — do not treat plan ownership as evidence of knowledge.' Here the client owns five plans but claims to have no understanding of pension planning. This is a material gap between the report's apparent assumption of adequate knowledge (given the recommendation of consolidation and complex product features) and the evidence of no understanding recorded in the Risk Profile questionnaire.
