  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended product, Aviva Pension Portfolio with Flexi Access Drawdown, is Complex. Complexity drivers cited are: (1) Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options; (2) Multiple fund choices within platform; (3) Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required; (4) Death benefit options and nominations; (5) Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. The report states the client 'understands the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' and that the client has 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The fund recommendation comprises two balanced risk-5 funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund 50%), described as providing 'a professionally managed, diversified investment solution' kept 'fairly simple' per client preference.

    File:     The Aviva Pension Portfolio Key Features [P2] document states the product's aims include giving 'flexibility and a range of options to control how your retirement pension pot is invested' and multiple retirement benefit options (lump sum, income drawdown, annuity combinations). The KIID [P14] for CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund classifies it as Risk Category 4 (medium fluctuations). The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio [P7] states the fund 'aims to provide growth in line with its risk profile in the long term, which is a period of five years or more' and is 'actively managed' with asset allocation reflecting risk levels. The Suitability Report [P18] records the client's knowledge as: 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and 'you understand that returns are not guaranteed'. The report also states [P18]: 'As a Balanced investor, you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk. If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach.' This characterisation appears in the context of post-questionnaire discussion where the adviser felt the initial questionnaire-derived rating of 4 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and the client agreed to increase to rating 5.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report characterises the client's knowledge as 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' suitable for a Balanced investor, but simultaneously records 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements', which contradicts 'some experience'.

      - The report designates the recommended product as Complex and lists five complexity drivers (pension freedoms, multiple fund choices, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, death benefit options, IHT planning), yet the fund selection itself (two balanced multi-asset funds) is described as 'fairly simple' and designed to be 'easily understood', creating tension between product complexity and fund simplicity characterisation.

      - The report cites client understanding of 'pension freedoms' as a rationale for the recommendation, stating the client 'understands the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown', but the evidence only shows the client agreed to increase risk rating from 4 to 5 in discussion; no passage demonstrates the client's explicit comprehension of pension freedoms, tax treatment of drawdown, or IHT implications from April 2027.

    The report recommends a product designated as Complex to a client whose knowledge level is documented as limited ('experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'). However, the guard specifies that 'a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex; do not escalate on product volume or value alone'. The Aviva Pension Portfolio with the two recommended balanced multi-asset funds (HSBC and CT Universal MAP, both Risk 5 Balanced category) falls within mainstream personal pension practice. The complexity drivers listed—pension freedoms, multiple fund choices, tax-efficient withdrawal, death benefits, IHT treatment—are features of any modern flexi-access drawdown product and are generic to the product wrapper rather than evidence of unusual complexity. The fund selection itself is positioned as simple and diversified for non-expert investors. The discrepancy arises not from mismatch between client knowledge and product type, but from internal report inconsistency: the product is labelled Complex while the funds and approach are described as simple. The client's agreement to Risk 5 rating was reached through adviser-led discussion rather than demonstrated client-initiated comprehension. Critically, no evidence in the file shows the client asked questions about, received explanations of, or demonstrated understanding of the specific complex features itemised (particularly tax treatment of drawdown post-75, IHT changes from April 2027, or the mechanics of flexible withdrawal). The report states [P10] that 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility', but this statement is unsupported by any showing of comprehension in the case file; it appears to be the adviser's characterisation of what the client should understand given agreement to proceed, not evidence of what the client demonstrably understood. For a product designated Complex, the absence of any record of client questions, explanations provided, or comprehension checks is a material gap, particularly where the client's prior knowledge is explicitly limited.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the client has 'Moderate' knowledge and experience, and recommends a 'Complex' product (Aviva Platform Personal Pension with HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund).

    File:     The risk profile report [P1] shows the client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to questions about stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning and income in retirement. The client confirmed 'No' to having invested in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products in the last 5 years. The client stated 'Yes, by myself' only for Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, and National Savings — basic cash products. [P2] records 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience and under Capacity for Loss questionnaire discussions, indicating no supplementary evidence of knowledge or comprehension.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client has 'Moderate' knowledge; risk profile questionnaire [P1] shows 'No understanding / knowledge' responses to stocks and shares, pensions, and complex products, with no prior investment experience in these asset classes beyond cash savings

      - Report recommends 'Complex' products (mixed asset funds and pension consolidation); client's demonstrated knowledge is limited to basic cash savings with no recorded experience of collective investment schemes, equities, or pension planning

      - No record in [P2] of adviser discussion or client questions about the complexity of the recommended products, their features, or how they work

    The report's assertion that the client possesses 'Moderate' knowledge is contradicted by the risk profile evidence [P1]. The questionnaire responses are unambiguous: the client selected 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning. The only investment experience recorded is with basic cash products (savings accounts, cash ISAs, National Savings), which does not evidence knowledge of complex collective investment schemes or pension consolidation strategies. The recommended product involves: (1) a multi-asset balanced fund (CT Universal MAP) that invests in equities, bonds, derivatives and REITs with 30-70% equity exposure [P3]; (2) an actively managed equity-focused fund (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio); and (3) consolidation of five separate pension arrangements requiring understanding of transfer mechanics and fund switching. This complexity is material. The guard 'You confirmed you understand' does not apply here because there is no category C record showing the client confirmed or demonstrated understanding of these complex features. [P2] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' following discussions of investor experience and capacity for loss, providing no corroboration that the adviser explained or the client grasped the complexity. The report's narrative describes the client as having 'Moderate' knowledge suitable for balanced products, but the questionnaire evidence establishes the client had 'No understanding / knowledge' of the underlying asset types and investment mechanisms. This is a direct contradiction between the report's characterisation and the file's factual record.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the client 'feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section). The recommended products are two balanced funds: CT Universal Map Balanced (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (50%), allocated to an Aviva Platform Personal Pension. The report describes the client as a Balanced investor who 'have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' and states 'you understand that returns are not guaranteed' and 'you are aware that your funds may go up as well as down'. The report does not explicitly document client questions, comprehension testing, or explanation of the fund selection rationale specific to these two recommended funds.

    File:     The file notes confirm the client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire and agreed to risk rating 5 (Balanced). The file states: 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed' (P4, P11). The file records the client is 'aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' (P4, P11). However, the file contains no record of client questions about the specific recommended funds (CT Universal Map Balanced or HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio), no notes documenting how these funds were explained, no evidence of the client's questions about fund features, and no documentation of the client's comprehension of the fund selection or their characteristics.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the client has 'a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' but the file simultaneously records that the client's 'experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements', which is a material qualification not reflected in the report's confident framing of understanding.

      - The report describes the client as suitable for a Balanced approach 'if you have some experience in investments', yet the file explicitly constrains this: experience is 'limited to workplace pension arrangements', which is a narrower and less generalizable form of investment experience than the report's language suggests.

      - The report provides no documented evidence of client questions or comprehension testing for the two specific recommended funds (CT Universal Map Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio). The file contains no notes of discussions about these funds' features, risks, or suitability.

    The check applies because this is an advised case (trigger met: isAdvisedCase = true). The requirement is to evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of complex products based on their questions and comprehension. The two recommended funds are balanced multi-asset portfolios with 80+ investment options available in the Aviva platform alone, making these moderately complex products. The report asserts the client has 'reasonable understanding' and is comfortable with investments, but qualifies this by referencing the Balanced investor profile definition: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach.' The file reveals a material constraint: the client's experience is explicitly 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' — a narrow, passive investment experience that does not typically involve product selection, fund switching, or active evaluation of complex multi-asset strategies. The report does not adequately bridge this gap by documenting how the client's limited experience was assessed as sufficient for these products, or by recording the explanation and comprehension testing that would normally evidence such a judgment. The guard ('An absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand — report it as an evidence gap, weighted by product complexity') applies here: the absence of questions is an evidence gap. However, given the product complexity (balanced multi-asset funds requiring understanding of asset allocation, diversification, and risk characteristics) and the client's explicitly limited investment experience, the absence of any record of explanation, questions, or comprehension testing is material. The report's assertion of 'reasonable understanding' rests on the client completing a questionnaire and agreeing to a risk rating, but no evidence appears in the file that the client was then walked through the fund selection, asked questions, or demonstrated comprehension of how CT Universal Map Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio work, what they hold, or why they suit a 69-year-old client with limited investment experience approaching retirement. The file does not record that the adviser addressed the mismatch between the client's limited experience and the complexity of active fund-based portfolio management. No vulnerability indicators are recorded that would explain why no such documentation appears.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies the recommended product (Aviva Pension Portfolio) as Complex with the following complexity drivers: Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options; Multiple fund choices within platform; Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required; Death benefit options and nominations; Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. The report provides multiple rationales for the recommendation across 10 stated reasons including: Employer Contributions (People's Pension to be retained as employer still contributing); Small Pots Encashment (Scottish Widows £211.32 pot); Lower Costs (charge comparison); Lifestyling Risk (moving away from lifestyling funds); Greater Investment Choice (wider range of options); Adviser Charging Facilitation (charges deductible directly); Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments; Multi-asset Fund Access (blend of funds to achieve risk level 5); Enhanced Retirement Flexibility; and Consolidation Benefits.

    File:     The file evidences the client's knowledge and experience as follows: The client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire indicating risk rating 4, but the adviser and client agreed to increase to risk rating 5 (Balanced investor). File notes state: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' The client's capacity for loss is described as medium, based on receipt of State Pension (~£230/week), HGV driver income (~£300/week net), additional income from Sea Cadets (£20/week), and spouse employment income (£1,100/month), with savings and pension funds available. File notes indicate the client's 'experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' but states 'you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The client confirmed 'potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' The policy research document notes the client 'has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work' and 'ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement.' The Key Features document (Aviva Pension Portfolio) describes the product's aims including: flexibility to control retirement benefits through multiple options (lump sum, drawdown, annuity, combination); control and flexibility to manage investments; access to wider investment choices; death benefit nominations; and access to tax-efficient benefits. The product Terms and Conditions detail complex features including Flexi-access Drawdown with no income limits, Phased Drawdown, Death Benefit options (Lump Sum, income, annuity), Dependant definitions, and various crystallisation options.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states product is Complex but file records client experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' with understanding limited to basic pension mechanics, creating tension between complexity classification and demonstrated knowledge level.

      - Report identifies 'Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required' as a complexity driver, but no evidence in file of client discussion or comprehension of drawdown taxation, withdrawal strategies, or how marginal tax rates affect income drawdown.

      - Report cites 'Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027' as complexity driver, but file contains no evidence of client discussion of IHT, estate planning, or reference to April 2027 IHT threshold changes.

      - Report identifies 'Multiple fund choices within platform' as complexity driver and recommends two-fund portfolio (CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%), but no evidence in file of client being asked to understand or select between multiple fund options or demonstrate comprehension of multi-asset fund construction.

      - Report's rationale on 'Lower Costs' states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but derived figures show Aviva Platform total charge of 0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) compared to Zurich existing 0.93%, a difference of 0.49% not 0.06%, and this charge comparison alone does not address product complexity rationale.

      - File notes record client concerns about pension consolidation ('doesn't understand how they work'), indicating limited knowledge at outset, yet report recommends consolidation into a platform offering complex Flexi-access Drawdown, multiple death benefit options, and discretionary crystallisation events without evidence of client comprehension of these features.

      - Report rationale on 'Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options' as complexity driver is not matched by any evidence in file of adviser explaining these options to client, testing client comprehension, or confirming client understands the difference between drawdown, annuity, and lump sum options.

    The requirement asks whether the recommended product's complexity is consistent with the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience, and whether the rationale is adequately justified. The file records the client's knowledge level as 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' with initial confusion about his existing multiple pensions. The client's risk rating was increased to 5 (Balanced) based on adviser assessment that questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite.' The Balanced investor profile in the file states: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach.' This suggests moderate knowledge sufficient for balanced risk positioning, not the technical knowledge needed for a complex product offering pension freedoms, tax-efficient drawdown strategies, multiple death benefit nominations, and IHT planning. The report classifies the Aviva Pension Portfolio as Complex and identifies five complexity drivers. However, examination of the evidence shows: (1) Pension freedoms and drawdown options are identified as a complexity driver, but there is no file evidence of the client being asked to demonstrate or explain understanding of drawdown mechanics, income limits, tax treatment, or crystallisation events. (2) Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies are listed as requiring complexity management, yet the file contains no discussion of marginal tax rates, personal allowance utilisation, withdrawal sequencing, or client comprehension testing on these matters. (3) Inheritance Tax planning from April 2027 is cited as a complexity driver, but no evidence exists in the file of any discussion of IHT, estate planning, beneficiary nomination strategy, or client awareness of threshold changes. (4) Multiple fund choices are flagged as complex, but the adviser has actually made the fund selection for the client (50/50 split), limiting choice to execution rather than decision-making. (5) Death benefit options and nominations are listed as complex features, but file notes show no evidence of adviser explaining Dependant definitions, successor arrangements, or testing client comprehension of these features. The rationales provided address suitability factors (employer contributions, lower costs, investment choice, ongoing monitoring) but do not substantively justify why a client with 'limited' experience should be placed in a product classified as Complex without evidence of comprehension. The guidance at P24 stating client understands 'returns are not guaranteed' addresses risk understanding, not the technical complexity of pension freedoms, drawdown taxation, or death benefit administration. A material gap exists between the complexity classification and the client's recorded knowledge level, and between the complexity drivers cited and the evidence of client discussion or comprehension of those features.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.8). Report asserts combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, but Fact Find shows Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00 (Net Income £1,430.00 vs Expenditure £1,718.00), indicating a monthly deficit not a comfortable surplus. Report asserts pension funds totalling approximately £106,000, but extracted figures show total of £116,998.47 across five existing arrangements (People's Pension £6,601.55 + Scottish Widows £211.32 + Aviva £3,744.36 + Zurich £103,439.24 + Standard Life £3,002.00).   [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Initial commitment: £3,305.55 (3% advice fee). Ongoing commitment: £1,101.85 per annum (1% of fund value). Affordability basis: combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; savings of around £6,000; no outstanding liabilities; pension funds totalling approximately £106,000. Client confirmed potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     Fact Find shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Total Monthly Disposable Income: -£288.00. Calculated total monthly household expenditure £1,718.00 with no expected changes. Existing arrangements total £116,998.47 (not £106,000). Savings of £6,000.00 corroborated. Report states initial fee of £3,305.55 taken from new solution and ongoing charge of 1.00% estimated as £1,101.85 first year based on transfer value £110,185 plus contributions.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, but Fact Find shows Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00 (Net Income £1,430.00 vs Expenditure £1,718.00), indicating a monthly deficit not a comfortable surplus.

      - Report asserts pension funds totalling approximately £106,000, but extracted figures show total of £116,998.47 across five existing arrangements (People's Pension £6,601.55 + Scottish Widows £211.32 + Aviva £3,744.36 + Zurich £103,439.24 + Standard Life £3,002.00).

      - Report cites expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month but Fact Find states Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure of £1,718.00.

    The initial commitment of £3,305.55 is a one-off adviser fee taken from the transfer value at outset and not an ongoing income commitment, so the guard against testing fees against monthly surplus applies. However, the affordability assertion itself contradicts the cashflow evidence. The report states income 'comfortably meets' expenditure of approximately £1,700 monthly, but the Fact Find—the primary financial document—records a negative monthly disposable income of -£288.00. This is material: the client has a structural monthly shortfall of £288. The report does not address or reconcile this deficit. The ongoing adviser charge of £1,101.85 per annum (approximately £91.82 per month) will be deducted from the pension fund through the Aviva Platform, not from income, so does not worsen the monthly cashflow deficit directly. However, the affordability assessment relies on a false premise: that income comfortably covers expenditure. The Fact Find establishes the opposite. The pension values differ by £10,998.47 between the two statements (£106,000 stated vs £116,998.47 actual), which affects the basis for the ongoing fee calculation and the apparent financial resilience. The emergency fund of £6,000 is correctly evidenced and is positive, but this modest reserve must cover a £288 monthly deficit, meaning it would be depleted in approximately 21 months without additional income sources or expenditure reduction. The report does not discuss how this deficit will be funded or whether the recommendation exacerbates or alleviates it. The transfer value used for ongoing charge calculation (£110,185) does not match either the sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47) or the stated pension total (£106,000), creating ambiguity about the basis of affordability projections.

    File:     The Fact Find [P2] shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, producing a Monthly Disposable Income of £-288.00 (a deficit).   [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts an emergency fund of £6,000 held amount, confirmed as sufficient by the client. The report states 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' (page 3). A second statement on page 8 notes 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' The report identifies emergency access needs via pension drawdown, noting 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8). The report does not quantify a required emergency fund amount. The proposed scenario shows liquid assets declining from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027, continuing to decline throughout the projection period. By 2056, liquid assets reach £0 and thereafter show negative shortfall positions (shown in red in the cashflow).

    File:     The Fact Find [P5] records £6,000.00 cash savings as at 22/09/2025. The intelliflo planning Baseline scenario [P14] shows total liquid assets of £6,000 in the asset tree. The proposed scenario [P19] also shows £6,000 in liquid assets (Savings) at 2025. The planning documents [P3], [P6], [P16], [P20] detail cashflow projections in which liquid assets are treated as a draw-down resource. Specifically, [P6] shows liquid assets declining from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), and continuing to deplete throughout the projection. By year 2056 (age 100/96), the proposed scenario [P7] shows liquid assets of £0 and an in-year deficit of (£2,930), with subsequent years showing increasing deficits: (£3,014), (£3,357). The baseline scenario [P11] similarly shows liquid assets declining to £0 by 2056 and deficits thereafter. The cashflow model [P22] notes 'Where income is insufficient to cover your expenditure, the modeller assumes that you will draw from your liquid assets.' No required emergency fund amount is quantified in any planning document.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts emergency fund of £6,000 is 'confirmed sufficient' but provides no quantified requirement to validate sufficiency against

      - Liquid assets of £6,000 are consumed by the cashflow model and depleted to £0 by age 100 (2056), which contradicts an assertion of sufficiency for 'unexpected emergencies' throughout retirement

      - Report states client is 'comfortable with liquid cash should you require instance access funds' but the projection model treats this entire £6,000 as available for regular expenditure drawdown, not reserved as emergency provision

      - Report addresses 'Emergency' access via pension drawdown (10-15 working days) but does not reconcile this with the depletion of cash reserves or establish whether pension drawdown timing meets emergency access needs

      - No emergency fund requirement is quantified in either the report or supporting documents, preventing validation of the claimed sufficiency

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Quantified emergency fund requirement or target amount, Specification of whether £6,000 cash is earmarked as emergency reserve or available for general cashflow drawdown, Rationale for claiming £6,000 sufficiency when projection model depletes it entirely by age 100, Assessment of whether pension drawdown (10-15 days) constitutes adequate emergency access when cash reserves are exhausted

    The report asserts that the client has 'confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies' and is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash'. However, the comparison cannot be properly performed because: (1) The report does not quantify a required emergency fund amount, only a held amount of £6,000; (2) The planning projections [P6], [P7], [P16], [P20], [P22] treat the £6,000 cash as a depletion resource to be drawn down to meet expenditure shortfalls, not as a reserved emergency fund; (3) The projections explicitly show liquid assets declining from £6,000 in 2025 to £0 by 2056, with subsequent years in deficit; (4) No guard explains this depletion as intentional ring-fencing or earmarking—the model treats it as available for regular cashflow; (5) The emergency access via pension drawdown (10-15 working days per page 8) is not reconciled against the timing needs of the named emergency access type or against the point at which cash reserves reach zero. Under the decision logic, an asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified and must be recorded as unquantified. The guard also requires checking whether cash is earmarked elsewhere; here, it is clearly earmarked for general expenditure drawdown in the cashflow, creating a material inconsistency between the suitability claim (emergency reserves confirmed sufficient) and the financial modelling (emergency reserves fully consumed). This is a material clarification requiring additional evidence: either a quantified emergency fund requirement and an explicit reserve strategy, or explanation of how expenditure projections that deplete liquid assets align with an asserted sufficiency for emergencies.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts initial commitment of £3,305.55 (3% adviser fee) and ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 annually (1% ongoing adviser charge). The report states affordability is supported by: 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, you hold savings of around £6,000, and have no outstanding liabilities.' Monthly surplus is derived as £2,786.67. The report also states 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.'

    File:     Fact Find page 10 shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00, Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00, Total Monthly Disposable Income £-288.00. This represents a monthly deficit of £288, not a surplus. Fact Find page 9 lists employment income as £1,200 monthly (employed, CDH Recruitment) plus State Pension £230 monthly = £1,430 total. Expenditure is itemized at £1,718 monthly. The illustration (P15) confirms initial adviser charge of £3,305.55 (3% of £110,185 transfer) and ongoing charge estimated at £89.07 monthly (1% annually). The emergency fund is evidenced as £6,000 savings. Planning projection (P17, P22) shows liquid assets declining from approximately £5,912 in 2027 onwards.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states monthly surplus of £2,786.67; Fact Find evidences monthly deficit of -£288.00

      - Report states 'combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find shows expenditure of £1,718 per month exceeding income of £1,430 per month

      - Report describes affordability as based on income coverage; actual position is negative monthly cashflow

      - Report asserts savings of 'around £6,000'; Fact Find confirms £6,000 exactly, but this is the sole emergency reserve against a monthly deficit

      - Ongoing commitment annualized at £1,101.85 per report, but illustration shows £89.07 monthly = £1,068.84 annualized, a discrepancy of £33.01 or 3%

    The comparison can be performed. The critical discrepancy is between the report's derived monthly surplus of £2,786.67 and the Fact Find's calculated monthly deficit of -£288.00. The Fact Find page 10 shows explicit calculation: £1,430 net income minus £1,718 expenditure equals -£288 monthly deficit. The report's stated surplus appears to derive from a different or unstated calculation not supported by the evidenced income and expenditure figures. The employment income of £1,200 monthly (page 9) is consistent across documents, as is State Pension of £230 monthly. The total expenditure of £1,718 is explicitly stated in the Fact Find. No reconciliation is provided for how £2,786.67 surplus was derived from £1,430 income. The initial adviser charge of £3,305.55 is corroborated by the illustration (P15). The ongoing charge estimate in the report (£1,101.85 annually) differs slightly from the illustration's £89.07 monthly (£1,068.84 annualized), but both are within the same order of magnitude. The material concern is that the client operates on negative monthly cashflow of -£288, meaning the ongoing adviser charge of approximately £1,069-£1,102 per annum cannot be funded from current income surplus. The £6,000 emergency fund would be depleted in approximately 21 months if the monthly deficit persists and charges are paid from savings. The report asserts affordability without this foundation. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence pack that would affect this assessment, though the negative cashflow position itself may warrant additional support or communication about affordability risk.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity needs are funded from Pension [/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/fundedFrom: 'Pension']. The report does not quantify an emergency fund shortfall. The canonical model does not record /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall.

    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Opening liquid assets of £6,000 in savings account [P1, P2, P5, P6]; (2) Surplus Account balance of £0 [P1, P2, P4, P5, P9]; (3) Cashflow projections in proposed scenario show Liquid Assets declining from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), £5,869 (2028), £5,825 (2029), continuing to decline to £5,573 (2035), £5,491 (2037), £5,451 (2038), and £5,177 (2045), with no year showing red bars indicating insufficient liquid assets [P6, P8, P14, P21]; (4) The liquidation order is Savings £6,000 (LIQUIDATED 2ND after Surplus Account) [P2]; (5) The proposed scenario with pensions transferred shows positive cashflow throughout with in-year surpluses of £0 in every year shown [P8, P14]; (6) No red bars appear on the Summary Cashflow graph indicating insufficient liquid assets to meet shortfalls [P12]; (7) The plan explicitly states income includes employment (£15,600 p/a + £1,040 p/a for JS, £13,200 p/a for KS until retirements) and State Pension (£11,973 from age 66) [P2, P22]; (8) Total Regular Expenses are £20,616 p/a [P6]; (9) Under stress test with 6% inflation and -30% immediate market crash, 'there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used' [P13].

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts emergency fund needs are 'funded from Pension' but the evidence shows: (1) Emergency fund remains in accessible liquid savings (£6,000 Cash Account), not pension; (2) The cashflow does not liquidate the emergency fund—it declines only slightly (£6,000 to £5,177 over 20 years) while maintaining positive in-year surpluses of £0 or more every year; (3) The Savings account is the SECOND liquidation priority after the Surplus Account (which is £0), meaning it is treated as available but not drawn upon; (4) Pension funds are excluded from scenario (EXCLUDED FROM SCENARIO status on Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life in proposed scenario) yet cashflow shows no pension withdrawals required in the projection years shown [P8, P14, P2].

      - The report does not quantify the emergency fund amount or address its adequacy, yet the evidence shows £6,000 available and untouched throughout the planning horizon despite full cashflow modelling.

    The guards require careful application here. Guard 1 states 'A transfer between pension wrappers does not deplete an emergency fund — only new money from cash does.' The recommendation transfers £110,185 from existing pensions to Aviva Platform; this is a wrapper transfer, not a depletion of the emergency fund. Guard 2 states 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it, whatever the report says about retaining liquid capital. Read the liquidation order in the scenario before accepting an assertion of sufficiency.' The evidence shows the liquidation order is (1) Surplus Account £0, (2) Savings £6,000. The cashflow projection over 35 years (2025–2060 in proposed scenario and 2025–2047 in baseline) shows NO years with red bars indicating insufficient liquid assets, NO required withdrawals from pensions (in-year surpluses show £0 in every year), and Liquid Assets declining only from £6,000 to £5,177 by 2045 [P21], never reaching zero or triggering pension access. The 'Liquid Assets' column in the proposed scenario asset statement [P6, P21] shows the savings account is never depleted to fund shortfalls; it is merely drawn down slightly by the very small annual deficits that appear to accumulate, but the cashflow statement [P8, P14] shows in-year surplus/deficit as £0 in every projected year from 2025 onwards in the proposed scenario. This means the £6,000 emergency fund is neither consumed by the recommendation nor made unavailable. The report's assertion that liquidity needs are 'funded from Pension' contradicts the evidence: the cashflow shows pension funds are excluded and remain untouched (EXCLUDED FROM SCENARIO status), while the emergency fund in savings remains available and largely intact. However, the report does NOT assert that the emergency fund is depleted or unavailable—it is silent on emergency fund quantification. The extracted absence of /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall means the report made no explicit claim about emergency fund shortfall. The recommendation does not lock capital away; the Aviva Platform is a personal pension offering flexi-access drawdown from age 55, permitting access when needed [P19]. The report does not claim the emergency fund is consumed or inaccessible. The evidence supports that the emergency fund (£6,000 savings) remains available and is not depleted by the recommendation. The slight decline in liquid assets (£6,000 to £5,177) is driven by the model's discretionary surplus savings assumption and does not represent an emergency fund depletion triggered by the recommendation itself.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report recommends a Personal Pension wrapper with a selected retirement age of 71. The report asserts in the cashflow analysis that 'There are no issues with your cash flow forecast' and that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.' The client is currently age 69 (turning 70 in 2026), and the minimum pension age is 55. The report projects income and expenditure through to age 100, with no projected shortfalls in liquid assets or accessible pensions.

    File:     The People's Pension product information [P1] states the client 'can access your pension savings at any age on medical grounds if you're unable to continue your usual occupation' and offers multiple access options including small pot lump sums (if under £10,000), flexi-access drawdown, income drawdown, and annuity purchase. Aviva product information [P2] confirms 'Your client s pension money has to stay in a pension until they at least reach the minimum pension age. This is currently age 55' and states 'Moving funds to drawdown is available for this plan from age 55'. The client is already aged 69, which is above the minimum pension age of 55 (confirmed in [P16]). The Aviva Key Features [P3] confirm access is available 'at any time from the normal minimum pension age. This is currently age 55. From 6 April 2028 this will be age 57'. The cashflow projection in [P10] and [P11] shows 'Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions' throughout the plan with positive 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' figures, indicating no projected need for emergency access before age 71. The intelliflo planning baseline scenario [P7] and proposed scenario [P18] both show a 'Surplus Account' with £0 in the baseline but maintained throughout the proposed scenario.

    The requirement tests whether the Personal Pension wrapper creates a liquidity mismatch by locking capital beyond the client's access needs. The guard applies here: 'Pension access restrictions are irrelevant where the client is already over the minimum pension age'. The client is aged 69, which exceeds the minimum pension age of 55 by 14 years. The Personal Pension wrapper recommended by the report is compliant with pension rules that restrict access before age 55 (moving to 57 from April 2028), but this restriction does not apply to this client. Both The People's Pension [P1] and Aviva [P2] product documentation confirm flexible access options available to the client immediately. The selected retirement age of 71 is a planning assumption, not a restriction. The client can access funds from age 55 onwards at any time, or earlier on medical grounds. The cashflow projections in [P4], [P10], [P11] show no projected shortfalls or liquidity crises before, at, or after age 71, indicating the recommendation does not force the client to wait beyond their actual access needs. The 'required from' dates for liquidity needs are not present in the extracted model, but the evidence shows the client has immediate legal access to their pension funds should they require them, and the financial projections show no anticipated need for emergency withdrawal. The Personal Pension wrapper permits access when the client needs it, either as planned at 71 or earlier if circumstances change. No mismatch between the wrapper's restrictions and the client's needs is evidenced.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four foreseen changes: (1) Client retirement in 2027 with loss of HGV employment income (£1,300/month) and Sea Cadets income (£86.67/month), net impact -£1,386.67/month. (2) Kim's retirement in 2026 at age 66 with loss of £1,100/month employment income. (3) Kim's State Pension commencement in 2026 at age 66 adding £1,000/month (stated as "around £230 per week"). (4) Inheritance Tax regime change from 6 April 2027 affecting pensions inclusion in taxable estate. The report also records one liquidity need: access to funds in future (pension drawdown) will take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking. No quantified emergency fund is stated. Current monthly surplus/deficit is stated as -£288.00 (expenditure £1,718 exceeds income £1,430).

    File:     Fact Find [P1] records: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00, Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00, Total Monthly Disposable Income -£288.00. Expected changes in expenditure: No. Expected changes in income: No. Intelliflo planning baseline scenario [P13] shows first-year regular income: Employment £16,640 (JS), State Pension from combined sources, Total Regular Income £28,613 (JS) + £13,200 (KS combined) = £41,813. First-year regular expenditure £24,001 (Tax & NI £3,385 + General Expenses £20,616). The timeline [P16] marks retirement events: Kim retires 2026 (age 66), John retires 2027 (age 71). Intelliflo proposed scenario [P20] itemises income: JS employed CDH Recruitment £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly) until 2027 retirement; Sea Cadets £1,040 p/a (£20 weekly) until 2027; Kim's Income £13,200 p/a (£1,100 monthly) until 2026 retirement; State Pension £11,973 p/a from age 66 for each. Cashflow tables [P18] show 2025 income £41,813, 2026 income £40,703, 2027 income £24,181, indicating sharp drop post-retirement events. Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions [P18]: 2025 £122,997, declining through retirement years. Baseline [P14] shows 2027 liquid assets £5,912, accessible pensions £117,073. Proposed scenario [P2] shows 2039-2047 liquid assets £5,411-£5,101. Emergency fund: Savings £6,000 (cash account) noted [P6] and [P20] with 1.25% expected return. No quantified emergency fund target or adequacy analysis provided in file.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Kim's State Pension impact as £1,000/month net but file states State Pension £11,973 p/a which is £998/month, a minor difference (£2/month) that aligns within rounding.

      - Report asserts client 'typically earning around £300 per week net' [P1 page 3 context] but Intelliflo [P20] models deterministically at £300 weekly without qualification of variability, creating potential for actual income to differ from plan assumption.

      - Report shows monthly income-expenditure surplus of -£288.00 in Fact Find [P1] but Intelliflo annual figures [P18] show 2025 total money in £41,813 against first-year expenses; this is not directly comparable as Intelliflo includes tax & NI and state pension while Fact Find shows net employment income only. The comparison requires clarification of income definition (gross/net, sources included).

      - Report derives client retirement financial impact as -£1,386.67/month (loss of £1,300 HGV + £86.67 Sea Cadets) but Intelliflo [P20] shows HGV income £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly × 52 = £15,600, not £1,300/month = £15,600/year) and Sea Cadets £1,040 p/a (£20 weekly × 52 = £1,040). The report calculation of £1,300/month appears to conflate weekly (£300) with monthly, yielding £300 × 4.33 = £1,299, which is close but the £86.67/month figure should derive from £1,040/year ÷ 12 = £86.67, consistent with file. The net impact derivation is supported by file figures.

      - Report asserts no quantified emergency fund is present; file shows Savings £6,000 in cash account [P6] [P20]. This asset exists but is not framed as an emergency fund with an adequacy target in either document.

      - Liquidity access time asserted as 10-15 working days for pension drawdown; file does not contradict this but does not independently confirm it either. This is standard practice language and not contradicted by evidence.

    The report's foreseeable changes are supported by file evidence: retirement dates (2026 Kim, 2027 John), income loss figures (calculated from weekly/annual rates in Intelliflo), and State Pension commencement are all documented. However, material gaps and inconsistencies appear. First, the current affordability position is not clearly reconciled: the Fact Find shows monthly deficit of £288, yet Intelliflo projects positive cash position (2025 in-year surplus £24,001, then managed decline post-retirement). This gap suggests either (a) the Fact Find deficit does not account for the full income picture (e.g. State Pension already in place, as [P20] confirms JS receiving State Pension), or (b) the recommendation assumes expenditure reduction or income acceptance not documented in the Fact Find's 'No' answer to income/expenditure changes. Second, the emergency fund is identified as £6,000 savings but is never analysed for adequacy. Intelliflo projects this declining to £5,912 by 2027 (retirement year) and stabilising around £5,100-5,400 thereafter, but no guidance is given on whether this level is sufficient for a 69-year-old couple approaching retirement. Third, the report's treatment of foreseeable needs is selective: it addresses retirement income drop and IHT regime change but does not quantify or analyse planned future expenditure (care needs, major repairs, gifts) despite these being standard retirement planning considerations. The Intelliflo file notes no 'illness' or 'care cost' custom expenses despite the timeline marking 'Illness' events at ages 100/96. Fourth, the income definition inconsistency: Fact Find income £1,430/month (employment net) is not directly comparable to Intelliflo £41,813/year (which includes State Pension and tax/NI treatment differently). This creates ambiguity about true affordability and whether the plan's surplus assumptions are grounded in the same baseline as the suitability advice. The report does not explicitly reconcile this or explain the transition from negative monthly disposable income to sustainable retirement cashflow. The guards permit undated needs and aspirational desires, but all material future needs here are dated (retirements 2026-2027) and the current deficit position creates material doubt about whether contributions/withdrawals are truly affordable without expenditure reduction the report does not address.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.8). Report states rationale for Zurich alternative: 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but file note [P24] evaluates Zurich based on fund availability and performance, not cost comparison to recommended solution. Report marks 10 of 28 rationale statements as isClientSpecific: false, indicating they are generic/boilerplate across multiple recommendations, which contradicts the requirement for client-specific rationale for each recommendation. Report recommends retention of People's Pension with rationale 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement' but also recommends consolidation of four other pensions to Aviva under objective OBJ3 (consolidation), creating inconsistency in consolidation strategy. 21 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report contains 28 rationale statements across multiple recommendations. Of these, 18 are marked as client-specific (isClientSpecific: true) and 10 are marked as generic/templated (isClientSpecific: false). The rationale texts include: (1) retention of workplace scheme with ongoing contributions; (2) small pot tax treatment analysis; (3-5, 7-9, 11-13, 15-17) repeated boilerplate rationale about lower costs, lifestyling, investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring; (6, 10, 14, 16) generic statements about fund diversification and monitoring; (18-27) fund selection rationale including risk profiling and performance; and (28) multi-asset fund explanation. Six recommendations are linked to objectives: OBJ3 (consolidation), OBJ2 and OBJ3 (switching arrangements), OBJ1 and OBJ2 (charging facilitation). The rationale for the Zurich arrangement specifically states: 'Multi-asset fund selection - Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund has been used... the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva.'

    File:     File note [P24] records adviser evaluation of alternatives: 'Aviva – Alternative option: Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR. Discounted switch.' 'Zurich – Alternative option: Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used to create the below 5... Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types. The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally. For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch.' 'Standard Life – Alternative option: Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva.' Research report [P5] and [P9] show agreed risk level 5 Balanced, with investments in CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), with combined costs of 0.37% [P7] and individual costs [P7] showing HSBC at 0.19% and CT Universal MAP at 0.55%. File note [P4] states: 'Summary of the Advice: Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement. Will be recommending to consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning around his retirement.' Fact Find [P18] confirms 'Retirement Planning' as the advice area. Intelliflo proposed scenario [P11] shows final asset position: Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211, New Aviva pension £110,185.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states rationale for Zurich alternative: 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but file note [P24] evaluates Zurich based on fund availability and performance, not cost comparison to recommended solution.

      - Report marks 10 of 28 rationale statements as isClientSpecific: false, indicating they are generic/boilerplate across multiple recommendations, which contradicts the requirement for client-specific rationale for each recommendation.

      - Report recommends retention of People's Pension with rationale 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement' but also recommends consolidation of four other pensions to Aviva under objective OBJ3 (consolidation), creating inconsistency in consolidation strategy.

      - Report states charge rationale 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' but appears twice with different stated differentials: one states '0.06% more expensive' and another states '0.08% more expensive' than recommended solution.

      - Report rationale for Aviva switching states 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR' per file note [P24], but this contradicts the recommendation to move to Aviva with risk level 5 funds (HSBC 50% + CT Universal MAP 50%).

      - Report provides repeated verbatim boilerplate across recommendations: 'Greater Investment Choice - The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options, allowing for a more tailored investment strategy that better suits your risk profile and retirement goals' appears identically in recommendations for different arrangements.

      - Report provides repeated verbatim boilerplate: 'Adviser Charging Facilitation - The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support without the need for separate payments' appears identically across multiple recommendations.

      - Report provides repeated verbatim boilerplate: 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments - By moving to the recommended scheme, I will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust your investments as required, helping to ensure they remain aligned with your changing needs and market conditions' appears identically across multiple recommendations.

    The report contains a mix of client-specific and generic rationale. The extraction metadata confirms that 10 of 28 rationale statements (35.7%) are flagged as not client-specific. Boilerplate text appears verbatim across multiple recommendations: 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', and 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' rationales are identical across different recommendations and arrangements, contradicting the requirement for personalised language demonstrating consideration of individual circumstances. The primary stated objective is consolidation (OBJ3) from file note [P4]: client 'wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand'. However, the report recommends retaining People's Pension whilst consolidating four other pensions, which is inconsistent with stated consolidation objective. File note [P24] documents evaluation of alternatives with reasoning (Aviva risk level mismatch, Zurich fund performance and lack of multi-asset options, Standard Life higher costs, Scottish Widows lack of servicing), and this evaluation is reflected in the report's rationale statements. However, the charge comparison statement in the report ('The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Zurich) appears unsupported by the file note analysis, which focuses on fund performance and availability rather than cost comparison. The fund selection rationale is supported by research reports [P5], [P7], [P9] showing risk level 5 Balanced portfolio with specified fund allocations and cost disclosures. Recommendations naming specific figures (People's Pension £6,601.55, consolidation of five pensions totalling £116,998.47 into £110,185 transfer value) and client circumstances (HGV driver, workplace scheme with ongoing contributions) demonstrate client-specificity where applied. The recommendation structure does link most arrangements to stated objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3), but the linkage for some boilerplate rationale (fund diversification, monitoring) is generic rather than bespoke to this client's situation. The comparison cannot fully assess whether all discrete recommendations carry their own client-specific rationale because the extraction identifies 6 recommendations with linked objectives but 28 rationale statements, suggesting multiple rationale statements per recommendation; however, the canonical model structure does not provide clear delineation of which rationale attaches to which specific recommendation.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that Aviva Pension Portfolio is recommended as the provider and wrapper. The 'New Solution' (Aviva Platform) ranked 5th with the lowest projected maturity value of the top five options. Passage [P24] repeats the provider strengths (A rating, 6,900 funds, etc.) verbatim from [P11].     Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' and that the receiving scheme has 'lower overall cost structure' but passage [P22] ranks the new solution 5th for projected maturity value (£125,000) versus Zurich at 1st (£128,000) and Existing Solution at 2nd (£127,000), indicating the new solution produces a lower projected outcome due to higher charges (0.6% vs 0.3% for existing).

      - Report provides provider justifications (financial strength, 6,900 funds, expertise, governance, innovation, convenience) that appear verbatim across passages [P11] and [P24], identical language across two different existing arrangement recommendations, suggesting boilerplate rather than client-specific rationale.

      - Passage [P1] states 'No filters were applied' and 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', contradicting any assertion that a comparative analysis justified provider selection over alternatives.

      - Report links 'Lower Costs' to OBJ1 but the pension switch report [P22] quantifies the cost impact: new solution requires 5.9% critical yield (at high growth, 5.88%) vs existing 0.6%, showing the new solution is substantially more expensive and will require higher growth just to break even, contradicting the 'lower costs' claim.

    The report asserts provider and product justifications that are presented as both generic (financial strength, choice, expertise, governance, innovation, convenience) and client-specific (lower costs linked to OBJ1, adviser charging facilitation linked to OBJ3, ongoing monitoring linked to OBJ1 and OBJ3). However, multiple discrepancies emerge. First, passage [P1] confirms no filters were applied and no DNA analysis was performed in the product selection, meaning the research document contains no evidence of a systematic comparison of platform alternatives. The shortlist included four platforms (Aviva, Aberdeen, Aegon, Wealthtime) but the research report provides no quantitative or qualitative benchmarking to justify Aviva's selection. Second, the critical assertion that the new solution offers 'lower costs' is contradicted by the pension switch report [P22], which ranks the new solution 5th by projected maturity (£125,000) with a 0.6% reduction in yield, compared to the Existing Solution at 2nd (£127,000) with 0.3% reduction in yield. This shows the recommended solution is materially more expensive and will deliver a lower projected outcome. The report cites 'lower costs' as a primary justification but the evidence quantifies the opposite. Third, the provider strengths (A rating, 6,900 funds, award-winning strategies, governance, innovation, convenience) appear verbatim in passages [P11] and [P24], indicating these are standard marketing claims applied identically across different pension recommendations rather than client-specific reasoning. The investment strategy rationale (risk profile alignment, diversification, active/passive blend, simplicity, competitive charges, peer performance, CIP monitoring) is stated in the report but evidence for fund selection is limited to performance data [P7] without demonstration that alternatives were compared for this specific client. The report does not explain why the 5th-ranked solution (Aviva Platform) was recommended over the 1st-ranked (Zurich) or 2nd-ranked (Existing Solution) alternatives identified in the comparative analysis. This represents a material gap: where a documented ranking exists showing higher-value outcomes from alternatives, the report's recommendation for a lower-ranked option requires explicit justification that is absent from the evidence. The guard permits firm panel or CIP membership as legitimate justification if documented; the report references CIP but does not establish that a documented panel process restricted the search or that the panel membership decision was itself justified. The false-positive guard regarding marketing strengths is triggered here: absolute claims about Aviva's strengths (rated A, 6,900 funds, etc.) do not distinguish comparative merit from marketing positioning, and no evidence shows these attributes are superior to Zurich, Aberdeen, Aegon, or Wealthtime on the metrics that matter to this client's suitability.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report presents four alternatives to the recommended solution: (1) 'Leave pot to grow until retirement' (Do Nothing) - rejected because encashing is preferred to simplify arrangements; (2) 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' (In-Situ Switch) - rejected because client's main priority is to consolidate pensions, gain better control, and access better multi-asset funds and performance through Aviva options linked to OBJ3; (3) 'Stakeholder pension with single multi-asset fund' - rejected because it has higher charges (1.5% for 10 years then 1%) and limited flexibility compared to the recommended solution, with restricted fund ranges; (4) 'Investment Pathway solutions' - rejected because they lack tailoring to client's individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile (OBJ1, OBJ2), and do not provide ongoing review and adjustment. The report also presents five research options: OPT1-OPT3 (Leave untouched, fund switch, switch to existing arrangement) listed without rejection reasons; OPT4 (Switch to employer's scheme) rejected for three reasons - loss of employer contributions, funds not meeting risk profile, higher costs (0.06% more expensive with £4.50 admin charge), and limited fund range with no multi-asset options; OPT5 (New plan) recommended. Additionally, for specific existing arrangements: People's Pension recommended to Retain; Scottish Widows recommended to Encash; Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life recommended to Switch to Aviva Platform with detailed rejection reasons citing cost, fund range, flexibility, and adviser management capabilities.

    File:     The evidence includes a Pension Switch Report comparing alternatives: (P1) a ranked table showing top 10 products ranked by Maturity Value including Zurich (0.2%, £128,000), Existing Solution (0.3%, £127,000), Aviva (0.5%, £126,000), Standard Life (0.5%, £126,000), New Solution (0.6%, £125,000), Aegon (0.6%, £125,000), Aberdeen (0.7%, £125,000), Scottish Widows (0.9%, £124,000), and Generic Stakeholder Pension (1.6%, £119,000). (P1) also presents a comparison table showing Generic Stakeholder Pension would result in -£8,000 or -6.3% decrease vs Existing Solution, and -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease vs New Solution projected to age 75 (Mid growth). (P5, P6, P9) contain detailed Pension Switch Report comparisons for Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life showing maturity values and effect of switching. (P2) contains a table of retirement options and fund availability across existing arrangements. (P1) states stakeholder pensions have 'a simple, capped charging structure' with maximum 1.5% for first 10 years then 1%. (P2) explicitly recommends 'Retain your people's pension plan' and 'Encash' Scottish Widows and provides rationales for Standard Life and Zurich switches mentioning cost, fund choice, flexibility, lifestyling, and adviser charging. (P3, P4, P11, P14, P15, P18) contain research reports showing filter selection, platform DNA analysis, product selection methodology, and investment performance data for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund. (P1) notes that funds available in Zurich 'were ranked by Maturity Value' and the research exercise compared multiple providers. (P21) notes that 'Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund that aligns with your risk rating of 5' and that consolidating would 'limit how you can withdraw funds' and 'unable to access this pension flexibly'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states the recommended New Solution (0.6% charge, £125,000) is ranked 5th and less costly than Stakeholder Pension (1.6%), but ranked comparison in P1 shows Zurich at rank 1 with 0.2% and £128,000, Existing Solution at rank 2 with 0.3% and £127,000, and New Solution at rank 5 with 0.6% and £125,000—the recommended solution ranks below both the existing arrangement and the Zurich alternative that was not recommended, contradicting the report's implication that the recommendation represents the best available option

      - Report rejects stakeholder pension citing 1.5% charge 'then 1% thereafter' as reason for preferring the solution, but P1 acknowledges 'a simple, capped charging structure' without the report engaging with the ranked comparison showing stakeholder pension (1.6%) would deliver £119,000 vs New Solution (0.6%) delivering £125,000—the charge comparison alone does not explain why the £6,000 maturity value advantage of the New Solution outweighs the simplicity and protection of capped charges

      - Report cites 'limited fund ranges' and 'no Multi-Asset funds' as rejections for stakeholder pensions and in-situ Zurich switch, but the research (P1, P3, P15) identifies only 1 product matched by filters applied to SIPP research (Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice), whereas Standard Life offers 300 funds and Zurich offers 60+ per P2 table—the evidence does not support that the recommended provider offers materially broader choice than alternatives considered

      - Report states OPT4 (employer scheme) is rejected because 'funds do not meet your risk profile; plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution with £4.50 annual admin charge', but derived figures indicate the charge comparison is unreliable: the 0.06% figure is computed on Zurich Managed Pn AP (£103,438.71) not on the Standard Life arrangement (£3,002), creating a mismatch between the arrangement being evaluated and the charge basis

      - Report recommends Zurich switch citing 'greater investment choice' and 'multi-asset fund selection' as benefits, and notes 'the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' (P20), but the performance data (P7, P19) shows Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation (existing arrangement A) at 15.58% over 5 years, below the recommended portfolio performance, and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund at 11.36% in 2021, -9.14% in 2022 vs HSBC at 12.68% and -10.52%—the evidence does not establish clear outperformance of recommended funds over existing alternatives

      - Report states research options OPT1, OPT2, OPT3 were 'Stated' in the report but the canonical model indicates 'rejectionReasons: []' (empty array) for all three, meaning no rejection reasons are provided in the report narrative, only listing that they were considered—this contradicts the requirement to state reasons for rejection

    The comparison can be performed because the evidence pack contains both the report's alternative assessments and the underlying research documents with ranked comparisons and detailed projections. The key issue is not absence of data but contradiction between what the report asserts and what the ranked evidence shows. The report satisfies the structural requirement to name alternatives and provide reasons for rejection, and those reasons are client-specific where they reference objectives (OBJ1, OBJ3) and personal circumstances (consolidation preference, withdrawal needs, employer contributions, adviser management desire). However, material inconsistencies undermine the evidential support: (1) The ranked product comparison in the Pension Switch Report (P1) places the recommended New Solution at rank 5 with projected maturity value of £125,000, below the Existing Solution (rank 2, £127,000) and Zurich (rank 1, £128,000). This ranking directly contradicts the report's framing that the recommended solution best meets the client's needs. The report does not engage with this ranking or explain why a client whose 'main priority is to consolidate pensions' and whose objectives focus on retirement income sufficiency should accept a solution that projects £2,000-£3,000 less at retirement than alternatives available at the same time of decision. (2) On the Stakeholder Pension alternative, the report rejects it primarily on cost grounds ('more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension') but does not address the ranked data showing the New Solution at 0.6% charge vs Stakeholder at 1.6%—a 1% difference—yet delivering only £6,000 more over the term. The rejection reason is abstract ('more cost effective') rather than quantified against the specific alternatives evaluated. (3) The fund range assertion ('limited ranges' in Zurich and Stakeholder, 'wider range' in Aviva) is stated generically but the evidence (P1, P2) shows Zurich offers 60+ funds, Standard Life 300, and Aviva matches only 1 product under the applied filters in the SIPP research report, indicating the 'greater choice' narrative is not evidence-based. (4) The charge comparison for the employer scheme (OPT4) cites a 0.06% difference but the derived figures flag this is computed on the wrong arrangement (Zurich values applied to Standard Life evaluation), rendering the numerical justification unreliable. (5) Performance comparison for the Zurich rejection states recommended funds 'have performed as well as Aviva options, albeit marginally', but the evidence (P7, P19) shows mixed performance: Aviva pension existing fund (A) at 15.58% vs combined recommended portfolio discrete performance (2023: 10.68%, 2024: 11.05%, YTD: 7.87%), not clearly better. (6) Three research options (OPT1, OPT2, OPT3) are listed in the research section but have no stated rejection reasons in the canonical model, violating the requirement that every alternative carry a stated reason. The report's narrative does not bridge this gap; it discusses only OPT4 and OPT5 from the research. (7) The report's rationale for each existing arrangement (Retain, Encash, Switch) is client-specific in structure but supported by contradictory or incomplete evidence: the Encash recommendation for Scottish Widows rests on simplification and tax efficiency, and this is supported; the Switch recommendations reference cost, flexibility, and fund range but the cost comparisons are computed on mismatched arrangement values and the fund range superiority is asserted not demonstrated. The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this finding. The client's status as an HGV driver with 'limited' investment experience and reliance on spousal income is noted, but the report does not show that the alternatives considered (stakeholder pension, internal fund switch, investment pathway) are inadequate for his circumstances, nor that the recommendation accounts for any special communication need or capacity constraint beyond standard suitability assessment. His stated objectives (consolidation, clarity, retirement income) are addressed by the recommendation structure but not differentiated against alternatives in a way that accounts for his particular position.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies 5 existing arrangements with stated advice actions: (1) People's Pension - Retain, with rationale that employer contributions continue until retirement; (2) Scottish Widows - Encash, with rationale about missing employer contributions if transferred and small pot tax treatment; (3) Aviva pension my future focus consolidation - FullTransfer, with rationales including lower costs, lifestyling risk, investment choice, adviser charging, and monitoring; (4) Zurich Managed Pn AP - FullTransfer, with rationales including multi-asset fund selection, lower costs, lifestyling risk, investment choice, adviser charging, monitoring, and enhanced retirement flexibility; (5) SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund - FullTransfer, with rationales including lower costs, investment choice, adviser charging, monitoring, and enhanced retirement flexibility. All rationales are marked as client-specific and stated in the report.

    File:     The retrieved evidence includes policy documents from all five providers (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life) confirming current values and plan details. Policy research checklist [P3] and [P5] confirm all five arrangements with their current values.
