People's Pension policy [P19] provides illustration and transfer information. The evidence establishes that all five arrangements exist and have been researched, but does not contain the specific rationales stated in the report for each recommended action.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states People's Pension should be retained with rationale about continuing employer contributions, but evidence [P19] shows only illustration and transfer information; no statement confirming employer will continue contributing until retirement is present in evidence.

      - Report states Scottish Widows should be encashed with specific tax calculation (25% tax-free, 75% taxed as income, £170 net estimated), but evidence [P2] contains only generic information about annuity options and does not provide the specific calculation or endorsement of encashment recommendation.

      - Report rationale for Aviva transfer cites lifestyling funds moving outside agreed risk range, but evidence [P7] confirms Aviva plan is in 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' with 'Lifestage Investment Approach' - no evidence states this will move outside the agreed risk range of 5.

      - Report states Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds and recommends transfer partly because of this limitation, but evidence [P13] regarding Zurich notes 'The client's agreed risk level is 5' without stating Zurich lacks risk 5 funds; evidence [P9] describes Zurich's target retirement fund range but does not explicitly state absence of risk 5 funds.

      - Report cites 'Lower Costs' as rationale for transfers to Aviva, but derived figures show EA5 (Standard Life, £3,002 current value) charge calculation is performed on Zurich Managed AP values (£103,438.46) rather than the correct plan, making the charge comparison unreliable for that arrangement.

      - Report states total transfer value is £110,185 on page 9, but sum of all five existing arrangement values (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002) = £116,998.47, a discrepancy of £6,813.47 unexplained in evidence.

    Each of the five existing arrangements has a stated recommended outcome (Retain, Encash, FullTransfer, FullTransfer, FullTransfer) and multiple rationales are provided for each, marked as client-specific. The rationales cover relevant topics including costs, fund choice, risk profile alignment, and ongoing monitoring. However, material discrepancies exist between the report's assertions and the evidence base. The report asserts specific factual propositions as rationales (e.g., employer contributions continuing for People's Pension, Zurich lacking risk 5 funds, specific tax calculations for Scottish Widows encashment) that are not corroborated or are contradicted by the evidence. The charge comparison for Standard Life (EA5) is computed on the wrong underlying plan value, undermining the 'lower costs' rationale. The discrepancy between stated total transfer value (£110,185) and the sum of individual values (£116,998.47) is significant and unexplained. The rationales provided are verbose and repetitive across arrangements (e.g., 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring' appear verbatim in multiple recommendations), which suggests generic language applied across different recommendations rather than discrete justification tailored to each arrangement's specific circumstances. The evidence confirms all five arrangements exist and current values are documented, but does not substantiate the key factual claims underlying the rationales, particularly for the arrangements being transferred out. The People's Pension retention rationale rests on employer contribution continuation, which is asserted but not evidenced; the Scottish Widows encashment rationale rests on specific tax calculations not found in the evidence; the transfer rationales rest partly on Zurich's fund limitations, which are not documented. The vulnerability overlay does not apply as no vulnerability characteristics materially affecting this requirement's assessment have been identified in the evidence.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome

  Potential Concern — citations unverified

    Report:   The report makes six recommendations with explicit action types (Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch, Rebalance). All six recommendations are marked as 'Recommended' in adviceStatus. All six have willAdviserImplement set to true and responsibleParty set to 'Adviser'. Each recommendation includes an intendedOutcome field describing the rationale for the change. Implementation details state: expected timescale 10-15 working days; cancellation rights of 30 days; adviser will complete nomination of beneficiaries form after transfer process begins; adviser will handle out-of-market risk disclosure. The report explicitly states the adviser will implement and take responsibility for all recommended changes.

    File:     File notes (P24) reference alternative options considered for some arrangements (e.g. 'Scottish widows – alternative option – 30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three'; 'Peoples Pension – alternative option'; 'No alternative as Defaqto risk rates all of them as a 9'). Terms and Conditions (P1-P22) describe platform mechanics, charges, drawdown options, and death benefits but contain no specific statements about whether the changes to this client's arrangements are recommended, who will implement them, or intended outcomes. The evidence does not corroborate or contradict the report's explicit statements on recommendation status, implementation responsibility, or intended outcomes for the six changes proposed.

    The requirement asks whether the report states (1) that a change is recommended, (2) who will implement it, and (3) the intended outcome. The report clearly satisfies all three limbs: (1) all six recommendations carry adviceStatus='Recommended'; (2) all six specify willAdviserImplement=true and responsibleParty='Adviser'; (3) each recommendation includes an intendedOutcome explaining why the change is suitable (e.g. consolidation, cost reduction, flexibility enhancement, diversified portfolio aligned with risk rating). The implementation section specifies timescale (10-15 working days) and states the adviser will execute the transfers and complete beneficiary nomination. The file evidence does not address these specific statements, but the guard 'An implementation step handled by the provider rather than the adviser is still a stated step; the finding is silence, not who happens to act' indicates that the report's duty is independent of supporting documentation. The report makes explicit, granular statements on each element required by the limb. No contradiction or silence exists within the report's own assertions.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states implementation timescales and responsible parties for recommendations. All six recommendations (Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch, Rebalance) assign responsibility to 'Adviser'. Expected timescale is stated as '10-15 working days' for administrative, disinvestment and banking processes. Next steps include: (1) Complete nomination of beneficiaries form after transfer process begun (stated as client-specific); (2) Review cancellation notice within 30 days (stated as generic); (3) Acknowledge out-of-market risk between realisation and purchase, noted as client-specific because 'You are comfortable with this concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access to funds' (page 8). Cancellation rights are stated as '30 days to review and consider options after plan is set up'.

    File:     The evidence contains the Aviva Pension Portfolio Terms and Conditions and Key Features documents which describe standard procedures and timeframes. P1 describes capped drawdown review processes and cancellation/change procedures for income drawdown payments with 10 working day notice requirements. P7 states Structured Products involve penalties if withdrawn before fixed term end, and full encashment may be required. P15 (Key Features) describes standard procedures: if moving to USA or Republic of Ireland, investments must be sold within 30 days; regular reviews should be conducted; adviser involvement is required for certain changes. P23 confirms 30-day cancellation period from receipt of first contribution, with statement that 'For transfers, we'll let you know when we have received t[he transfer]' but does not specify the 10-15 working day timescale. P24 describes confirmations provided online after transactions complete and quarterly statements. The documents describe provider responsibilities and standard processes, but do not corroborate the specific client-facing implementation narrative linking actions to the particular client's circumstances or confirming who (adviser firm name or individual) will execute the transfers.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts out-of-market risk acknowledgement as client-specific ('You are comfortable with this concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access to funds'), but the evidence (P9) discusses income drawdown risks and means-tested benefits generically without linking liquid cash reserves to out-of-market comfort. No evidence confirms the client's stated comfort or liquid cash position supports this risk tolerance claim.

      - The report names 'Adviser' as responsible party for all six actions, but the evidence does not name the adviser firm or individual. The guard permits 'naming the adviser firm rather than an individual', but the report uses only the generic role title.

      - The 10-15 working day timescale for overall process is stated in the report (page 8, 16) as a general administration standard ('Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete'), and this is corroborated as provider service standard in P23 (Key Features) which states procedures take time, though not with identical timing. However, the report does not distinguish between this general service standard and the specific implementation timescale for this client's recommended actions.

      - The report states cancellation rights as '30 days to review and consider options after plan is set up'. Evidence (P23) confirms 30-day cancellation period but specifies it runs 'from us receiving your first contribution' or 'when we receive the contribution' for transfers, not explicitly 'after plan is set up'. The phrase 'plan is set up' is used in the report but not verbatim in evidence.

    The comparison can be performed because the report makes specific assertions about implementation (what action, when, by whom) and the evidence base contains the provider's standard terms and client-facing documentation. The guard on 'naming the adviser firm rather than an individual' permits use of the generic role 'Adviser' if firm name is absent; the report provides no firm name, only the role. The guard on 'timescale quoted as a provider service standard' means the 10-15 working day period stated as a general administrative standard in the report is not a finding, as it reflects standard Aviva processing time evidenced in generic Key Features. The out-of-market risk disclosure presents a material issue: the report asserts the client 'is comfortable with this concept' based on having 'liquid cash', but the evidence does not corroborate that the client has confirmed comfort or that the adviser has confirmed the client's liquid cash position. This is a client-specific assertion (flagged 'isClientSpecific: true') without supporting evidence. The cancellation period timing (30 days) is corroborated by evidence; the phrase 'after plan is set up' versus 'after we receive contribution' is a minor semantic variation that reflects the same substantive protection and does not contradict suitability. All six recommendations clearly state action and nominate adviser as responsible party, meeting the basic requirement for 'what' and 'by whom'; the 10-15 day timescale meets 'when'. The omission of adviser firm name or individual identity is permitted by the guard but represents a gap in specificity. The critical concern is the client-specific assertion regarding out-of-market risk comfort, which is stated in the report but not evidenced in the case file.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report states Total Charge 0.44% but this figure is computed from Platform 0.21% + Investment Management 0.22% = 0.43% (rounded to 0.44%); however the fund charges (0.18% + 0.29%) are stated separately and do not appear to be arithmetically combined with the platform and investment management charges in the report's stated total, creating ambiguity about whether 0.44% is the complete aggregated charge or excludes fund charges. The monetary amount basis for the £484.81 total charge is stated as 'Unspecified' in the report's structured data. At 0.44% this implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which does not match: the sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47), the sum of transfer values (£116,998.47), or the stated transfer value in the switch analysis on page 9 (£110,185). Platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,185.71, and Investment Management charge of £253.43 at 0.22% implies fund value of £115,195.45. These two constituent charges are calculated on different implicit fund values, making their arithmetic sum unreliable. 17 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following costs for the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension: Platform charge 0.21%, Investment Management charge 0.22%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc fund charge 0.18%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund charge 0.29%, with a Total Charge of 0.44%. The report states monetary amounts of £231.39 (platform), £253.43 (investment management), and £484.81 (total). An initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) is disclosed as payable directly by pension schemes on transfer. An ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum is disclosed, stated as circa £1,101.85 for the first year based on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee. Transaction costs are excluded with the stated reason that they are variable and subject to change based on market conditions and trading activity, but are already accounted for in fund performance figures presented net of all charges. The report provides an aggregated total (0.44%) and states the cumulative effect will be shown in the personal illustration. A comparison table shows existing Aviva plan at 0.50% versus recommended alternative at -0.06% impact, implying 0.44% new charge.

    File:     [P1] confirms Platform 0.21% (£231.39), Investment Management 0.22% (£253.43), Total Charge 0.44% (£484.81). [P3] the Aviva illustration shows identical figures: 0.21% platform charge, fund charges of 0.29% (CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc) and 0.18% (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc), with weighted average fund charge of 0.23%. [P3] states the Aviva charge calculation is based on total value of investments through the platform service. [P1] contains the Pension Switch Charges table showing Aviva existing 0.50% vs impact of -0.06%, and explanatory text: 'Transaction costs are not fees paid to the asset manager; instead, they include expenses like broker commissions, taxes, and the cost of executing trades. Despite their variability, these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges.' [P7] discloses initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) payable directly by pension schemes on transfer, and ongoing adviser charges documented in Client Agreement with service benefits listed (reviewing suitability, market updates, access to adviser team, holistic financial planning), with cost stated as 1% per annum, circa £1,101.85 for first year. [P3] states 'The charge in monetary terms shown here may differ from the illustration due to differences in calculation methods' and 'The annual recurring charges assume no investment growth.' [P3] contains cumulative effect disclosure via personal illustration reference and shows projection table at end of year 1, 3, 5 and at age 75 with columns 'Before charges' and 'After taking all charges'. [P21] Aviva illustration shows cumulative impact: year 1 £113,000 before charges becomes £108,000 after all charges; year 5 £127,000 becomes £114,000; at age 75 £129,000 becomes £115,000, demonstrating 2.1% reduction in growth rate from 2.9% to 0.8%.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Total Charge 0.44% but this figure is computed from Platform 0.21% + Investment Management 0.22% = 0.43% (rounded to 0.44%); however the fund charges (0.18% + 0.29%) are stated separately and do not appear to be arithmetically combined with the platform and investment management charges in the report's stated total, creating ambiguity about whether 0.44% is the complete aggregated charge or excludes fund charges.

      - The monetary amount basis for the £484.81 total charge is stated as 'Unspecified' in the report's structured data. At 0.44% this implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which does not match: the sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47), the sum of transfer values (£116,998.47), or the stated transfer value in the switch analysis on page 9 (£110,185).

      - Platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,185.71, and Investment Management charge of £253.43 at 0.22% implies fund value of £115,195.45. These two constituent charges are calculated on different implicit fund values, making their arithmetic sum unreliable.

      - Ongoing adviser charge stated as 'circa £1,101.85 for the first year' but this is based on 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'; the precise calculation basis is unclear and the qualifier 'circa' introduces uncertainty not quantified.

      - The existing Aviva arrangement is listed in existing arrangements at £3,744.36 fund value with 0.50% charge (£18.72 annually). The recommended solution replaces this with Aviva Platform at effective 0.44% total charge. The report states on page 6 that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the Pension Switch Charges table shows -0.06% impact, indicating the new plan is 0.06% cheaper. These statements directly contradict each other.

      - The report asserts all five existing pensions will be transferred to the new Aviva Platform solution, with a stated total transfer value in recommendation context of £110,185. However, the sum of all five individual existing arrangement values is £116,998.47, a discrepancy of £6,813.47. The basis for the £6,813.47 reduction is not disclosed or explained in the report.

    The comparison can be performed because the report discloses specific charge percentages and amounts, and corroborating provider illustrations are present in the evidence pack. However, multiple material issues emerge. First, the arithmetic consistency of the stated total charge of 0.44% cannot be verified: the platform (0.21%) and investment management (0.22%) sum to 0.43%, and the fund charges (0.18% for HSBC + 0.29% for CT Universal MAP, weighted 50/50 = 0.235%, or approximately 0.24%) are disclosed separately in both the report and illustration but their relationship to the stated 0.44% total is not clearly explained. The evidence shows fund charges are quoted separately in [P3] (0.18% and 0.29%) and the weighted average is stated as 0.23%, suggesting the total should be approximately 0.44% (0.21 + 0.22 = 0.43%, plus approximately 0.01% rounding or fund charge incorporation), but the report does not explicitly show this calculation. Second, the monetary charge amounts are internally inconsistent: £231.39 at 0.21% and £253.43 at 0.22% imply different fund values (£110,185.71 and £115,195.45 respectively), yet both are presented as applying to the same solution. The report notes in [P3] that 'The charge in monetary terms shown here may differ from the illustration due to differences in calculation methods,' which provides a partial guard for the monetary discrepancy, but does not resolve the underlying ambiguity. Third, a critical contradiction exists on page 6 of the report versus the Pension Switch Charges table: text claims 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the charge comparison table in [P1] shows Aviva existing 0.50% with -0.06% impact, clearly indicating the new plan is 0.06% cheaper, not more expensive. This is a material factual contradiction between two statements in the same report. Fourth, the ongoing adviser charge of 'circa £1,101.85' is stated with uncertainty ('circa') and qualified as based on 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee,' but the precise basis for this calculation is not shown and it is unclear whether this differs from the stated 1% per annum ongoing charge rate. Fifth, the total transfer value discrepancy of £6,813.47 (stated transfer value £110,185 vs. sum of individual values £116,998.47) is not explained. The report does not disclose whether any pension is being retained, partially transferred, or subject to charges that reduce the transfer value; nor does it explain why the aggregated transfer value is lower than the sum of individual fund values. The extraction notes confirm this internal inconsistency: 'Sum of all five existing arrangements = £116,998.47, not £110,185. Discrepancy of £6,813.47 unexplained.' Sixth, transaction costs are disclosed as excluded with reasons [P1], and the evidence confirms this exclusion is stated with justification that costs are already reflected in net fund performance [P3]. This is a stated exclusion and is documented, hence not an omission. The cumulative effect is disclosed via reference to the personal illustration [P3], and actual projections showing cumulative impact over time are present in [P21]. The benefits of the ongoing 1% adviser charge are articulated in [P7] (reviewing suitability, market updates, access to adviser team, holistic financial planning) and are consistent across the evidence. However, the report does not explicitly compare these benefits against the 1% cost or justify why this additional charge is warranted relative to alternatives. The charges in the report are supported by and consistent with provider illustrations [P3], [P21] for the product and investment layers. The advice fees match the client agreement in respect of the 3% initial fee and 1% ongoing (both disclosed in [P7]); no alternative fee structure is evident in the evidence. However, the arithmetic inconsistencies in monetary amounts and the fund value discrepancy create material uncertainty about whether the stated charges are correctly calculated and applied.

At age 75 Before charges £129,000.00, After taking all charges £115,000.00. All charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £115,000.00 - this is the same as reducing the investment growth after inflation from 2.9% to 0.8%. This is a reduction in the growth rate of 2.1%."

        "Your personal illustration contains further information regarding the cumulative effect the charges will have on your investment returns."

 

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Initial fee: 3% of amount invested, £3,305.55, paid by provider on transfer. Ongoing fee: 1% per annum of fund value, approximately £1,101.85 in first year, paid via Aviva Platform. Both fees stated to be 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement'.

    File:     Client Agreement [P4], [P9], [P11], [P13], [P21] states fees are 'set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation' and confirms a service model with 'a fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews'. Agreement references rates of 3% initial and 1% ongoing in [P19] example table. No maximum stated in agreement; agreement permits amendment with 30 days notice. Suitability report [P3], [P23] cites these rates explicitly.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states initial fee is 3% of 'amount invested' (£3,305.55) but derived transfer value total is £116,998.47; £3,305.55 is only 2.83% of this total, not 3%

      - Report states ongoing fee is 1% of 'fund value' (£1,101.85) but does not specify which fund value this is calculated from; derived figures show ongoing charges (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment = 0.44%) on £110,184.09 basis, not 1% on any stated total

      - Arithmetic: £3,305.55 ÷ £110,185 (the implied fund value from platform charge calculation) = 3.00%, but this does not match the stated transfer value total of £116,998.47

      - Client Agreement does not state the initial fee percentage or ongoing fee percentage explicitly; it only references that fees are 'set out in the suitability report'—creating circularity rather than independent corroboration

    The report asserts both fees are compliant with 'the terms stated in our Client Agreement' [P3]. The Client Agreement [P4], [P9], [P11], [P21] confirms a two-tier fee model (initial and ongoing) but does not itself state the percentage rates or amounts; instead, it delegates to the suitability report. This creates a dependency chain rather than independent verification: the agreement says 'fees are set out in the suitability report', and the report says they comply with the agreement. The Client Agreement [P19] does contain an example of 1% ongoing charge in a different context ('For most investment funds... we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1%'), which corroborates the 1% rate stated in the report. However, the initial fee of 3% is not explicitly stated in any document except the suitability report itself [P3], [P23]. The agreement [P4] permits fees to be amended 'to reflect changes in costs, charges, taxes, duties or interest' with 30 days notice, but this is prospective, not retrospective. The arithmetic reveals a hidden discrepancy: the report cites £3,305.55 as 3% but this figure arises from a transfer value of £110,185, not the sum of all five plans (£116,998.47). The extraction identified that the platform charge (0.21%) on £110,185 yields £231.39, investment management charge (0.22%) yields £253.43, and combined 0.44% yields £484.81 [P10]. This implies the solution is being charged on a reduced basis—the initial advice fee of £3,305.55 is 3% of £110,185, a figure that does not appear anywhere in the evidence as a named plan value or confirmed transfer total. The Client Agreement itself does not establish any specific rate as the binding rate; it states fees 'may be charged' and shall be 'agreed', but the only explicit agreement to rates appears only in the suitability report's own assertions. No separate signed schedule, fee letter, or client confirmation is present establishing the 3% and 1% rates independently of the report. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to affect this check materially: no evidence of communication needs, learning support, or adjustments is cited that would alter the suitability of the fee structure or the ability to compare stated rates to the agreement.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P3)

        "For researching, recommending and arranging these investments, we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values, this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values. This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P23)

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report presents reduction in yield (RIY) figures and critical yield figures across three growth scenarios. For the recommended plan: Low growth: RIY 2.1%, Critical Yield 1.9%, Effect of switching -£10,400 (9.6%). Mid growth: RIY 2.2%, Critical Yield 4.8%, Effect of switching -£12,000 (9.4%). High growth: RIY 2.3%, Critical Yield 7.8%, Effect of switching -£15,000 (10.0%). For existing arrangements: RIY 0.3% across all scenarios. The report states these figures show 'the cumulative effect the charges will have on your investment returns' and that 'all charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £115,000.00 - this is the same as reducing the investment growth after inflation from 2.9% to 0.8%. This is a reduction in the growth rate of 2.1%'. Annual platform charge £231.39 at 0.21%, investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22%, total recurring charge £484.81 at 0.44%.

    File:     Evidence from [P18] (Suitability Report extract) corroborates all RIY and critical yield figures for the recommended plan: Low 2.1%/1.9%, Mid 2.2%/4.8%, High 2.3%/7.8%, and all effect of switching amounts: £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), £15,000 (10.0%). Evidence from [P3] (Illustration) states 'All charges could reduce this pension fund from £129,000.00 to £115,000.00 - this is the same as reducing the investment growth after inflation from 2.9% to 0.8%. This is a reduction in the growth rate of 2.1%.' Evidence from [P6] (Illustration) specifies platform charge 0.21% and investment-related charges: CT Universal MAP Balanced 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 0.18%, Weighted Average 0.23%. Evidence from [P7] (Report table) shows platform 0.21%/£231.39, investment management 0.22%/£253.43, total 0.44%/£484.81. Evidence from [P4] states the critical yield requirement: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states RIY 2.1% (Low growth) but the underlying fund charges (HSBC 0.18% + CT Universal MAP 0.29% = average 0.235%) plus platform 0.21% plus adviser fees 1.00% should produce different cumulative impact, though the illustration basis may differ from report basis

      - Report table at [P7] shows investment management charge 0.22% but fund KIIDs at [P6] show CT Universal MAP 0.29% and HSBC 0.18% with weighted average 0.23%, creating a 0.01% discrepancy in the stated investment management component

      - Report states effect of switching as -£10,400, -£12,000, -£15,000 but the derived figure computation shows solution charge figures (platform £231.39 at 0.21%, investment management £253.43 at 0.22%) imply a fund value of £110,184-£115,195, which does not reconcile to the individual existing arrangement values (Zurich £103,439.24, People's £6,601.55, Aviva £3,744.36, Scottish Widows £211.32, Standard Life £3,002) totalling £116,998.47, creating ambiguity about which fund value base the switching costs are calculated on

    The cumulative impact of costs is presented through RIY and critical yield metrics. All figures stated in the report for RIY (2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3% for recommended; 0.3% for existing) and critical yield (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) are corroborated by [P18]. The effect of switching amounts (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) and percentages (9.6%, 9.4%, 10.0%) are also corroborated by [P18]. The illustration at [P3] confirms the cumulative reduction in growth rate of 2.1% due to all charges. However, there is an internal computational concern: the platform and investment management charges as stated in the report table (0.21% + 0.22% = 0.43%, shown as 0.44% total) do not precisely reconcile with the fund charges shown in the KIIDs (0.29% + 0.18% = 0.235% weighted average, or 0.23% stated). The report excludes transaction costs from the charged figures but states they are already accounted for in fund performance figures net of all charges. The discrepancy between the total existing fund value (£116,998.47) and the charge calculation basis (which appears to be approximately £110,185) is noted in the extraction but the report does not explicitly address this. The report does present all-in cost impact through the RIY metric, which aggregates product, platform, fund and adviser charges. The adviser fee of 1.00% per annum is disclosed separately and included in critical yield calculations. The comparison is performable and supported by evidence; however, the reconciliation between individual charge percentages and the aggregate RIY figures depends on illustration assumptions (prescribed growth rates, inflation adjustments) which are explained in [P3] and [P18] but the exact computational path from component charges to aggregate RIY is not fully transparent in the report itself, though the final figures are corroborated.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts on page 11 that 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'This means that your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year (in monetary terms).' The Pension Switch Charges table on page 11 shows: Aviva existing charge 0.50% (£18.72), impact -0.06% (£-2.25); Zurich existing charge 0.93% (£961.98), impact -0.49% (£-506.85); Standard Life existing charge 0.18% (£186.19), impact +0.26% (£268.94). The overall comparison basis is stated as 'LikeForLike' on page 11. The recommended solution charges are given on page 11 as Aviva Platform 0.21% platform charge (£231.39) plus 0.22% investment management (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81). Existing arrangements charges on page 5 are: People's Pension 0.50% fund + £4.50 admin; Scottish Widows 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform; Aviva 0.50% fund; Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12 monthly admin; Standard Life 0.52% fund.

    File:     Evidence P1 (page 5 table) corroborates existing arrangements fund values and charges: People's Pension £6,601.55 at 0.50% fund + £4.50 annual admin; Scottish Widows £211.32 at 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform; Aviva £3,744.36 at 0.50% fund; Zurich £103,439.24 at 0.18% fund + £2.12 monthly admin; Standard Life £3,002 at 0.52% fund. P3 (page 11) shows recommended Aviva Platform charges as 0.21% platform (£231.39), 0.22% investment management (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81). The Pension Switch Charges table in P3 lists three plans: Aviva 0.50% existing, Standard Life 0.18% existing, and a third entry. However, the table structure in P3 is truncated and does not clearly show all per-arrangement rows. P3 states 'The difference in monetary terms is based on the current fund value of the existing plan' but the calculation method is ambiguous.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's overall comparison figure of -0.24% (0.24% cheaper) cannot be reconciled from the per-arrangement impacts shown. Aviva -0.06%, Zurich -0.49%, Standard Life +0.26% do not aggregate to -0.24% unless weighted by fund values. Computing: (3,744.36 × -0.06% + 103,439.24 × -0.49% + 3,002 × 0.26%) / total = (-2.25 - 506.85 + 7.81) / 110,185.60 = -501.29 / 110,185.60 = -0.45%, not -0.24%.

      - The monetary comparison figure of -£264 in year one does not match the per-arrangement impacts. Reported impacts are -£2.25, -£506.85, +£268.94, which sum to -£240.16, not -£264.

      - The Standard Life row in the comparison shows existing charge 0.18% (£186.19). Evidence P1 table lists Standard Life charge as 0.52% fund charge, not 0.18%. The £186.19 calculation at 0.18% implies a fund value of £103,438.89, which is the Zurich plan's value (£103,439.24), not Standard Life's (£3,002). The row is computed on the wrong plan.

      - The Zurich existing charge in the comparison table is stated as 0.93%, but evidence P1 table shows Zurich charge as 0.18% fund + £2.12 monthly admin charge. The 0.93% figure does not appear in any evidence and cannot be derived from the Zurich product documentation.

      - The Zurich impact of -0.49% and £-506.85 are computed against a fund value of £103,439.24 (visible in the arithmetic check), which is correct for Zurich, but the existing charge baseline of 0.93% is unsupported.

      - The recommended solution transfer value basis is unclear. The report's stated charges (£231.39 at 0.21%, £253.43 at 0.22%) imply a fund value of approximately £110,185, but the sum of all existing arrangement values is £116,998.47. The comparison is therefore not made on a consistent basis (the report compares against £110,185 but existing plans total £116,998.47).

      - The Aviva existing charge is listed as 0.50% in the comparison table (matching P1 evidence), but the Aviva arrangement value in the comparison is £3,744.36 (from P1), yet the new solution is sized at £110,185 (implied by the charge calculations). This is not a like-for-like comparison of the same Aviva plan; it compares an old small Aviva holding to a much larger new consolidated arrangement.

      - The report states on page 6 (per extraction) 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' regarding Aviva, but the table shows Aviva impact as -0.06% (i.e., 0.06% cheaper), contradicting the text statement.

      - No aggregation of all five existing plan charges to a weighted baseline is shown for comparison to the recommended 0.44%. Without this, the reader cannot verify whether -0.24% is correct.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Correct existing charge percentage for Standard Life (listed as 0.18% in comparison but is 0.52% in policy; row appears to be computed on Zurich data), Correct existing charge percentage for Zurich (listed as 0.93% in comparison but only 0.18% fund charge is documented; composition of 0.93% is not explained or evidenced), Clarification of the transfer value basis: whether the £110,185 implied by the solution charge calculations includes or excludes the People's Pension and Scottish Widows holdings that are retained, or whether it is correct at all, Aggregated baseline charge figure showing how the overall -0.24% is computed from the per-arrangement impacts, with weightings by plan value disclosed, Reconciliation of the -£264 annual impact figure to the three per-arrangement impacts shown (-£2.25, -£506.85, +£268.94), Confirmation whether the text statement 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' (page 6) contradicts the table showing -0.06% impact, or whether these refer to different metrics

    The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the per-arrangement charge figures contain material errors. The Standard Life and Zurich rows in the Pension Switch Charges table are computed on mismatched data: the Standard Life row uses the Zurich fund value (£103,439.24), and the Zurich existing charge (0.93%) has no evidentiary support — evidence P1 shows only 0.18% fund charge for Zurich. The guard 'Recompute' applies: when a monetary charge is not the stated percentage of the stated plan value, rows may be transposed or erroneous. Here, £186.19 at 0.18% does equal approximately £103,438.89, but that is the Zurich value, not Standard Life's £3,002. The Zurich 0.93% figure does not reconcile with any documented charge. The report's overall -0.24% figure does not arithmetically aggregate from the three per-arrangement impacts when weighted by their fund values (calculation yields -0.45%). The £264 annual saving does not sum from the per-arrangement amounts. The comparison basis is also confused: the solution charges imply a fund value of £110,185, but the five existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47, a difference of £6,813.47 (as noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies). This unexplained gap means the comparison is not made on a consistent basis. The report does not disclose which arrangements are included or excluded from the comparison, or why. The text statement on page 6 that the Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive' contradicts the table entry of -0.06% impact (meaning 0.06% cheaper). No guard explains these contradictions; they reflect calculation errors and internal inconsistency rather than legitimate differences in basis. The evidence establishes that Standard Life's actual charge is 0.52%, not the 0.18% used in the comparison. The evidence does not support the 0.93% Zurich charge or the aggregation method used to reach -0.24%. The comparison is arithmetically unsound and internally contradictory.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts lower overall cost structure (page 7), lifestyling risk mitigation, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset diversification, and enhanced retirement flexibility as benefits. Critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 is stated as achievable based on past performance. Recommended Aviva advised funds claimed to have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing holdings. The funds are stated to align with agreed risk tolerance and deliver comparable performance with reduced volatility. Total ongoing charge for new solution stated as 0.44% (platform 0.21% + investment management 0.22%). Fund recommendation shows HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%.

    File:     P1 states 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' and 'there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.' P2 reports: recommended Aviva advised funds have not outperformed current Zurich funds (Zurich 46.14% vs Aviva 42.40% over 5 years). P2 notes Zurich investments are higher-risk funds with greater volatility. P6 shows fund charges: HSBC 0.18%, CT Universal MAP 0.29%. P13 shows new solution day one value £106,880.60 versus existing £110,185.60, a shortfall of £3,305. P17 shows Zurich retained at 0.29% charge with £120,000 projected maturity, new solution at 0.6% with £3,430 projected maturity. P3 and P22 show combined performance of recommended funds at 42.40% over 5 years. Pension switch report (P17, P19) shows critical yield scenarios ranging from 0.3% to 5.9% depending on growth assumptions, not 4.8% as single stated figure. No evidence of 4.8% critical yield calculation presented.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'Lower Costs - lower overall cost structure' but the arithmetic from extracted values shows new solution charges (0.44%) when applied to the transfer value generate figures that do not reconcile cleanly with stated individual fund charges.

      - Report states critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 is 'achievable' based on past performance; evidence in P17 and P19 shows critical yield ranges from 0.3% to 5.9% depending on growth scenarios, not a single 4.8% figure.

      - Report asserts recommended Aviva funds demonstrate 'stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' (page 13); P2 confirms this for Aviva comparison but simultaneously reports Zurich funds (46.14%) have outperformed recommended Aviva funds (42.40%) over 5 years.

      - Report asserts benefits of moving to new scheme; P1 explicitly states 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider.'

      - New solution day one value (£106,880.60 per P13) is £3,305 lower than existing pension value (£110,185.60), creating immediate loss not explained in benefits narrative.

      - Report claims platform provides 'adviser charging facilitation' as benefit; no evidence in file of adviser charging structure, rates, or how this facilitates client benefit versus existing arrangements.

      - Platform charge calculation: 0.21% stated as platform charge on P17 new solution, but the basis value for this percentage is unclear and does not match any single existing arrangement value per derived figures.

    The report presents seven benefits to justify the recommendation but the evidence does not consistently support them. (1) Lower costs claim: The report states 'lower overall cost structure' but the derived figures show platform charge (0.21%) and investment management (0.22%) sum to 0.44%, while individual fund charges are 0.18% and 0.29%. The critical issue is that this charge structure cannot be arithmetically reconciled against the stated plan values per the computation rules. (2) Performance and outperformance: The report claims past performance supports achieving 4.8% critical yield; P17 and P19 show critical yield ranges of 0.3% to 5.9% depending on growth rate assumptions. No single 4.8% yield is evidenced. The report simultaneously claims Aviva funds show 'stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' which P2 confirms, but P2 also shows those same recommended Aviva funds (42.40% 5-year) have not outperformed the Zurich funds (46.14% 5-year) the client currently holds. (3) Risk and volatility benefit: P2 acknowledges Zurich funds are higher-risk with greater volatility, justifying the comparison; however, the narrative benefit of 'reduced volatility' in the recommended funds is presented as unqualified advantage without acknowledging the client's agreed risk rating is 5 (balanced/higher risk) and the recommended funds also carry risk rating 5. (4) No guarantee statement: P1 contains explicit disclaimer that switching does not guarantee higher pension fund or outperformance, directly contradicting the implicit promise in the benefits narrative. (5) Immediate value loss: P13 shows the new solution creates an immediate £3,305 shortfall (£106,880.60 versus £110,185.60), which is not disclosed or explained in the benefits section. (6) Adviser charging facilitation: Claimed as benefit on page 7 but no supporting evidence of adviser charging structure, rates, or how this benefits the client versus existing arrangements exists in the file. (7) Guard application: The guard requires supporting performance data before accepting 'achievable' claims; the stated 4.8% critical yield lacks discrete supporting calculation, only reference to 'past performance of recommended funds' which P22 shows at 42.40% cumulative 5-year, not a forward-looking critical yield metric. The consistency check fails on multiple limbs: the report's benefits narrative is not supported by consistent evidence (performance not shown to outperform on-hold choice), cost claims are not arithmetically transparent (platform and fund charges do not reconcile cleanly), and higher or comparable costs are not offset by quantified benefits (adviser charging, flexibility benefits are asserted but not evidenced with client-specific value analysis).

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P2)

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report makes two assertions about costs: (1) the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans when adviser fees are included, stated on page 11 as 'our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans'; (2) the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans when considering annual recurring charges only, also stated on page 11 as 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans'. The report frames the 4.24% figure as the 'required outperformance' needed to justify the recommendation despite higher all-in costs.

    File:     The case file contains no independent evidence passages to corroborate or contradict these figures, as the comparison is internal to the report itself. However, the derived figures computed from the report's own extracted values reveal critical arithmetic problems: (1) The solution total ongoing charge of £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, but the actual total transfer value across all five arrangements is £116,998.47 — a mismatch of £6,814.38. (2) EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% is computed on Zurich Managed Pn AP's value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's value (£3,002.00), making this row unreliable. (3) EA5 impact charge of £268.94 at 0.26% is similarly computed on the wrong arrangement. (4) The required outperformance figure of 4.24% at £4,672.00 implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which does not match any stated arrangement value or total.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states ongoing annual recurring charges are 0.24% less expensive, but derived fund values used in that calculation (£110,184.09) do not align with actual total transfer value of £116,998.47

      - Report states Aviva plan requires 4.24% outperformance to justify recommendation, but this figure is calculated against an implied fund value of £110,188.68 which does not match any stated arrangement value

      - Report's charge comparison appears to use selective or incorrect fund values for one or more arrangements, particularly Standard Life (EA5) which should have £3,002 current value but rows are calculated against £103,438.46-£103,438.89

    The report makes internal comparative assertions about costs and required outperformance. However, the arithmetic underlying these assertions is unreliable. The derived figures reveal that the charge calculations in the recommendation table operate on implied fund values that do not correspond to the stated transfer value total or individual arrangement values. Most critically: (1) The ongoing charge calculation (0.44% = £484.81) implies a fund value of £110,185, which is £6,814 less than the stated total transfer value of £116,999. This means either the charge figure, the transfer value, or both are internally inconsistent. (2) The Standard Life arrangement (EA5) with a current value of £3,002 is treated in charge rows as if it were worth £103,438, suggesting computational error in at least two rows. (3) The 4.24% required outperformance figure cannot be verified because its calculation basis (fund value of £110,189) is not independently validated. The report's comparative cost assertions therefore rest on calculations that appear arithmetically or logically impossible when tested against the report's own stated values. The discrepancy between the implied fund value in charge calculations (£110,185) and the actual total transfer value (£116,999) is material and suggests either: charges are understated (if applied to only part of the fund), or the transfer value stated elsewhere in the report is incorrect. Either way, the cost comparison cannot be relied upon. Additionally, the report does not substantively address benefits justifying the 4.24% higher all-in cost — it merely asserts the required outperformance as a mathematical fact without explaining what benefits the client receives in exchange for bearing that cost premium.

 

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states that the recommended Aviva Platform solution provides 'Lower Costs' with 'a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension, meaning more of your savings remain invested for growth rather than being lost to charges.' For Standard Life, it states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.' For Zurich, the report recommends switching and states the new scheme has 'a lower overall cost structure.' The report presents the solution as superior in cost terms to justify the recommendation and claims this benefit supports the switch decision.

    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Aviva Platform charges of 0.21% platform fee + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total ongoing cost [P8, P13, P16]. (2) Zurich existing arrangement shows 0.93% charge on £103,439 fund value [derived from figures]. (3) Standard Life shows FAD available but specific charge not disclosed in retrieved passages. (4) Aviva existing arrangement shows 0.50% charge. (5) The derived figures show the solution transfer value (£110,185) does not match the sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47), a discrepancy of £6,813.47 unexplained. (6) The pension switch report [P16] contains comparative analysis but no passage retrieved directly compares all-in costs across the five arrangements being consolidated. (7) No evidence retrieved quantifies the benefits (investment choice, flexibility, adviser facilitation) in monetary or performance terms to offset any cost difference. (8) The report claims lower costs but the comparative cost tables in the switch report [P16] are not fully cited in passages retrieved.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims Standard Life recommendation states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the arithmetic in the derived figures shows this comparison rests on charges calculated against the Zurich fund value (£103,438.46), not Standard Life's own fund value (£3,002), making the comparison unreliable.

      - Report asserts 'lower overall cost structure' for Aviva Platform (0.44% total) vs. Zurich (0.93%), but the transfer value used to calculate Aviva charges (£110,185) does not match any single existing arrangement or the sum of all five existing arrangements (£116,998.47), so the cost comparison is not on equivalent fund values.

      - Report recommends switching five pensions to consolidate but the total transfer value stated (£110,185 on page 6 per P12) is £6,813.47 less than the sum of existing arrangement values (£116,998.47), an unexplained loss that contradicts the claim that more funds will remain invested.

      - Report claims benefits of 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', and 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' but no evidence quantifies these benefits in cost or performance terms, nor shows how they offset any higher costs relative to alternatives.

      - The pension switch report [P16] references 'Alternative comparisons' and 'Effect of switching' but the specific comparative cost tables are not retrieved in full; the report's own statement about cost savings cannot be verified against the detailed switch analysis.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Specific ongoing charges for Standard Life arrangement (P24 confirms fund management charge varies by fund chosen but exact rate not stated in retrieved passages), Complete transfer value reconciliation: why £110,185 recommended transfer differs from £116,998.47 sum of existing arrangements, Detailed charge comparison table from pension switch report showing side-by-side all-in costs for each of the five existing arrangements vs. the new Aviva solution (referenced in P16 but not fully retrieved), Quantified benefit analysis: monetary value or performance impact attributed to 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', and 'Ongoing Monitoring' relative to alternatives, Adviser fee schedule and how ongoing adviser charges will be applied to the £110,185 solution, since report mentions 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' as a benefit but no fee structure is disclosed in retrieved passages

    The check requires that where costs are referenced and higher costs arise, the report must clearly explain the benefits expected in exchange and support them with evidence. The comparison cannot be reliably performed because: (1) The transfer value used to calculate new solution charges (£110,185) is not equal to any existing arrangement or the aggregate of all five (£116,998.47), creating an inconsistency of £6,813.47 that is material to any cost comparison. The report does not explain this loss. (2) The report asserts 'Lower Costs' but the arithmetic for the comparison—particularly for Standard Life—is computed on the wrong fund value (Zurich's £103,438, not Standard Life's £3,002), per the extraction's own assessment that 'EA5 existing annual charge' and 'EA5 impact' rows are 'computed on the wrong plan'. This means the comparison on which the report's cost claim rests is unreliable. (3) The report claims benefits ('Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring') but provides no quantified evidence of these benefits—no performance projection, no fee savings, no measurable value relative to alternatives. The investment pathways analysis on P21 acknowledges that investment pathways offer 'Potentially lower costs' and 'Reduced complexity' but dismisses them in favour of a tailored solution without cost comparison. (4) No complete charge comparison table spanning all five existing arrangements and the new solution is retrieved; the pension switch report [P16] is referenced but the detailed comparative tables are not fully available for verification. (5) The adviser fee structure for the new arrangement is not disclosed, yet the report presents 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' as a benefit—a cost layer that should be quantified if it is to justify higher total charges or is presented as an advantage. The false-positive guard states: 'A cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons (fund range, access, servicing) is a valid outcome.' This applies to some rejections (e.g., Scottish Widows rejected because it does not offer risk level 5 funds and does not allow adviser management; People's Pension rejected for similar reasons), but the guard does not excuse the report's failure to disclose all material cost layers or to reconcile the transfer value discrepancy. The guard protects a comparison where non-cost factors explain the choice; it does not permit silence on the reconciliation of fund values used in cost calculations or omission of adviser fee disclosure when adviser facilitation is presented as a benefit.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts six RIY figures across three growth bases (Low, Mid, High) for both Recommended and Existing arrangements, all to age 75: Recommended Low 2.1%, Mid 2.2%, High 2.3%; Existing Low 0.3%, Mid 0.3%, High 0.3%. Critical yield figures are stated for Recommended only: Low 1.9%, Mid 4.8%, High 7.8%. Effect of switching is stated as: Low £10,400 (9.6% decrease), Mid £12,000 (9.4% decrease), High £15,000 (10.0% decrease), all at age 75.

    File:     Passage P10 (Pension switch report) contains a table titled 'Existing pension' vs 'New solution' showing RIY and critical yield at three growth bases to age 75. For Low growth (0.00%): Existing 0.3%, New solution 2.1%, Critical yield 1.9%, Effect of switching £10,400 or 9.6% decrease. For Mid growth (2.95%): Existing 0.3%, New solution 2.2%, Critical yield 4.8%, Effect of switching £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. For High growth (5.88%): Existing 0.3%, New solution 2.3%, Critical yield 7.8%, Effect of switching £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. Passage P6 (Illustration - new.md) shows cumulative charge impact reducing growth from 2.9% to 2.1% (reduction of 0.4% for product and investment charges; 2.1% total reduction including adviser charges). Passage P3 (Scottish Widows Policy Info) shows RIY calculation: growth rate from 2.7% reduced to 2.4% after charges, a reduction in growth of 0.3% per year.

    All RIY figures asserted in the report are corroborated by the pension switch report (P10) at matching growth bases and retirement age. The Recommended RIY figures (2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3%) match exactly across Low, Mid and High growth scenarios. The Existing RIY figures (0.3%, 0.3%, 0.3%) are also consistently matched. Critical yield figures (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) are corroborated for the Recommended solution at each growth basis. Effect of switching amounts (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) and percentages (9.6%, 9.4%, 10.0%) are all matched in P10. The cumulative cost illustration in P6 shows the overall reduction in yield effect (2.1% total) when adviser charges are included, which aligns with the report's disclosure of these combined impacts. The guard regarding growth rate basis is satisfied: all comparisons are made at the same prescribed growth basis (Low 0.00%, Mid 2.94%/2.95%, High 5.88%) and retirement age (75). No contradictions exist between the stated RIY/critical yield figures and the evidence. The pension switch report provides comprehensive corroboration for all material yield and switching impact figures.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1% per annum (page 17, 'Future contact and ongoing services' section). The services included for this charge are: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure portfolio remains suitable; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit needs; (6) Continuous review of risk profile moving forward in line with ongoing review meetings. Review frequency is stated as annually.

    File:     The client agreement (P5, P16) confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum). P5 describes the service as including: 'Review meeting at least once a year and recommendations which may include the following as part of this service; bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk', 'Statements and Performance Reports twice per year', 'Cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial', 'Family benefit where you can have an adviser fee free advice for direct family members for their ISA or pension set up on top of up to three hours advice for your children', 'Regular market updates', 'Quarterly e-magazine', 'On-line access for portfolio valuations', and 'Access to your adviser should you need us in the meantime'. The client agreement (P1) states fees are 'set out in the suitability report' and that for ongoing reviews 'we will do on your behalf' there is 'a fee based upon any ongoing reviews'. P21 describes ongoing services as 'Providing periodic on-going reviews of your existing and/or any new investments we arrange on your behalf to ensure they remain suitable for you'.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report describes 'reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable' whereas the evidence describes this more specifically as part of 'Review meeting at least once a year' with 'updated fact find and attitude to risk' as one element among several (fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance). The report's formulation treats risk review as a standalone service proposition whereas the evidence presents it as one component of a broader annual review meeting.

      - The report states 'Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation' as a discrete service. The evidence (P5) states 'Regular market updates' and 'Quarterly e-magazine' but does not explicitly mention legislative updates.

      - The report states 'Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs' as an ongoing service included in the 1% annual charge. The evidence (P1) discusses 'other work not directly linked to a particular investment' for which 'we will agree a fee based on our hourly rate, either one-off or ongoing before we undertake this work', suggesting holistic planning beyond the ongoing review scope may incur separate fees. This creates ambiguity about whether comprehensive holistic planning is included in the 1% or charged separately.

      - The report does not mention specific deliverables such as 'Statements and Performance Reports twice per year', 'Quarterly e-magazine', 'On-line access for portfolio valuations', or 'Family benefit' (fee-free advice for direct family members) which are explicitly listed in P5 as part of the ongoing service. The report's description is less granular and omits these concrete service elements.

    The core 1% annual ongoing adviser charge is consistently stated in both the report (page 17) and the client agreement evidence (P5, P16). The review frequency of 'at least annually' is also aligned between the report ('Annually') and the evidence (P5). However, the service proposition differs in scope and specificity. The report lists five conceptual service themes (risk review, portfolio suitability, market updates, ad-hoc advice access, holistic planning) presented as discrete service propositions. The evidence (P5) presents an integrated annual review meeting that includes specific, itemised service components: review meeting at least once yearly, portfolio recommendations (including fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, rebalancing), statements twice yearly, cash flow analysis, family benefits, market updates, e-magazine, online access, and adviser access. The report's description is more abstract and higher-level; the evidence is more concrete and operationalised. The discrepancy about 'holistic financial planning' is material: the report includes it as part of the ongoing 1% service, but P1 (Client Agreement) indicates that work 'not directly linked to a particular investment' incurs separate fees on an hourly basis agreed in advance. This suggests the 1% covers periodic reviews of existing arrangements, not comprehensive holistic planning of new life events or financial restructuring, which would be charged separately. The report does not clarify this boundary. The omission from the report of specific deliverables (statements twice per year, quarterly e-magazine, online access, family benefit advice) that are explicitly promised in P5 means the client may not have full transparency of what they are receiving for their 1% fee based on the report alone. Under the SetCoverage method with no guard that explains these differences, the report's service proposition is materially less complete and specific than the evidence. The assertion 'full holistic financial planning' overstates what the 1% covers relative to the client agreement's hourly-rate structure for work outside periodic reviews. The report should have either aligned its description with the itemised list in P5 or explicitly cross-referenced the client agreement and clarified the boundary between included review services and additional chargeable work.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts 8 benefits to justify the recommended solution: (1) Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension, meaning more savings remain invested for growth; (2) Current lifestyling funds automatically reduce investment risk as approaching retirement, eventually moving portfolio outside agreed risk range; (3) Greater investment choice allowing more tailored strategy suited to risk profile with scope for future adjustments; (4) Adviser charging facilitation allowing direct deduction of charges from plan without separate payments; (5) Ongoing monitoring and adjustment of investments to ensure alignment with changing needs and market conditions; (6) Multi-asset fund selection providing professionally managed diversified investment solution aligned with financial goals and risk profile; (7) Enhanced retirement flexibility providing more flexible retirement options for tax-efficient withdrawal strategies; (8) Based on past performance of recommended funds, the plan is believed achievable to outperform by critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75; (9) Recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings; (10) Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility; (11) Ongoing review service includes attitude to risk assessment, investment suitability review, market updates, legislation changes, ad-hoc advice access and holistic financial planning.

    File:     The evidence pack contains: (1) Key Features document [P2, P6, P19] confirming Aviva Pension Portfolio offers flexible retirement options including flexi-access drawdown, UFPLS, annuity options, and tax-free lump sum options. (2) SIPP research report [P7, P21, P24] showing recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), with combined aggregated fund costs of £406.74 (0.37%), and risk level 5 matching agreed risk level. (3) Fund factsheets [P12, P15, P18] for HSBC fund showing it is actively managed with ongoing charges of 0.18%, transaction costs 0.01%, total 0.19%, and fund rating 4 on risk scale (medium fluctuations). (4) Pension switch report [P10, P14] showing projected value at age 75 for existing solution £127,000-£141,000 vs new solution £125,000-£138,000, representing £2,000-£3,000 (1.6-2.1%) decrease, with critical yield 6.3% required to match existing, and RIY 0.6% for new solution. (5) Existing arrangements show Aviva pension my future focus consolidation current value £3,744.36 at 0.50% charge, and Zurich Managed at £103,439.24 at 0.93% charge. (6) Key Features [P2] states 'You should retain the services of a financial adviser' and 'Your adviser will be able to give you more information'. (7) Scottish Widows policy info [P16] confirms Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing advisor charging. (8) Peoples Pension [P4] offers 3 investment profiles (balanced, cautious, adventurous) with automatic allocation. (9) Test Case research [P3, P5] shows existing arrangements include lifestyling strategies in some plans (People's Pension, Scottish Widows).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension' but pension switch report [P10, P14] shows new solution projected value £125,000-£138,000 vs existing £127,000-£141,000, a reduction in projected outcome of £2,000-£3,000 (1.6-2.1%), contradicting the lower cost assertion when critical yield of 6.3% is required for new solution to match existing.

      - Report claims benefits of ongoing monitoring and adviser charging facilitation, but existing arrangements include Scottish Widows Money4Life Plans [P16] which 'do not support ongoing advisor charging', and Standard Life and People's Pension which do not evidence ongoing review capabilities in the file.

      - Report asserts outperformance at critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75, but pension switch report [P14] states critical yield required is 6.3%, not 4.8%, creating a 1.5 percentage point discrepancy in the outperformance requirement.

      - Report claims 'Aviva platform funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' but the only Aviva existing holding is the pension my future focus consolidation, and no comparative performance data is provided in the evidence to support this claim.

      - Report asserts 'comparable performance with reduced volatility' for Aviva platform funds vs existing, but HSBC fund factsheet [P15] shows it is actively managed 'aim of maximising returns in line with risk profile' with 'medium fluctuations' (category 4), and no volatility comparison data is evidenced between existing Aviva fund and recommended funds.

      - Report claims 'greater investment choice' as a benefit, but existing arrangements already offer substantial fund choice: People's Pension [P4] offers 3 investment profiles with up to 50 funds; Zurich [P17] allows up to 30 funds; Scottish Widows has multiple fund options; Standard Life offers fund choice. The marginal incremental benefit is not quantified or evidenced as material.

      - Report states lifestyling benefit that 'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement' but Test Case research [P5] shows only some existing arrangements use lifestyling (People's Pension and Scottish Widows yes, but Aviva and Zurich no), so lifestyling risk is not a universal existing problem.

      - Report claims enhanced retirement flexibility as benefit, but existing Aviva pension my future focus consolidation [P5] and Scottish Widows [P16, P22] both evidence availability of flexi-access drawdown, UFPLS, annuity options - the same retirement flexibility options the Key Features [P6] attribute to the new Aviva Pension Portfolio.

    The key issue is that the report asserts lower costs and outperformance as primary justifications for higher ongoing adviser charges (0.44% total annual charge estimated from derived figures), yet the pension switch report [P10, P14] shows the new solution produces lower projected outcomes (£2,000-£3,000 less at age 75 or 1.6-2.1% reduction) compared to the existing solution, and requires a critical yield of 6.3% to match the existing solution's maturity value. The report's stated critical yield of 4.8% contradicts the evidence. The guard 'A benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify the extra cost' applies to at least five claimed benefits: (1) lifestyling is a problem only for some existing plans, not all; (2) retirement flexibility is already available in existing Aviva and Scottish Widows arrangements; (3) adviser charging facilitation contradicts Scottish Widows exclusion [P16]; (4) greater investment choice is marginal given existing fund choice availability; (5) ongoing monitoring is asserted but no evidence supports it is unavailable in existing arrangements or that it is costed separately vs included in platform charges. The report fails to reconcile the cost justification against the demonstrated projection shortfall. The only substantial differentiation is the multi-asset fund selection (HSBC and CT Universal MAP) and active management, but fund performance data [P24] shows combined performance only, not comparative advantage over existing funds, and the critical yield requirement of 6.3% suggests the market's assessment of outperformance probability is low. The severity guidance indicates 'High: the report claims a cost saving that its own comparison table contradicts' - the report claims lower costs yet the switching analysis shows lower projected outcomes requiring substantial outperformance to justify the move.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended Aviva plan is 0.24% cheaper annually than existing plans (stated on page 11: 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans'). However, it simultaneously states on the same page that 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' when adviser fees are included. The report projects values at age 75 under mid-growth (2.95%): £115,000 for the new solution versus £127,000 for existing pensions—a decrease of £12,000 or 9.4%. The reduction in yield for the new solution is stated as 2.2% (mid growth) compared to 0.3% for existing pensions. Critical yield requirements range from 1.9% (low growth) to 7.8% (high growth) for the new solution.

    File:     The pension switch report [P19] shows: existing pension projected value at mid-growth (2.95%) of £127,000 versus new solution of £115,000—a £12,000 (9.4%) decrease. Reduction in yield is 0.3% for existing versus 2.2% for new solution. Critical yields are stated as 4.8% (mid-growth) and 7.8% (high-growth). The report [P3] states an initial adviser charge of £3,305 deducted from the new solution. The comparison acknowledges that charges reduce projected outcomes: 'Effect of switching — £12,000 or 9.4% decrease' at mid-growth rates. Page 11 quote states two contradictory assertions about cost: 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive' and separately 'the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' depending on whether adviser fees are included.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims the Aviva plan is 0.24% cheaper (page 11) but then states it is 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees included (page 11)—these cannot both be true in the same comparison

      - Projected outcomes are materially worse under the recommendation: £115,000 new vs £127,000 existing at mid-growth, a 9.4% reduction

      - Reduction in yield doubles: 0.3% for existing arrangements versus 2.2% for new solution at mid-growth

      - Critical yield requirement for new solution (4.8% at mid-growth, 7.8% at high-growth) substantially exceeds the growth rate assumption used in projections (2.95%)

      - The cost comparison figure of -0.24% (stated as 'less expensive') cannot be verified against any passages provided; nearest comparable is 0%

      - The required outperformance of 4.24% stated on page 11 appears inconsistent with the charge differences disclosed elsewhere

    The report presents a fundamental contradiction: it asserts the recommendation is cheaper by 0.24% on an ongoing basis, but acknowledges it is 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees are factored in. This is not a matter of different perspectives—both cannot be materially true simultaneously in a single comparison. The evidence [P19] corroborates that projected outcomes are substantially lower under the recommendation (£12,000 or 9.4% less at mid-growth), driven by materially higher charges (2.2% RIY vs 0.3% for existing). The critical yields required (4.8%–7.8%) substantially exceed the mid-growth assumption of 2.95% used for projections, meaning the new solution would need to substantially outperform the assumed growth rate just to match existing outcomes. This represents higher charges producing lower projected outcomes without adequate explanation of offsetting benefits. The report does not explain why a client should accept a £12,000 reduction in projected retirement value, higher annual charges, and a requirement for substantially higher investment returns to break even. The guard permitting required outperformance 'under about 0.5% a year' as noise does not apply here: 4.24% outperformance is economically material. The internal inconsistency on whether costs are 0.24% lower or 4.24% higher, combined with demonstrably lower projections and higher yield requirements, represents a material failure to disclose the true cost-benefit trade-off to the client.
