CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report states 'Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans' but calculations from passages show existing Aviva 0.50% charge vs new solution 0.44% charge, a 0.06% improvement not 4.24% disadvantage. Report quantifies required outperformance as £4,672 in year one at 4.24% cost differential, but evidence shows charge reduction not increase. Report states withdrawal restrictions would prevent 'flexible' access but does not cite what flexibility the existing arrangements offer or explain what specific restrictions apply. 14 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies three material disadvantages: (1) Critical Yield Requirement—the recommended plan needs to grow 4.8% per year to age 75 to match the current plan value; (2) Adviser Charge Impact—the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (3) Lifestyling Drift—funds in lifestyling arrangements will drift outside agreed risk range at retirement. The report also states limitations: withdrawal restrictions under the new plan, fund range constraints at Scottish Widows requiring cash holdings, and generic reference to product literature for other risks. Generic disadvantage stated: no guarantee of outperformance.

    File:     Pension switch report [P1-P13] provides detailed projections showing material loss scenarios. At mid-growth (2.95%), switching three pensions results in £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in maturity value (existing £127,000 vs new £115,000) [P13]. Critical yield requirement stated as 4.8% [P13] matches report. Reduction in yield shown as 0.3% for existing vs 2.2% for new solution [P13]. Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs new Aviva Platform 0.44% (0.06% improvement, not 4.24% disadvantage as stated in report) [P1, P13]. Key Features [P14-P15] disclose general pension risks: value goes down as well as up, could get back less invested, withdrawal restrictions, income drawdown risks, property liquidity issues, currency risks. Terms and conditions [P16, P18-P21] detail switching procedures, cash account risks, disinvestment costs (greater of £10 or 10%). Product literature references income drawdown specific risks but no lifestyling drift discussed. Fact Find [P11, P24] shows client is tenant with £6,000 savings.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states 'Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans' but calculations from passages show existing Aviva 0.50% charge vs new solution 0.44% charge, a 0.06% improvement not 4.24% disadvantage

      - Report quantifies required outperformance as £4,672 in year one at 4.24% cost differential, but evidence shows charge reduction not increase

      - Report states withdrawal restrictions would prevent 'flexible' access but does not cite what flexibility the existing arrangements offer or explain what specific restrictions apply

      - Report references lifestyling drift moving portfolio 'outside agreed risk range' but evidence does not explain what the agreed range is or how drift breaches it

      - Report states 'no guarantee of return on new investment greater than existing arrangement' (stated three times) but does not contextualize this against the 9.4% projected decrease shown in pension switch report

    The report's critical yield assertion of 4.8% is corroborated by the pension switch report [P13] which states exactly this figure. However, there is a material and unexplained contradiction on adviser charge impact. The report asserts the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive, with £4,672 outperformance required in year one, but the evidence shows Aviva existing charges at 0.50% and the new solution at 0.44%, representing a 0.06% saving. The £4,672 figure does not reconcile with available values. The pension switch report clearly shows at mid-growth rate a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in value (£127,000 to £115,000) [P13]. This loss scenario is material and is quantified in evidence but the report does not present it with prominence—the 4.8% critical yield is stated but the underlying adverse outcome (9.4% projected decrease) is not highlighted as a disadvantage, only as technical calculation detail. Withdrawal restrictions are mentioned but not explained with reference to what the client is losing. The lifestyling drift concern is stated without supporting evidence from product literature—no passage from the file mentions automatic de-risking or drift outside risk ranges. Generic product risks are disclosed in Key Features [P14-P15] covering value volatility, investment loss, currency risk, and property liquidity, and Terms and Conditions [P16-P21] covering switching costs and disinvestment charges, but these appear only in appendix literature, not collected in the report's own statement of disadvantages. The vulnerability overlay: client is recorded as tenant with modest savings (£6,000) [P11, P24] and income noted as 'typically' £300/week [P24] suggesting variability. No evidence of support needs or communication requirements identified. The tenancy and income profile may bear on the materiality of withdrawal restrictions and income drawdown risks, but no analysis in the report links the client circumstances to these limitations.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report states product-specific risks including: (1) AccessDelay: 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8); (2) MarketTimingRisk: detailed explanation of stock market movements between realisation and purchase of funds (page 8); (3) PerformanceVariability: 'Your recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds. It's important to note that your Zurich investments are allocated to higher-risk funds, which are more susceptible to market volatility, resulting in greater fluctuations in value' (page 13); (4) NoPerformanceGuarantee: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (page 12); (5) InvestmentRisk: 'Unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise and there is no guarantee you will receive a return of your original capital, especially in the early years or if you choose to take an income from it' (page 12). The report also states risks are repeated across multiple sections including within recommendation detail.

    File:     The evidence pack contains KIIDs for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP funds, Fund Factsheets, Key Features documents for Aviva Pension Portfolio, and pension switch research reports. These documents disclose: HSBC KIID [P1][P3] identifies the fund as Risk Category 4 with 'medium fluctuations historically', material risks including 'Counterparty Risk', 'Credit Risk', 'Default Risk', 'Derivatives Risk', currency risk, liquidity risk, and recommends it 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' [P9]. CT Universal MAP KIID [P14] discloses 'Derivative Risk: derivative values rise and fall at a greater rate than equities and debt instruments. Losses can be greater than the initial investment', Fixed Interest Securities Risk, Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, Liquidity Risk, Currency Risk, Leverage Risk. Aviva Key Features [P6][P12] discloses income drawdown risks, tax consequences of withdrawal, currency exposure risks, property investment risks, and states 'The projections within the illustration aren't guaranteed. The actual performance will depend on the investment performance of the underlying investments and any applicable charges' [P12]. Fund Factsheets [P4][P16] state 'The value of investments and any income from them can go down as well as up and investors may not get back the amount originally invested' and note liquidity risks.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's PerformanceVariability statement at page 13 claims recommended Aviva funds 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' but the suitability report does not cite specific fund performance data from the KIID or research documentation to support this comparison. The research report [P20] shows HSBC and CT Universal MAP performance but does not appear to benchmark against the specific Zurich funds the client holds.

      - The report does not specifically disclose the Derivatives Risk as stated in the CT Universal MAP KIID [P14]: 'derivative values rise and fall at a greater rate than equities and debt instruments. Losses can be greater than the initial investment.' This is a material specific risk of one of the two recommended funds.

      - The report does not disclose Emerging Markets Risk for CT Universal MAP as listed in [P14], nor does it address the specific emerging markets exposure stated there.

      - The report does not prominently disclose the 5-year minimum investment horizon recommendation from the HSBC KIID [P9] which states the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years', despite the client being age 55 and retirement being discussed.

      - The report states standard generic capital-at-risk language ('Unit prices and the value of your pension fund can fall as well as rise') but does not map this to the specific asset allocation risks (Equities 45-70%, Bonds 20-55%) disclosed in HSBC KIID [P3] or the risk category 4 rating, making the warning generic rather than product-specific.

      - The CT Universal MAP risk disclosures in the product literature [P14] include 'Smaller Companies Risk: smaller companies carry a higher degree of risk and their value can be more sensitive to market movement' and 'Emerging Markets Risk: this Fund invests in emerging markets which are less developed and have additional legal and political risks', which are absent from the suitability report's product-specific risks section.

      - The report does not disclose currency risk specifically despite both recommended funds holding overseas investments. HSBC KIID [P3] and Aviva Key Features [P12] both disclose currency risk, but the suitability report does not address this for the client's recommended portfolio.

    The suitability report does disclose some product-specific and investment risks, particularly market timing risk, access delays, and general capital-at-risk warnings. However, a systematic comparison reveals material gaps. The report cites generic disclaimers but does not translate the specific risk factors disclosed in the KIID and Key Features documents into client-facing product-specific explanations. The PerformanceVariability statement comparing Aviva and Zurich performance is not supported by cited evidence from the research documents provided. Most significantly, the CT Universal MAP KIID discloses Derivatives Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, and Emerging Markets Risk which are entirely absent from the suitability report's risk sections, despite these being material product-specific risks. The 5-year investment horizon recommendation for HSBC is not addressed despite being a potentially relevant constraint for a client age 55. The currency risk is disclosed in multiple pieces of product literature but not addressed in the report. The report appears to rely on generic risk boilerplate rather than systematically mapping disclosed product risks into the suitability narrative. The repetition of the same risks across multiple recommendations suggests templated text rather than client-specific product risk articulation. While some risks are present, the omission of material product-specific risks identified in the product literature (Derivatives, Emerging Markets, Smaller Companies for CT Universal MAP; 5-year horizon for HSBC; currency exposure) represents an incomplete and inconsistent risk disclosure relative to the supporting evidence.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states five tax-related consequences: (1) A general caveat that tax treatment statements are based on current tax law and HMRC practice, subject to change (page 16). (2) The client understands pension freedoms and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown flexibility (page 9). (3) Death benefits taxation: tax-free if death before age 75; taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate if death after 75 (page 9). (4) Tax relief on personal contributions: net £80 becomes £100 gross (page 9). (5) Small pots rule applied to Scottish Widows £211.32 pension: 25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 (pages 6 and 9).

    File:     The evidence supports all five tax consequence statements. P2 and P4 (Key Features) confirm the death benefits taxation structure matches the report's statement. P2 states that up to 25% can be withdrawn tax-free and income tax applies to benefits taken above HMRC allowances. P4 details: death before 75 results in tax-free benefits to beneficiaries; death after 75 results in taxation at beneficiary's marginal rate. P2 and P6 (Terms and Conditions) confirm income and capital gains tax treatment within pensions. P17 (Suitability Report) discusses flexi-access drawdown, confirming the client's flexibility preference. P6 and P7 confirm tax relief principles and charge administration. P11 (Suitability Report) repeats the small pots calculation: £200 withdrawal with 25% tax-free (£50) and 75% taxed (£150), with estimated net £170 receipt. The calculations and principles are consistent across documents.

    All material tax consequences stated in the report are corroborated by the evidence. The report's five tax assertions are: (1) the general caveat is a standard disclosure consistent with P6 Terms and Conditions section 20 which states 'This information on taxation is for Your information only...based on Our understanding of current legislation and is subject to change'. (2) Flexi Access Drawdown is confirmed in P2, P4, P15 and P17 as the chosen withdrawal method with tax flexibility. (3) Death benefits taxation is explicitly stated in P4 Key Features matching the report exactly: tax-free before 75, marginal rate after 75. (4) Tax relief on contributions is standard pension tax treatment confirmed in P2 and P6. (5) The small pots calculation is worked through in P11 (the report itself) and the math is accurate: £211.32 × 25% = £52.83 tax-free; £211.32 × 75% = £158.49 taxable. The report states 'We estimate you would receive £170 net.' This calculation assumes taxation at the client's marginal rate. The client's tax position (income approximately £300 per week net from employment plus £20 from volunteering, plus state pension £230 weekly, plus spouse's £1,100 monthly) is documented in P5, placing the client likely in basic rate tax band, making the £170 estimate reasonable. The report presents tax consequences clearly in context—the small pots example in the 'My Recommendation' section (page 6) is immediately accessible, not buried. Death benefits and flexi-access information appear in the 'Recommended Solution' section (page 9). No material tax consequence identified in the evidence is absent from the report. No contradiction between report and evidence exists.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that it has a dedicated section titled 'Other Considerations of Switching' on page 8 for prominently presenting disadvantages and risks, and that this section does not appear only in an appendix. The report states material disadvantages including: loss scenarios (£10,400–£15,000 reductions at different growth rates), charge impacts (critical yield requirements of 1.9%–7.8%), and specific risks such as market volatility, no performance guarantees, stock market movements during pending transactions, and the risk that funds may go up or down. Tax consequences are addressed: the report explains tax treatment of pension benefits (tax-free at age 75 or younger; taxable at marginal rate if taken after 75), mentions tax relief on contributions, and notes that from April 2027 most pensions will be included in taxable estate for IHT purposes. The report presents at least three loss scenarios in tabular form showing projected values at low (0.00%), mid (2.95%), and high (5.88%) growth rates with corresponding reductions in yield and critical yield metrics.

    File:     Evidence confirms a section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' appears on page 8 [P6]. The section is not relegated to an appendix; it appears in the main body of the report. Material disadvantages are stated: market movements risk during pending transactions [P6], no guarantee of outperformance [P5], volatility and performance data comparisons [P3, P5, P17], and charge impacts clearly quantified [P4]. Loss scenarios are presented in tabular form [P1] showing projected values at three growth rates (0.00%, 2.95%, 5.88%) with reductions in yield (0.3%–2.3%), critical yields (1.9%–7.8%), and effect of switching (£10,400–£15,000 or 9.4%–10.0% decrease). Tax treatment is explained: state pension implications [P7, P11], pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown [P13], tax position on death before and after age 75 [P13], and IHT treatment from April 2027 [P13]. Benefits being foregone are noted: lifestyling funds automatically reducing risk [P6]; enhanced retirement flexibility [P6, P22]; and adviser charging facilitation [P6, P19]. Performance comparisons show recommended funds have not outperformed existing Zurich holdings and have higher volatility in some comparisons [P17]. Cashflow stress tests are documented showing resilience to high inflation (6%) and immediate 30% market crash [P9]. The report explicitly states 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' [P5]. Charges are detailed in tabular form [P4, P20, P21] with ongoing annual adviser charge of 1% explained [P8, P24].

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims lower costs ('Lower Costs — The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' [P6, P22]) but the evidence shows the Standard Life arrangement comparison states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P6], creating internal contradiction about whether costs increase or decrease for this particular switch.

      - The report states on page 6 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Aviva [P6] but the charge comparison table [P4] shows Aviva existing at 0.50% and new Aviva Platform at 0.44%, implying the new plan is 0.06% cheaper, not more expensive. The text contradicts the numerical evidence.

      - Charge arithmetic inconsistency: The critical yield analysis states the new solution must grow at 4.8% per year to match the existing plan value [P2], but computed fund values from charge percentages do not align with any stated existing arrangement total, casting doubt on whether the comparison basis is sound.

    The report satisfies the core requirement of [G8.5] that material disadvantages, risks and loss scenarios are fairly and prominently presented. Evidence confirms a dedicated 'Other Considerations of Switching' section exists on page 8 (not relegated to appendix), meeting the prominence requirement. All three loss scenarios at different growth rates are presented in tabular form with quantified reductions (£10,400–£15,000) and critical yield thresholds (1.9%–7.8%), satisfying the requirement for illustrated loss scenarios. Tax consequences are explicitly addressed across multiple locations: pension tax treatment on withdrawal, state pension interactions, IHT changes from April 2027, and tax relief on contributions. Material disadvantages are stated: no guarantee of outperformance, market volatility risk during transfer, funds going up or down, and performance data showing recommended funds have not outperformed Zurich holdings. Features foregone are disclosed: lifestyling benefits, flexible retirement access comparisons, and adviser charging mechanics. The discrepancies identified relate to internal consistency within the report rather than to absence of material content from the report. The claim of 'lower costs' contradicts specific charge comparisons in some instances (Standard Life shows 0.08% increase, not decrease; Aviva text states 0.06% increase while table shows 0.06% decrease). These contradictions are material to the suitability conclusion but do not undermine the presence and prominence of disadvantage and risk disclosure itself. The guard 'A dedicated risk section is not mandatory; disadvantages distributed but clearly stated can still be prominent' does not apply here because a dedicated section does exist. The guard 'A material disadvantage quantified only in an appendix, while the body leads with a benefit, is a prominence finding' does not apply because loss scenarios appear in the main body on page 8 and are not confined to the appendix. The charge arithmetic discrepancies suggest underlying calculation errors in fund values but do not demonstrate absence of risk disclosure. Per the vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability drivers are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this requirement. The client's status as an HGV driver with variable income ('typically earning around £300 per week' [P10, P7]) introduces potential income volatility not fully modelled in the deterministic cashflow, but this is a limitation of the underlying analysis rather than a disclosure failure.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report identifies one feature being given up: employer contributions. Page 6 states 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The report also notes that all five existing arrangements have no safeguarded benefits (stated on page 5 for each arrangement). The existing arrangements offer various retirement options including Flexi Access Drawdown, UFPLS, and Partial UFPLS.

    File:     The evidence from product literature confirms: (1) People's Pension [P3, P11, P17] mentions employer contributions under auto-enrolment minimum contribution rules (3% employer contribution requirement), tax relief on contributions, and the ability to combine pensions with no transfer charge; (2) All five plans are confirmed to have no Guaranteed Annuity Rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, no guaranteed fund at retirement, and no safeguarded benefits [P1, P2, P8, P13, P14]; (3) Retirement access options vary by plan: People's Pension [P3, P11] offers flexible drawdown, tax-free lump sum (25%), and annuity purchase from other providers; Scottish Widows [P2, P4] offers tax-free cash lump sum (25%) and pension income; Aviva [P8, P10, P19] permits drawdown and annuity; Standard Life [P13] facilitates drawdown; Zurich [P14] permits transfers and requires supplementary declaration for guaranteed annuities or mortality basis if present. (4) No life cover is mentioned in the policy schedules [P23]. (5) People's Pension [P3, P11, P17] provides no annuity option (client must purchase from another provider).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report identifies employer contribution loss but does not specify which arrangement(s) offer employer contributions. Evidence shows People's Pension has auto-enrolment 3% employer contribution, but unclear if this is the only plan or if others also receive contributions.

      - Report is silent on loss of the People's Pension's 'no charge for transfer' feature [P3, P11], which is a material benefit being given up if transferring from People's Pension.

      - Report does not mention that People's Pension does not offer an annuity option [P11], meaning client loses access to guaranteed income purchasing through that provider if transferred.

      - Report states all arrangements have no safeguarded benefits, which is consistent with evidence, but does not explicitly state that retirement options differ across plans and some options are being consolidated or restricted.

    The requirement asks whether the report fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently explains all material disadvantages, risks, and benefits/features the client is giving up. The report identifies employer contribution loss as a disadvantage, which is corroborated by evidence that People's Pension has auto-enrolment contributions (minimum 3% employer contribution). However, the report omits several material features being given up: (1) the People's Pension's fee-free transfer feature [P11] is not mentioned; (2) the fact that People's Pension does not offer direct annuity purchasing at retirement [P11] means this option is lost, but this is not disclosed; (3) the report does not explain that retirement access options differ between plans being consolidated. The extraction report notes that `/replacementAnalysis/0/featuresOrGuaranteesLost` contains only one entry when multiple features exist in the evidence. The safeguarded benefits absence is consistently stated across all five plans in both report and evidence, so no inconsistency there. The guards specify that benefits the client cannot access at current age are still benefits being given up, and that generic product literature benefits must be checked against the actual policy schedule. The People's Pension features (employer contributions, no transfer fees, limited annuity options) are specific to that plan and material to the switch decision. The report's failure to disclose these represents silence on material disadvantages present in the file.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four justifications for the recommendation: (1) 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' in relation to a critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75; (2) 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings'; (3) 'the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility'; (4) Ongoing review services including risk assessment, suitability review, market updates, and access to advisers. The report also states 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (stated three times). The report indicates the recommended solution is 'RecommendedCheaper' in terms of costs.

    File:     The pension switch report [P1] shows projected values at age 75 under mid-growth (2.95%): existing pension £127,000 vs new solution £125,000, a reduction of £2,000 or 1.6%. Under high-growth (5.88%): existing £150,000 vs new £147,000, a reduction of £3,000 or 2.0%. The critical yield required for the new solution ranges from 0.3% (low growth) to 6.2% (high growth) [P1]. The report's own research [P17] shows HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 5YR: 40.06% and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund 5YR: 44.73%, with both showing mixed recent performance (2024: HSBC 10.82%, CT 11.27%) [P22]. Product literature [P24] shows the Aviva charge is 0.21%, investment charges weighted average 0.23%, totalling 0.44% platform + investment charges. Historical performance data [P17] does not demonstrate the Aviva platform funds as clearly outperforming the existing Aviva holdings across all periods. The pension switch report [P19] shows the new solution produces materially reduced projected values: low growth £97,600 vs £108,000 (9.6% decrease), mid growth £115,000 vs £127,000 (9.4% decrease), high growth £135,000 vs £150,000 (10.0% decrease).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past performance, but [P1] shows critical yield requirement of 4.8% at mid-growth, yet the new solution projects £115,000 vs existing £127,000—a reduction not an outperformance

      - Report states recommended funds 'have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings,' but [P17] and [P22] show CT Universal MAP 5YR 44.73% vs HSBC 40.06%, and neither clearly outperforms the existing Aviva pension across all time periods shown

      - Report projects mid-growth value of £115,000 at age 75 [extracted as per canonical model], but [P1] detailed pension switch report shows the same scenario produces £125,000 for existing pension vs £115,000 for new solution, indicating reduced outcome not justified by performance claims

      - Report indicates solution is 'RecommendedCheaper' but shows projected values lower across all three growth scenarios than existing arrangements [P1] [P19], which would typically require stronger performance justification, not merely historical data

      - Report offers no explanation why client should accept 9.4% to 10.0% reduction in projected retirement values under mid and high growth scenarios [P19], only stating funds 'have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility' without quantifying the volatility reduction or trade-off

    The report justifies the recommendation primarily by reference to past performance of the recommended funds and alignment with risk tolerance, but the evidence reveals a material discrepancy: the actual projected outcomes show substantial reductions in retirement fund value compared to retaining existing arrangements. Under mid-growth, the new solution projects £115,000 versus the existing pension's £127,000—a £12,000 or 9.4% reduction [P19]. The report's justification ('past performance,' 'stronger performance,' 'comparable performance with reduced volatility') does not adequately explain why this reduced projected outcome is acceptable. The critical yield of 4.8% required under mid-growth assumptions [P1] is presented as 'achievable' based on past performance, but the evidence does not demonstrate the recommended funds will reliably outperform by this margin relative to what the client would receive by retaining existing arrangements. The report includes a disclaimer that 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' but this is a generic statement that does not constitute justification—it merely acknowledges the risk. The guard 'Where costs fall and projections improve, the absence of a justification is not a finding' does not apply here, as costs do not fall (0.44% platform+investment vs 0.3-0.6% existing per [P1]) and projections deteriorate materially. The report's assertion that ongoing services (review, updates, adviser access) justify the recommendation is weak on the facts: these services are generic, offered across the market, and do not explain away a 9-10% projected value reduction. The false-positive guard regarding cost justification does not rescue this finding: the report makes claims about performance that are contradicted by the projections it produces.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report discloses three material disadvantages: (1) a critical yield requirement of 4.8% per year to match existing plan value (stated on page 11); (2) adviser charge impact of 4.24% extra cost, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one (page 11); (3) lifestyling drift causing portfolio to move outside agreed risk range (page 7). The report also discloses three limitations: (1) withdrawal restrictions and inflexibility of the recommended plan (page 6); (2) fund range constraint at Scottish Widows requiring 20-30% cash holdings (page 6); (3) generic statement that all other risks are in product literature (page 8). Product-specific risks disclosed include: access delay of 10-15 working days (page 8) and market timing risk during fund switches (page 8).

    File:     The evidence contains material risks and limitations not mentioned in the report: (1) From P3 Key Features and P6-P7 KIIDs, counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, and liquidity risk are identified as material risks not fully captured by standard risk indicators. (2) From P3, income drawdown risks include impact on means-tested benefits, depletion of pension fund if income exceeds growth, and reduced provision for dependants or future annuity purchase. (3) From P3, tax position variability and potential overpayment of tax requiring tax returns. (4) From P6-P7 KIID, fund not appropriate for investors planning withdrawal within 5 years (twice stated). (5) From P8 Key Features, client must retain financial adviser services and restrictions apply if adviser relationship ends. (6) From P13 Zurich Policy Info, specific risks including property fund valuation delays up to 12 months, high yield bond risks, emerging market and concentrated portfolio risks, and political/economic risks in less developed markets. (7) From P11 Fund Factsheet, currency risk, bond interest rate risk, and liquidity risk affecting fund holdings. (8) From P8 Key Features, multiple retirement options with different tax consequences (lump sum, drawdown, annuity combinations) not explained in report.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report does not disclose counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, or derivatives risk identified in KIIDs (P6, P14, P16, P18)

      - Report does not disclose liquidity risk identified in multiple fund documents (P3, P6, P11, P14)

      - Report does not disclose income drawdown risks including impact on means-tested benefits, depletion risk if income exceeds growth, or reduced provision for dependants (P3)

      - Report does not disclose tax position variability risks and potential tax overpayment scenarios (P3)

      - Report does not disclose that recommended funds may not be appropriate for investors planning withdrawal within 5 years (P6, P7)

      - Report does not disclose requirement to retain financial adviser and restrictions if adviser relationship ends (P8)

      - Report does not explain specific tax consequences of different retirement income options (lump sum, drawdown combinations, annuity purchase) available in the Aviva product (P8)

      - Report does not disclose currency risk from overseas investments (P11)

      - Report does not disclose bond interest rate risk (P11)

      - Report does not disclose property fund specific risks including valuation delays up to 12 months (P13)

      - Report does not disclose high yield bond fund risks (P13)

      - Report does not disclose emerging market and concentrated portfolio risks (P13)

      - Report does not disclose political and economic risks in less developed markets (P13)

      - Report does not disclose operational risk affecting transactions and valuation (P14)

    The report discloses certain material disadvantages and risks specific to this client's recommendation, including the critical yield requirement, adviser charge impact, and lifestyling drift. However, comparison against the evidence reveals substantial gaps. The evidence contains material risks present in the product literature (KIIDs, Key Features, Fund Factsheets, Zurich Policy Info) that are neither product-specific boilerplate nor covered by the report's generic statement that 'all other risks are detailed in product literature.' Material omissions include: counterparty, credit, default and derivatives risks from fund KIIDs; liquidity risk across multiple documents; specific income drawdown risks particular to pension income phase; tax position variability; unsuitability of recommended funds for investors with 5-year horizons; adviser retention requirements; and specific tax consequences of retirement options. The 5-year unsuitability warning is particularly material given the client's retirement age is 75 and current age approximately 69-70 (5-year horizon). The report's reliance on the blanket statement that risks are in product literature does not constitute fair, clear and prominent disclosure of material risks identified in the file. Several risks are product-specific to Aviva and the recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) and should have been explicitly highlighted in the suitability report itself, not delegated to appendices or product documents. The report fails to address that the recommended solution with its ongoing charges and critical yield requirement may present heightened risk given the client's short time horizon and income-dependent financial position (HGV driver, £300 per week income, 'typically' variable).

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report asserts Zurich existing charge is '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' but the extracted canonical model indicates this calculation applies to a £103,438.71 fund value per charge arithmetic, when the actual Zurich current value is £103,439.24 — minor rounding but the 0.93% figure itself is derived, not evidenced in any single provider document passage. Report compares Zurich at '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' vs recommended 0.44%, claiming '-0.49% -£506.85' benefit; however, the switching report shows mid-growth projections of Zurich existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 (1.6% decrease in maturity), contradicting the cost-saving narrative. Report states Aviva existing 0.50% vs new 0.44% with impact '-0.06% -£2.25'; this assertion appears to understate the Aviva comparison. Charge arithmetic shows 0.50% on £3,744.36 = £18.72 annually, and 0.44% on the same = £16.47, a difference of £2.25. However, the switching report shows Aviva existing projected £108,000 (low growth) vs new £97,600, a £10,400 (9.6%) decrease — contradicting the 'Better' assessment for cost savings to drive overall benefit. 16 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report presents 14 comparison rows across 5 existing arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), each assessing dimensions including Charges, FundRange, RiskAlignment, AdviserServicing, and RetirementOptions. For the Zurich arrangement (£103,439.24), the report compares existing charges of '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' against recommended 0.44%, marking this 'Better' with impact '-0.49% -£506.85'. For Aviva (£3,744.36), existing charges are stated as '0.50% fund charge' vs recommended '0.44% (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management)', marked 'Better' with '-0.06% -£2.25'. For Standard Life (£3,002), existing is '0.50% fund charge' vs recommended '0.44%', marked 'Better'. For risk alignment, the Zurich arrangement shows existing 'Risk level 7' vs recommended 'Risk level 5 - Balanced (50% HSBC + 50% CT Universal MAP)', marked 'Better'. The Aviva arrangement shows existing lifestyling fund 'Risk level 2' moving outside agreed risk range vs recommended 'Risk level 5 - Balanced', marked 'Better'. Retirement options are presented as equivalent or improved (e.g., Standard Life 'FAD' vs recommended 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS; Flexi Access Drawdown'). Fund range comparisons show improvements from 7, 60+, or 80+ existing to 80+ recommended across plans.

    File:     The Pension Switch Report dated in the evidence shows: (1) Aviva existing arrangement comparison indicates 0.3% reduction in yield for existing vs 2.1% for new solution at low growth (P12, P8), with mid-growth projections of £127,000 existing vs £115,000 new (9.4% decrease in effect of switching). (2) Zurich arrangement shows in switching analysis: low growth 0.3% RiY existing vs 0.6% new, mid-growth £127,000 existing vs £125,000 new (1.6% decrease). (3) Standard Life arrangement: low growth £2,910 existing vs £2,900 new (0.3% decrease), mid-growth £3,440 projected. (4) The switching report's 'top 10 results' ranked by maturity value (P4) show: rank 1 Zurich 0.2% RiY £128,000; rank 2 Existing Solution 0.3% £127,000; rank 5 New Solution 0.6% £125,000. (5) Comparative death benefit analysis (P6, P8) shows existing pension £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00. (6) Switching cost in switching report explicitly states £3,305.00 initial adviser charge taken from new solution. (7) Fund details in new solution (P6): 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced + 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio. (8) Charges breakdown (P18): Aviva Platform 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total, estimated £484.81 annually. (9) Withdrawal options in switching report (not explicitly detailed per plan in passages, but retirement flexibility discussed in suitability report P15, P22).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts Zurich existing charge is '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' but the extracted canonical model indicates this calculation applies to a £103,438.71 fund value per charge arithmetic, when the actual Zurich current value is £103,439.24 — minor rounding but the 0.93% figure itself is derived, not evidenced in any single provider document passage.

      - Report compares Zurich at '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' vs recommended 0.44%, claiming '-0.49% -£506.85' benefit; however, the switching report shows mid-growth projections of Zurich existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 (1.6% decrease in maturity), contradicting the cost-saving narrative.

      - Report states Aviva existing 0.50% vs new 0.44% with impact '-0.06% -£2.25'; this assertion appears to understate the Aviva comparison. Charge arithmetic shows 0.50% on £3,744.36 = £18.72 annually, and 0.44% on the same = £16.47, a difference of £2.25. However, the switching report shows Aviva existing projected £108,000 (low growth) vs new £97,600, a £10,400 (9.6%) decrease — contradicting the 'Better' assessment for cost savings to drive overall benefit.

      - Report's comparison of Standard Life shows existing 0.50% fund charge vs recommended 0.44%, but switching report (P9) shows Standard Life existing £2,910 (low growth) vs new £2,900 (0.3% decrease), which does not demonstrate clear cost advantage translating to material benefit.

      - Report states retirement options for Standard Life as existing 'FAD' vs recommended 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS; Flexi Access Drawdown' (marked 'Equivalent'), but this is an upgrade, not equivalent — the recommended has more options.

      - Report asserts fund range for People's Pension as '7 funds available' existing vs '80+ funds available' recommended; however, P20 (Suitability Report) shows People's Pension fund count as 7 and Aviva as 80+, and P15 shows recommendation to 'Retain your people's pension plan', not switch it. This comparison row appears to address an Aviva arrangement already being switched, not People's Pension.

      - The report's claim that Aviva lifestyling fund is 'Risk level 2' is not explicitly corroborated by the product documentation retrieved; P19 (People's Pension Policy Info) describes a 'glidepath' moving toward lower risk but does not assign a numeric risk level of 2 to it.

      - Switching report (P2, P16, P17) shows overall maturity values: Existing Solution £127,000 (rank 2) vs New Solution £125,000 (rank 5), yet report frames all comparisons as 'Better' without disclosing that the switching analysis shows the new solution ranking below the existing solution in the market-wide comparison.

      - Report does not disclose or analyze the £3,305.00 initial switching charge (evidenced in P6, P16) within the cost/benefit comparison dimensions; this is a material switching cost not reflected in the 'Charges' dimension comparison (which shows ongoing charges only).

      - Fund range stated for Zurich as '60+ funds available' vs '80+ recommended'; however, the underlying scheme documentation (e.g., P10, P11) does not explicitly confirm the fund count, and dates of this research data are not provided.

    The requirement demands that where a switch is recommended, the comparison of existing versus recommended product must be consistent with and supported by evidence. Discrepancies arise across multiple dimensions: (1) Cost comparisons are presented as uniformly 'Better' but the switching report's projected maturity values contradict this narrative. For Zurich, the report claims -0.49% charge advantage (£506.85 saving), but the switching analysis shows the existing pension projecting £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 at mid-growth (1.6% worse). For Aviva, the -0.06% charge advantage (£2.25 annual saving) is trivial and contradicted by a 9.6% projected maturity value decrease. For Standard Life, a 0.06% charge advantage is claimed without the corresponding maturity analysis disclosing a 0.3% projected decrease. The report's cost-benefit analysis fails to acknowledge that switching costs (£3,305.00 initial adviser charge from P6, P16) are not netted into the comparison. (2) The 'Effect of switching' metric in the switching report (P1, P12, P16, P17) is not referenced in the suitability report's comparison dimensions; the suitability report lists only ongoing charge impact, not the critical yield or maturity value effect of switching. (3) Risk alignment comparisons are stated (e.g., Risk 7 to Risk 5 for Zurich) but lack substantiation that the recommended Risk 5 funds (50% HSBC + 50% CT Universal MAP per P6) truly align better than existing arrangements. The report asserts lifestyling funds 'move outside agreed risk range' (P13, P22) but does not quantify this risk drift or show it is material. (4) The retirement options comparison claims equivalence or improvement, but for Standard Life, the shift from 'FAD' only to 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS; Flexi Access Drawdown' is an upgrade, not equivalent. (5) The vulnerability overlay is not applied: the client is aged 69, working as an HGV driver with variable income ('typically £300 per week'), receiving state pension, and expressing intent to retire within two years (P15). The switching recommendation does not address whether the larger recommended portfolio (consolidated at £110,185) remains aligned with capacity for loss, or whether the 9-10% projected maturity value decrease at mid-to-high growth affects the already-tight cash flow. (6) Alternative options analysis: the switching report shows a market ranking (P4) where the Existing Solution ranks 2nd and New Solution ranks 5th among 9 alternatives, yet no alternative to switching was considered in the suitability report's recommendation (per extraction flag). The guard requiring one-sided comparisons to be flagged applies here: the report does not disclose that existing arrangements outrank the recommended solution in an independent ranking. (7) The extraction metadata notes that internal inconsistencies include a total transfer value mismatch of £6,813.47 (individual plans sum to £116,998.47 but recommended transfer shows £110,185), creating ambiguity about which fund values underpin the cost calculations.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: (1) No exit penalties (£0); (2) Annual charge difference of -0.06% (-£2.25 annual saving) for unspecified plan; (3) Small pot withdrawal at £200 with net receipt of £170 after 25% tax-free, 75% taxed as income; (4) Multiple benefits including lower costs, lifestyling correction, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and multi-asset fund selection; (5) Charge difference of -0.49% for another plan with -£506.85 annual impact; (6) Stock market timing risk acknowledged between realisation and purchase.

    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Zurich fund switch charge £0.00 (P2); (2) Standard Life transfer charge £0.00 (P10); (3) No specific numerical validation in passages for -0.06% charge difference or -£2.25 annual amount; (4) Pension switch report (P5, P8, P21) shows multiple pensions with different charge comparisons but does not specify which plans attract which benefits; (5) Zurich-only comparison shows 0.29%-0.6% reduction in yield differential (P21); (6) Aviva comparison shows projected values lower in new solution across all growth scenarios (P5); (7) No evidence locates the £200 small pot or £170 net receipt calculation; (8) Multi-asset fund selection quote (P13) relates to Zurich lacking risk level 5 funds, requiring blend of level 7 and level 2, but this is a constraint, not a benefit.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states -0.06% annual charge difference (savings) but this figure is not corroborated in the charge comparison tables in the evidence

      - Report states -£2.25 annual charge difference but nearest matching figure in evidence is £2.32 (P3), 3.1% different, context is employer contributions not switching cost

      - Report claims £170 net receipt from £200 small pot withdrawal but no evidence locates these figures; 75% tax rate claimed but nearest comparable is 85% in evidence (P13)

      - Report lists -0.49% charge difference for another plan producing -£506.85 impact, but no evidence corroborates -0.49% or -£506.85; nearest figure is £500 in evidence (P2), 1.4% away

      - Report asserts benefits outweigh higher costs but pension switch report (P5, P8, P21) shows new solution produces lower projected values in all growth scenarios: at 2.95% growth, existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 (£12,000 or 9.4% decrease)

      - Report characterises multi-asset fund selection as a benefit but evidence (P13) states this addresses a constraint (Zurich lacks risk level 5 funds) by blending mismatched risk levels, not a solution advantage

      - Report does not identify which specific benefits attach to which specific ceding plan, making it impossible to verify that claimed benefits are plan-specific rather than generic

      - Report cites 'lower costs' as a general benefit but charge comparison data in evidence does not support net cost savings across the portfolio when aggregated

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Specific plan-by-plan mapping of which benefits apply to which existing arrangements, Validated charge comparison table showing -0.06% and -0.49% differentials with fund values they were calculated on, Evidence for £200 small pot value and £170 net receipt calculation including marginal tax rate applied, Corroboration of which alternative options were considered and rejected for each ceding plan, Analysis of whether benefits claimed justify the net projected value reductions shown in pension switch report

    The comparison cannot be reliably performed because critical quantitative assertions lack corroboration. The report states charge savings of -0.06% and -0.49% with corresponding annual amounts, but these figures do not match the charge comparison data in the pension switch report (P5, P8, P21). The small pot withdrawal figures (£200 principal, £170 net receipt, 75% tax rate) appear nowhere in the evidence. Most critically, the pension switch report (P5) directly contradicts the claim that benefits outweigh higher costs: at mid growth rate (2.95%), the existing pension projects to £127,000 while the new solution projects to £115,000, a reduction of £12,000 or 9.4%. This is not explained as acceptable in exchange for the claimed benefits. The multi-asset fund selection is presented as a benefit but is actually a workaround for Zurich's absence of risk level 5 funds (P13), achieved by blending level 7 and level 2 funds. The report does not articulate why blending risk levels is superior to the existing approach. No alternative options are documented as having been considered for each individual plan before recommending consolidation and switching. The extraction metadata notes that featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the canonical model, indicating the report does not address safeguarded benefits or guarantees that may be forfeited on transfer. The charge arithmetic computed from the report's own figures shows inconsistencies: the solution total ongoing charge of 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which matches neither any individual arrangement nor the sum of all arrangements (£116,998.47). The £268.94 annual charge difference for EA5 was computed against Zurich (£103,438.46) not against Standard Life (£3,002), meaning the comparison row is computed on the wrong plan. Without reliable plan-specific charge and benefit analysis, and with projected maturity values showing a net loss, the claim that benefits justify the switch cannot be verified.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts one feature lost: 'Employer contribution will be lost' for one of five existing arrangements (people's Pension, flagged as isClientSpecific=true). The report states on page 6: 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' All five existing arrangements are declared to contain no safeguarded benefits: 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' (page 5, repeated five times).

    File:     Provider documentation confirms: People's Pension (P4, P13) accepts transfers and offers no annuity guarantees but does not document employer contribution status. Scottish Widows (P1, P5, P18, P23) confirms 'Guaranteed Minimum Pension: No', 'Guaranteed Annuity Rates: No', no life cover, no waiver of premium, no fund rebates, and member has left the company with no current contributions. Aviva (P9) shows £0 pension input for tax years 2023/24, 2024/25, 2025/26; no guaranteed annuity rates or guaranteed minimum pension stated. Zurich (P6) provides transfer documentation but no specific guarantee disclosures in provided extracts. Standard Life (P3, P21) states 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' Scottish Widows workplace master trust (P23) explicitly states member 'has left company' with 'no current regular contributions being made'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims employer contributions will be lost on the People's Pension if transferred away. Evidence (P4, P13) does not confirm current employer contributions are being paid into this plan or will be lost upon transfer. The People's Pension is described as a workplace savings scheme but current contribution status is not documented in the evidence. P17 (report table) shows 'You pay: £1,193.33 PA Employer pays: £897.13 PA' for People's Pension, confirming employer contributions exist. However, no provider document corroborates that these contributions will cease or be lost if the plan is transferred. The Scottish Widows plan (P23) explicitly states the member 'has left company' with no current contributions, contradicting any employer contribution risk for that arrangement.

      - Report states all five arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits. Standard Life (P3) provides explicit provider confirmation of this. Aviva (P9) confirms no guaranteed annuity rates or pension. Scottish Widows (P1, P18) confirms no guarantees. However, Zurich documentation provided does not include a definitive provider statement confirming absence of safeguarded benefits; the evidence is silent on whether Zurich Managed Pension AP contains guarantees or not.

    The report identifies employer contribution loss as a material feature being given up on the People's Pension. The suitability report's own table (P17) shows employer contributions of £897.13 PA are currently being paid. The concern is that no independent provider documentation confirms: (1) that these contributions will be lost on transfer, or (2) the terms under which they would cease. Critically, the Scottish Widows plan (P23) shows the member 'has left company' — there is a material inconsistency here: if the member has left their employer, they would not be receiving employer contributions on any workplace arrangement, including People's Pension. The report's claim that employer contributions 'will be lost' if transferred suggests they are currently active and material; the evidence file contains no provider letter, scheme documentation, or member correspondence confirming the current status of employer contributions to the People's Pension or the contractual or practical mechanism by which they would be forfeited on transfer. For the other four arrangements, safeguarded benefits absence is well corroborated by provider policy documents (Standard Life P3, Aviva P9, Scottish Widows P1/P18), but no equivalent provider confirmation exists in the case file for Zurich (P6 extracts provided concern transfer procedures only). The report's treatment of lost features is incomplete: it mentions employer contributions but does not address whether this is actually a 'safeguarded benefit' in regulatory terms, and it does not corroborate the claim with provider evidence that contributions will cease. The comparison cannot fully proceed without clarification of Zurich's safeguarded status and independent confirmation of People's Pension employer contribution cessation terms.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts zero exit penalties on all five existing arrangements ('No charges for transfer and/or switching' [P5]). For the Aviva arrangement, the report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' implying the new solution saves 0.06% annually. For the Zurich arrangement, the report states the same. The report claims annual charge differences of -£2.25 for Aviva and -£506.85 for Zurich (negative values indicating savings). For the Scottish Widows small pot (£211.32), the report recommends withdrawal under small pots rules with 'estimated net receipt £170' after tax treatment. The report cites multiple benefits: lower costs, lifestyling misalignment, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and multi-asset fund selection. A critical yield of 1.9% is stated for Zurich on page 18, and 4.8% on page 11 for the overall comparison.

    File:     The pension switch report [P1]-[P7] shows comparative analysis across multiple growth scenarios. For Zurich alone [P1], the Reduction in Yield increases from 0.3% (existing) to 0.6% (new solution) at low growth, and to 2.3% at high growth rate (5.88%). The Effect of Switching is shown as negative: £2,000 (2.0% decrease) at low growth, £12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid growth (2.95%), and £15,000 (10.0% decrease) at high growth. The critical yield required is 1.9% at low growth, 4.8% at mid growth, and 7.8% at high growth. [P15] shows the new solution ranked 5th by maturity value (£125,000 at mid growth) compared to existing solution ranked 2nd (£127,000). The immediate impact on fund value [P7] shows a shortfall of £3,305.00 described as an initial adviser charge. [P2] shows new solution day one value of £106,880.60 versus existing pension £110,185.60, a £3,305 reduction. [P24] confirms the Aviva Platform charge is 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management) versus 0.50% existing Aviva. For Zurich, no explicit new charge percentage is disclosed in the passages for direct comparison to the stated 0.29% or other figures.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states annual charge difference for Zurich is -0.06% (savings) but pension switch report [P1] and [P7] show Reduction in Yield increasing from 0.3% to 0.6%-2.3% depending on growth scenario, indicating higher charges in new solution, not lower

      - Report states charge impact of -£506.85 for Zurich but this figure is not corroborated in the retrieved passages; the passages show 'Effect of switching' as £2,000-£15,000 negative (losses), not charge differentials

      - Report claims 'no charges for transfer and/or switching' but [P7] and [P2] show an immediate £3,305 shortfall on fund value described as initial adviser charge, representing a direct switching cost of 3.0% of the Zurich fund value (£3,305 ÷ £110,185.60)

      - Report states annual charge difference for Aviva is -£2.25 (savings) but this is stated as -0.06% of fund value; derived from £110,185 fund value this would equal £66.11, not £2.25, creating internal inconsistency in the report's own figures

      - Critical yield of 4.8% stated on page 11 matches the mid-growth scenario [P11] for the Zurich-only comparison, yet the report does not clearly communicate that this rate must be achieved simply to match (not exceed) existing pension maturity value

      - Report recommends withdrawal of Scottish Widows small pot at estimated net receipt of £170 from £200 fund (15% loss) but this is presented as a benefit ('tax-efficient treatment') without disclosing the absolute loss or comparing to retention as transferred pension

      - Pension switch report [P15] ranks the new solution 5th in market comparison at £125,000 maturity value (mid growth) against the existing solution at rank 2nd (£127,000), contradicting the narrative that switching provides superior outcomes

      - Report states benefits include 'Lower Costs' and 'receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure' but [P7] shows Reduction in Yield of 2.2% for new solution versus 0.3% for existing at mid growth, indicating the new solution is materially more expensive in charge terms

    The requirement tests whether switching costs do not exceed benefits. The report asserts minimal switching costs (zero exit penalties, stated charge savings of -0.06% and -£2.25/-£506.85 annually) and substantial benefits (lower costs, better alignment, greater choice, ongoing servicing). However, critical inconsistencies emerge. First, the report claims charge savings but the pension switch report shows materially higher Reduction in Yield in the new solution (0.6%-2.3% vs 0.3% existing for Zurich), directly contradicting the cost advantage narrative. Second, the report omits the £3,305 initial adviser charge from the switching costs section, yet [P7] and [P2] show this as an immediate 3% reduction in fund value—a material switching cost entirely absent from the 'costsOfSwitching' analysis. Third, the Effect of Switching shown in [P1], [P7] and elsewhere demonstrates negative outcomes across all growth scenarios (£2,000-£15,000 losses for Zurich), yet the report positions switching as beneficial. Fourth, the critical yield of 4.8% required to match (not beat) the existing Zurich pension at mid-growth rates represents a high hurdle; the report does not address whether this is achievable or realistic. Fifth, the small pot withdrawal is presented as a benefit but represents a 15% immediate loss (£30 from £200) plus tax on remaining amount, without this being disclosed as a cost. The pension switch report [P15] ranking the new solution 5th in a market comparison, below the existing solution, undermines the suitability narrative. The guard permits non-financial benefits to justify cost increases if argued explicitly; the report does cite investment choice, monitoring, and adviser facilitation, but these are asserted without quantification and in direct tension with the claim of lower costs. The report's own figures on Reduction in Yield (0.6% new vs 0.3% existing for Zurich at low growth) contradict its stated charge comparison. The internal inconsistency between -0.06% as a percentage and -£2.25 as an amount (which would derive from £3,750, not the stated fund value) compounds the reliability concern.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that all five existing arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' with no penalties on exit. The canonical model shows `/existingArrangements[]/exitPenalties` populated uniformly across all five arrangements with `hasPenalty: false`. However, the extraction notes that `/existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp` is absent from the extracted model. The report does not explicitly address what safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features exist on the existing arrangements or what is being surrendered by switching.

    File:     Evidence from provider documents confirms the following regarding safeguarded benefits and guarantees: Scottish Widows (P8) states 'I can also confirm that there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' Scottish Widows Policy Info (P14) lists 'Guaranteed Minimum Pension: No', 'Guaranteed Minimum Fund: No', 'Reference Scheme Test: No', 'Waiver of premium: No', 'Life cover: No'. Standard Life Policy Info (P11, P12) shows transfer value of £3,002.02 with transfer charge of £0.00. Zurich Policy Info (P15, P16) states 'This plan does not have life cover' and confirms no former protected rights element exists as a separate item; it notes 'If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred' but then specifies the charge 'reduces by around 0.3%-0.4% a month until SRA where there is no charge. The charge is capped at 1% from age 55.' The suitability report's client is age 68 (DoB 7 June 1956, report date October 2025), placing him above age 55. Zurich Policy Info (P17) confirms 'No payments have been received in the last ten years' for the Personal Pension Plan. The Pension switch report (P20) states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' under the heading 'Benefits: The following is a list of benefits that will be lost when switching'. The test case policy research (P7, P23) confirms across all five arrangements: no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates, no Market Value Reduction, and no enhanced transfer values.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' uniformly for all five arrangements, but Zurich Policy Info indicates an exit charge applies if the fund is transferred, albeit one that reduces to zero by age 55. Client is age 68, so this charge may be zero, but the assertion of 'no charges' is stated without this age-specific caveat.

      - Report's canonical model contains no field `/existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp`, meaning the report does not record or analyse what safeguarded benefits are or are not being surrendered.

      - Report does not explicitly state whether safeguarded benefits exist on any arrangement or address the implications of their loss, despite COBS requirements to disclose this.

    The evidence demonstrates that no material safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features are being lost on any of the five existing arrangements. Scottish Widows explicitly confirms no guarantees, discounts or bonuses are attached. Standard Life shows no transfer charge. Zurich's exit charge, while theoretically applicable, is capped at 1% from age 55 and the client is 68, so the charge would be zero by contract terms. The Pension switch report (P20) explicitly states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' when listing benefits that would be lost on switching. The test case research documents (P7, P23) confirm uniformly across all five arrangements the absence of GARs, GMPs, guaranteed growth rates, MVRs, and other protected features. While the report's extraction model does not contain the field `/existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp`, this is not a material omission because the supporting evidence establishes that no such benefits exist to be given up. The report's assertion of 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' is substantiated by the evidence for the client's age, even though the Zurich documentation's reference to exit charges (albeit zero-rated above age 55) was not explicitly qualified in the report. No vulnerability factors are recorded in the case file that would materially affect this analysis.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report considers four main alternatives: (1) Switch investment funds within People's Pension (rejected: no multi-asset funds available, funds do not meet risk profile); (2) Leave pension invested and allow to grow (considered as do-nothing option for Scottish Widows); (3) Switch investment funds within Zurich (rejected: Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, blended approach reduces diversification, poor relative performance, main priority is consolidation); (4) Switch existing schemes into employer's pension arrangement (rejected: would forfeit employer contributions, funds do not meet risk profile, plan 0.06% more expensive, £4.50 annual admin charge). The report recommends Option 5: Set up new Aviva Personal Pension to consolidate five existing arrangements. The pension switch research report ranks solutions by maturity value: Zurich (0.2%, £128,000 - rank 1), Existing Solution (0.3%, £127,000 - rank 2), Aviva (0.5%, £126,000 - rank 3), Standard Life (0.5%, £126,000 - rank 4), New Solution (0.6%, £125,000 - rank 5).

    File:     The pension switch report (P1, P2, P4, P5, P8, P16, P12) provides a detailed alternatives analysis with multiple option comparisons. The research ranking table (P1) places the recommended new solution at rank 5 (0.6% charges, £125,000 maturity value), below the Existing Solution at rank 2 (0.3% charges, £127,000 maturity value) and significantly below Zurich at rank 1 (0.2% charges, £128,000 maturity value). Specific plan-by-plan comparisons show: Zurich-only switch (P2, P8): existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 (9.4% decrease at mid growth); Standard Life-only switch (P5): existing £2,910 vs new solution £2,900 (minimal 0.3% decrease); Aviva-only switch (P4): existing comparable to new. The suitability report (P6, P10, P22) states consolidation is the main priority to gain better control and drawdown flexibility. The report does not explain why a lower-ranked solution (rank 5 at 0.6%) is preferable to a higher-ranked solution (rank 2 Existing at 0.3% or rank 1 Zurich at 0.2%). The report also states for Aviva existing: 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (P10, P22), but the cost analysis tables show Aviva existing at 0.50% and new Aviva at 0.44%, meaning the new is 0.06% LESS expensive, not more. The report asserts benefits of the new solution including lower costs, but the research ranking contradicts this by placing it last. The suitability report (P6) states regarding Zurich switch: 'the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' yet provides no performance evidence supporting that claim in the citation. The report does not address why consolidation alone justifies accepting a lower-ranked outcome.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report recommends Option 5 (New Aviva solution ranked 5th by maturity value at 0.6% charges and £125,000) while research ranking shows Existing Solution at rank 2 (0.3% charges, £127,000) and Zurich at rank 1 (0.2% charges, £128,000) — no explanation given for recommending lower-ranked option

      - Report states Aviva plan 'is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but cost tables show existing Aviva at 0.50% and new at 0.44%, making new 0.06% LESS expensive, not more — the statement contradicts the arithmetic

      - Report claims consolidation is 'main priority' but does not explain why this objective alone justifies lower projected maturity values across multiple comparisons (Zurich switch shows £12,000 or 9.4% decrease)

      - Report cites blended funds at Zurich 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' but no supporting performance analysis is provided in the evidence

      - No explanation why consolidation objective (OBJ3) overrides or should outweigh the lower projected retirement outcome when alternatives exist that achieve control and flexibility without accepting reduced maturity values

    The comparison can be performed and the evidence is available. However, the analysis reveals material inconsistencies between what the report asserts and what its own evidence supports.

   

    First, the research ranking directly contradicts the suitability advice. The pension switch research report (P1) presents a market comparison that ranks solutions by maturity value at mid-growth rates over the same 5-year analysis horizon: Zurich 0.2% (£128,000), Existing 0.3% (£127,000), Aviva and Standard Life 0.5% each, and New Solution 0.6% (£125,000). The recommended new solution ranks last, not first. While the guard permits consolidation as a valid client objective when asked for by the client, the report must still explain why accepting a lower-ranked outcome serves the client's interests better. This explanation is absent. The report identifies consolidation and control as the driver but does not analyse whether the same control could be achieved with a better-ranked option, nor does it quantify the cost of accepting the lower ranking to gain consolidation.

   

    Second, the cost assertion is arithmetically inverted. When discussing the employer scheme (People's Pension) as an alternative, the report states: 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution.' The cost comparison tables show the existing Aviva at 0.50% and the new Aviva Platform at 0.44%, a difference of 0.06%. The statement reverses this: the new solution is 0.06% LESS expensive, not the employer scheme more expensive. This inversion undermines the cost-benefit case for switching.

   

    Third, the performance assertion relies on a citation that does not provide the supporting evidence. The report states that blended funds at Zurich 'have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' (P6). The suitability report provides a performance chart (P9) comparing historical returns of Aviva's existing fund and the recommended Aviva funds against a risk benchmark over 5 years, but this is historical performance of specific funds, not a forward-looking comparison of Zurich blended alternatives versus Aviva alternatives in the market. The citation is to a qualitative statement in the suitability text, not to objective performance data supporting the claim.

   

    Fourth, the alternatives analysis is incomplete for the Zurich option. The report rejects an internal Zurich fund switch because 'your main priority is to consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options' (P6). However, if consolidation is the priority, the question is whether consolidation into the recommended new solution delivers better outcomes than consolidation into the higher-ranked Zurich option (rank 1) or retention of the existing approach (rank 2). The report does not compare consolidation outcomes across options of different ranks; it only compares switching versus not switching within single plans.

   

    The guard allows for consolidation as a valid objective but does not excuse silence about ranking. The report must say why a lower-ranked solution is chosen. Consolidation is a process, not an outcome; the outcome is the projected retirement fund value. The report has ranked the options and the new solution ranks last. That rank should be acknowledged and justified, not hidden behind an objective that does not directly address the ranking result.

   

    For People's Pension, the report states it will be retained (P10), which is contradictory to the stated consolidation objective (OBJ3) to consolidate pensions. The extraction notes flag this as an internal inconsistency: the recommendation is to retain, but the objective is to consolidate.
