  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits justifying switches, including: lower costs (receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure), lifestyling issues (portfolio moving outside agreed risk range), greater investment choice enabling tailored strategy, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund selection, and enhanced retirement flexibility. For the Zurich arrangement, the report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 6), implying cost savings. For the Standard Life/SL arrangement, it claims switching provides 'lower costs' with 'greater investment choice' and 'enhanced retirement flexibility'. However, for the Zurich arrangement specifically, the report also concludes a switch is justified despite stating higher annual charges of £268.94 more per year, asserting 'the benefits of proper risk alignment, consolidation, and active management justify the switch.'

    File:     The pension switching report evidence (P21, P22) shows mixed cost outcomes. For the Aviva/Zurich/Standard Life combined comparison (P22), the new solution has a 'Reduction in yield' of 0.6% versus existing 0.3%, meaning higher ongoing charges. At mid growth rate, the effect of switching is '-£2,000 or 1.6% decrease' in projected maturity value. For Zurich alone (P3, P4), switching to Aviva Platform shows 'Effect of switching' of '-£2,000 or 2.0% decrease' (low growth) to '-£15,000 or 10.0% decrease' (high growth). For Standard Life (P7, P8), the new solution shows identical 0.6% reduction in yield with effects ranging from '-£30 or 0.6% decrease' to '-£10 or 0.2% decrease'. The switching report (P21) notes 'Initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 which is taken from the new solution, reducing the initial investment accordingly. An ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%' but also states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' regarding lost benefits. The report confirms no transfer penalties (P5, P21): 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. Regarding fund choice, provider information (P20, P23) shows the existing Aviva plan 'does not allow adviser charging' and Scottish Widows (P17) 'do not support ongoing advisor charging'. The Zurich policy (P15) shows multiple fund choice questions but no evidence the client found existing fund choice inadequate.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'lower costs' as a benefit but calculated figures show the new solution has higher ongoing charges (0.6% vs 0.3% reduction in yield) for combined comparison

      - Report states for Zurich the plan is '0.06% more expensive than recommended solution' suggesting savings, but computed figures show Zurich charges are 0.93% (fund value £103,439) versus new solution charges at 0.44% (computed from £110,185 value), which is 0.49% cheaper, not 0.06%

      - Report asserts annual charge difference of -£2.25 for one arrangement but computed figures show £268.94 annual charge difference for Zurich arrangement, a material discrepancy in magnitude

      - Report claims 'greater investment choice' as benefit but evidence shows existing Aviva allows only fund switching without adviser management (P20), new Aviva Platform offers more funds but Scottish Widows (being encashed) supported 'as many of the available funds as they wish' (P17), so choice already existed

      - Report states benefits justify switch despite higher costs for Zurich, but pension switching report shows material negative 'Effect of switching' ranging from -£2,000 to -£15,000 in projected maturity value depending on growth rate (P3, P4), with no corresponding quantified benefit offset

      - Report lists 'adviser charging facilitation' as benefit but evidence shows existing Scottish Widows 'do not support ongoing advisor charging' (P17) so this is not a feature being lost; adviser charges of 1.00% annual are imposed on new solution (P21), not a benefit but a cost

      - Report claims 'ongoing monitoring and adjustments' benefit but this is a service feature not a quantifiable financial benefit supported by evidence; no document evidences this monitoring prevents losses or adds value

      - Report states 'multi-asset fund selection' prevents blending risk levels but evidence shows new solution uses exactly this approach: 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced (P2), which is a blend, contradicting the stated benefit of avoiding blends

      - Report claims switching prevents portfolio drifting outside risk range via lifestyling, but evidence does not show what timeline this drift occurs on or whether the benefit materializes before retirement age 75 in 5.5 years

      - Report asserts 'Enhanced retirement flexibility with more flexible retirement options' but no evidence document specifies what flexibility is gained, what existing flexibility was lost, or how this justifies financial detriment

    The requirement tests whether claimed benefits justify claimed costs. The report makes multiple assertions about benefits but the evidence reveals significant tensions. First, on costs: the report states 'lower costs' as the primary benefit but the pension switching report shows the new solution has higher ongoing charges (0.6% reduction in yield vs 0.3% for existing combined portfolio) and negative 'Effect of switching' figures ranging from -£2,000 to -£15,000 depending on growth assumptions. The report's own calculated charge difference of -£2.25 for one arrangement contradicts the computed figure of £268.94 for Zurich, suggesting either mislabeling or calculation error that undermines the cost-benefit case. Second, on investment choice: the report cites 'greater investment choice' and 'multi-asset fund selection' as benefits, but evidence shows (a) existing Scottish Widows allowed multiple fund investment (P17), (b) new solution uses the same blended approach (50/50 split, P2) that the report claims to avoid, and (c) the stated benefit of 'risk level 5 funds' is not evidenced—no document confirms risk level 5 funds exist on the new platform or that the blended approach actually achieves this. Third, on adviser services: the report lists 'adviser charging facilitation' and 'ongoing monitoring' as benefits, but evidence shows these are actually costs imposed on the client (1.00% annual adviser charge, P21; initial £3,305 adviser charge deducted upfront, P21), not existing benefits being retained or enhanced. Fourth, the report acknowledges no benefits were lost from existing products (P21: 'No benefits were listed for the existing products'), which weakens any case that switching avoids losing safeguarded features. Fifth, on the lifestyling issue: the report justifies switching to avoid portfolio drift outside risk range, but provides no evidence about when this drift would occur relative to the retirement horizon (5.5 years to age 75), whether intermediate rebalancing could prevent it, or quantifies the financial impact of drift. The financial detriment is documented (negative effect of switching) but the offsetting benefit is not quantified or time-bound. The guard 'Where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite' does not apply here because the recommended plan is demonstrably not cheaper—it has higher ongoing charges and worse projected maturity values. The analytical gap is material: the report asks the client to accept lower projected retirement outcomes in exchange for intangible benefits (monitoring, flexibility, adviser oversight) that are neither uniquely available nor quantified. The evidence supporting 'benefits' consists mainly of service promises and process improvements rather than financial or feature gains with corroborating documentation.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts five replacement actions with stated justifications: (1) Retain People's Pension workplace scheme citing ongoing employer contributions that would be forfeited if transferred; (2) Encash Scottish Widows citing unavailability of single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5, requiring two funds with 20-30% in cash; (3) Switch Aviva pension citing lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight; (4) Switch Zurich citing greater investment choice, multi-asset fund selection properly aligned with risk rating 5, and lower annual charges (0.49% saving); (5) Switch Standard Life citing avoidance of lifestyling fund drift, fixed risk level 5 portfolio, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, and ongoing adviser oversight, despite higher annual charges of £268.94 per year, which the report claims are justified by benefits of proper risk alignment, consolidation, and active management. The report also states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' as a cost benefit rationale.

    File:     The pension switch report [P5-P21] contains independent analysis comparing existing pensions to new solutions. On Aviva (Zurich pension only comparison) [P9], the projected maturity value for the new Aviva solution is £3,430 versus retained Aviva plan £4,280, showing the switch would decrease value. On the comprehensive comparison [P9], total maturity with new solution is £127,000 compared to existing £127,000 (no improvement stated). The charges analysis reveals: Aviva existing shown as 0.59% [P15], Zurich existing at 0.29% [P9]. File notes [P24] state that for Zurich 'a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used' and 'The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally'. On Scottish Widows, file notes [P24] note the plan was 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility'. Provider literature from Zurich [P3] states 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan', confirming no safeguarded benefits are being lost. Aviva transfer form [P2] warns 'Doing so will mean you will lose any features or guarantees you may have with us', but no specific features or guarantees are documented as existing on these plans. The pension switch report shows a death benefit shortfall of £3,305.00 immediately after switch [P21]. On costs, the report's own charge analysis contains errors: the Zurich pension switch is computed on a £103,438 value (matching Zurich, not Standard Life), and the higher charge claim of £268.94 more per year is computed against Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002 value—meaning the charge comparison for Standard Life switching is unreliable.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims Aviva switch provides 'lower costs' and '0.49% saving'; pension switch report shows Aviva existing 0.59% vs new solution 0.6% reduction of yield, indicating higher not lower costs for the new solution.

      - Report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' while Aviva existing charge is 0.59% and recommendation is 0.6%, making the new plan 0.01% more expensive, not 0.06% cheaper.

      - Report claims higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year) for Standard Life switch are 'justified by benefits'; the charge comparison is computed against Zurich's £103,438 value, not Standard Life's actual £3,002 value, making this comparison unreliable and the cost-benefit claim unsupported.

      - Report asserts Scottish Widows 'does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating of 5'; no evidence in the file confirms or denies what funds Scottish Widows offers. The plan documentation [P22-P23] shows existing funds but does not state what other funds are available.

      - Report claims switching to Aviva on Zurich pension comparison shows 'lower annual charges (0.49% saving)'; pension switch report [P9] shows Zurich existing 0.29% reduction in yield vs new solution 0.6%, indicating the new solution has higher charges, not savings.

      - Report asserts Swiss pension switch provides 'lower costs'; the switch report analysis does not support a cost saving for the Zurich arrangement, contradicting this rationale.

      - Report states retention of People's Pension is justified because 'you and your employer are still contributing to this'; evidence [P20] does not confirm current employer contributions are being made to this plan.

    The report contains multiple material inconsistencies between stated rationales and the evidence in the pension switch report. On costs: the report claims lower charges justify switching Aviva and Zurich, but the switch report indicates the new solutions have equal or higher reduction-in-yield figures than existing arrangements. The stated 0.49% saving on Zurich and 0.06% cheaper claim on Aviva are not corroborated by the switch report's charge figures. Most critically, the charge comparison for Standard Life has been computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's actual value (£3,002), rendering the £268.94 annual charge differential claim and its justification unreliable. The report does not address that switching away from Zurich (0.29% RIY) to the new Aviva solution (0.6% RIY) would increase costs by 0.31 percentage points. On Scottish Widows encashment: the report asserts unavailability of suitable multi-asset funds but provides no evidence from Scottish Widows documentation of what funds are or are not available. File notes record that Scottish Widows was 'discounted' but do not detail fund availability. The report's claim that multi-asset alignment cannot be achieved is unsubstantiated. On People's Pension retention: the rationale hinges on ongoing employer contributions, but no evidence in the file confirms employer contributions are currently being made. The scheme documentation [P20] does not state contribution status. On safeguarded benefits: the report does not document what safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or protections exist on each ceding plan, or what is being forfeited. Provider literature warns transfers mean loss of 'features or guarantees' but the report does not specify what these are for any plan, leaving the materiality of this loss unaddressed. The switch report shows a death benefit shortfall of £3,305 immediately post-switch, which the replacement rationale does not acknowledge or explain. On alternatives: file notes [P24] record alternatives were considered (Aviva fund switch within Zurich, other providers for consolidation) but these are not formally presented as alternatives considered and rejected in the client-facing rationale, only in internal notes. The guard acknowledging file notes on discounting does not excuse the absence of formal alternatives analysis in the recommendation itself.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts that employer contribution will be lost if client transfers away from People's Pension plan, stating 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The replacementAnalysis array includes employerContributionsLost = true for arrangement 0 (People's Pension) and false for arrangements 1-4.     File:     Evidence shows People's Pension received employer contributions historically.     The report identifies loss of employer contribution as a material drawback of transferring People's Pension (arrangement 0), citing page 6. The canonical model flags employerContributionsLost = true for this arrangement. However, the guard applies: 'An employer contribution that stops on leaving service is not lost by the transfer; check whether the client is still an active member.' The evidence shows historical employer contributions to People's Pension (P2) and Standard Life (P21), but does not confirm current active member status, ongoing contribution flow, or employment relationship. The file lacks explicit confirmation from the provider that the client is currently receiving employer contributions or would cease to receive them on transfer. While historical contributions are evidenced, this does not establish that a material current benefit would be forfeited. The assertion relies on an assumption about the state of the client's employment relationship and ongoing contribution arrangement that is not corroborated by the supporting documents. The report discloses the claimed disadvantage but bases it on an unverified premise about current circumstances.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 3 raised a concern (G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The report makes no mention in the canonical model of any adjustmentsMade to support client comprehension, yet the file evidence shows multiple documented support mechanisms: on-demand adviser access, holistic financial planning support, explicit ATR reassessment with documented client agreement, detailed risk capacity explanation, and discussion of emergency reserves. The report's silence on comprehension adjustments stands in contrast to documented evidence that the adviser engaged in active reassessment of the client's understanding (moving from ATR rating 4 to agreed rating 5), discussed capacity for loss in detail, and provided explanations of potential downside risk. No adjustments, communication adaptations, or comprehension support measures are named in the report's own assertions, despite the file showing clear documentation of measures taken. 3 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The canonical model contains no extracted value for adjustmentsMade or characteristics at the vulnerability paths. The report is silent on any adjustments made to support this client's comprehension.

    File:     The file contains evidence of communications and support mechanisms. [P1] records that the adviser provides 'Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required' and 'Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs.' [P1] also advises that 'It would also be prudent to set up and register Power of Attorney for your financial affairs / health and welfare, to ensure that in the event of your incapacity, your spouse or relative could act on your behalf.' [P19] and [P24] show extensive ATR discussions where the adviser explicitly addressed the client's risk understanding: 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' [P19] states 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value.' [P12] notes the report 'is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives - whilst at the same time highlighting any potential disadvantages and risks, as well as any other implications in terms of costs, charges and taxation.' [P16] confirms 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The report makes no mention in the canonical model of any adjustmentsMade to support client comprehension, yet the file evidence shows multiple documented support mechanisms: on-demand adviser access, holistic financial planning support, explicit ATR reassessment with documented client agreement, detailed risk capacity explanation, and discussion of emergency reserves.

      - The report's silence on comprehension adjustments stands in contrast to documented evidence that the adviser engaged in active reassessment of the client's understanding (moving from ATR rating 4 to agreed rating 5), discussed capacity for loss in detail, and provided explanations of potential downside risk.

      - No adjustments, communication adaptations, or comprehension support measures are named in the report's own assertions, despite the file showing clear documentation of measures taken.

    The comparison can be performed because the evidence is present in the file and the canonical model's silence is confirmed by the extraction metadata (expectedButAbsent entries show paths /vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[] and /vulnerability/perClient[]/characteristics[] are absent from the report). This is not an extraction failure; it is report silence. The guard states 'Standard cancellation rights are a regulatory feature, not an adjustment made for this client,' which does not apply here—the evidence describes adviser discretionary support, not regulatory defaults. The file documents multiple support mechanisms: (1) reactive adviser access for ongoing comprehension support [P1]; (2) holistic financial planning tailored to needs [P1]; (3) proactive ATR recalibration where the adviser overrode the questionnaire result and confirmed the client's understanding [P19, P24]; (4) explicit instruction on downside risk [P19]; (5) discussion of capacity for loss tied to financial position and income resilience [P19]; (6) confirmation of liquid reserves [P16]. These are adjustments made to support comprehension—the adviser moved beyond the questionnaire, explained reasoning, confirmed agreement, and ensured the client understood downside. The report itself asserts that it 'is designed to explain...potential disadvantages and risks' [P12], confirming that comprehension support was a stated aim. Yet the canonical model records none of this. The discrepancy is material: the suitability report asserts nothing about how the client's understanding was supported or what adjustments were made, while the file shows documented evidence that multiple such measures were taken. This is not ambiguity; it is absence from the report of support actions that are clearly recorded in the supporting documents.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report identifies six foreseeable harms: (1) market volatility and fund value fluctuations, mitigated by client awareness and multi-asset fund design; (2) underperformance of new funds, mitigated by performance analysis and critical yield assessment showing expected outperformance at 2.94% mid-growth; (3) pension switching costs reducing fund value, mitigated by absence of exit penalties and charge comparison; (4) illiquidity and administration delays, mitigated by £6,000 liquid cash reserves and client comfort with 10-15 working day timescale; (5) loss of employer pension contributions if People's Pension is transferred, mitigated by retention of People's Pension scheme; (6) Inheritance Tax impact from April 2027, mitigated by IHT analysis and monitoring at future reviews. The report records the client's financial position as supportable: employment income £300/week net, state pension £230/week, spouse contribution £1,100/month, capacity for loss described as 'medium' based on income comfortably meeting expenditure with savings and pension funds. Risk rating agreed at 5 (Balanced) after adviser discussion adjusted from questionnaire score of 4. Client confirmed not reliant on pension funds and seeks growth potential. No explicit statement is made that vulnerability characteristics have been assessed as immaterial to the recommendation or that the recommendation remains appropriate when vulnerability factors are considered.

    File:     The case file contains: (1) Risk profile report (P1, P5, P18, P23) recording that the client's natural risk level questionnaire indicated rating 4, agreed to rating 5 'Balanced' after discussion, with no additional notes on investor experience or capacity for loss assessment recorded; (2) Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses (P18) showing the client has 'No understanding/knowledge' of stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning; has invested only in Current Accounts/Savings within last 5 years by themselves; does check valuations annually; (3) Fact Find (P3, P4, P15) recording total net monthly income £1,430, total monthly expenditure £1,718, resulting in monthly disposable income of minus £288, with no expected changes in expenditure; employment income £1,200 net monthly as HGV driver, overtime £230 net (£20/week Sea Cadets role); spouse employment income £1,100 monthly; no recorded vulnerabilities, support needs, communication preferences, or third-party involvement requests; declaration signed with no sensitivity regarding data processing; (4) Intelliflo planning scenarios (P7, P10, P11, P13, P14, P16, P20, P21) showing baseline and proposed asset positions with timeline events 'Illness (John)' at age 100 in 2056 and 'Illness (Kim)' at age 100 in 2056, but no vulnerability indicators, support needs, or capability assessments recorded; planning assumptions show general 2% inflation applied uniformly; (5) File notes (P12) recording existing arrangement risk ratings (Aviva 2, Zurich 7, Standard Life 4) and Defaqto risk ratings all 9, with alternative options noted; (6) Suitability Report (P6, P8, P9, P19, P22) providing the recommendation details but containing no assessment of whether any recorded characteristics materially affect appropriateness, no re-evaluation of recommendation under vulnerability overlay, and no statement addressing whether the Balanced risk rating and concentrated portfolio remain appropriate for a client with low investment knowledge and marginal cash flow (deficit of £288/month).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client 'are aware' and 'confirmed' comfort with investment risks and illiquidity timescales, but file shows 'No understanding/knowledge' of stocks, shares, and pension planning on questionnaire (P18); the client's comprehension baseline is unaddressed in the recommendation.

      - Report states client 'not reliant upon pension funds' but Fact Find shows monthly expenditure £1,718 exceeds net income £1,430 by £288/month (P3); pension drawdown would become necessary to bridge ongoing deficit, contradicting the characterisation of non-reliance.

      - Report characterises capacity for loss as 'medium' justified by income meeting expenditure and liquid reserves, but actual monthly cash flow is negative (P3, P6); stated justification does not align with recorded numbers.

      - Report identifies and mitigates six foreseeable harms but does not assess whether these mitigations are adequate for a client with limited investment knowledge (no understanding of stocks, shares, pension mechanics per questionnaire P18) or whether the Balanced risk level recommendation remains appropriate given that knowledge gap.

      - Report recommends Balanced (risk rating 5) portfolio concentration of 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and 50% CT Universal MAP, but Defaqto risk ratings in file notes show all existing arrangements rated 9, creating ambiguity about whether the multi-asset recommendation is truly lower-risk relative to existing holdings or whether the file's risk context was considered in the recommendation (P12, P22).

      - File shows no recorded vulnerability assessment anywhere (risk profile notes record 'No additional notes' at investor experience and capacity for loss sections P23), yet report asserts mitigations for six specific foreseeable harms; source and justification for these harms identification is not traceable to a documented vulnerability assessment process.

      - Report states ongoing adviser monitoring and review will manage risks, but file contains no documented support needs, communication preferences, or accessibility requirements that would enable such monitoring to be tailored or effective for a client with low investment knowledge.

    The check asks whether information relating to vulnerability, support needs, communication preferences, capacity and comprehension requirements is fully supported by evidence and applied consistently. The overlay is an assessment-time frame: it asks whether the recommendation remains appropriate when vulnerability characteristics are present. The extraction's own report notes that /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] is absent from the canonical model, indicating no statement in the report about whether identified harms affect appropriateness. This is material: the report identifies and mitigates six foreseeable harms (market volatility, underperformance, switching costs, illiquidity, employer contributions, IHT), yet makes no statement that these mitigations, combined with the client's actual knowledge level and cash flow position, render the recommendation suitable. The file shows two material characteristics: (1) the client's questionnaire response 'No understanding/knowledge' of stocks, shares, pension planning, and investment concepts (P18) — this is a capability/comprehension indicator under FG21/1 that goes unaddressed in the report's reasoning; and (2) monthly expenditure exceeds income by £288 (P3), contradicting the report's characterisation of the client as 'not reliant on pension funds' (P6). The fact-find records these objectively. The risk profile records 'No additional notes' on investor experience and capacity for loss (P23), which is technically a record that nothing additional was noted, but it does not constitute a documented assessment that vulnerabilities were considered and found immaterial. The Balanced risk recommendation is justified by the client's stated wish for growth over 10 years (P6), but the file gives no evidence that the adviser addressed the knowledge gap (no understanding of pension planning, stocks, shares) when setting the risk level or when designing the mitigation approach. The £6,000 liquid reserve is recorded as mitigation for illiquidity, but the £288 monthly deficit means this reserve would be depleted within ~21 months under the plan's own assumptions, raising questions about sustainability. Defaqto risk ratings of 9 on all existing holdings (P12) creates ambiguity about the risk profile shift, but the report does not engage with this context. The guards allow for the finding that 'where vulnerability is genuinely absent and that has been assessed and recorded, the overlay adds nothing'. Here, absence is not documented: there is no statement that vulnerability was assessed and found absent. Instead, the risk profile form simply records 'No additional notes', which is a form field left blank rather than a documented conclusion. Under the severity guidance, this is 'High': no vulnerability assessment is visible in the case despite clear FG21/1 drivers (low knowledge/capability, low cash flow resilience, age 69). The overlay asks whether a recommendation appropriate for a resilient client is appropriate for this one. The file does not show that question was asked or answered.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts hasVulnerability = No. No vulnerability categories, characteristics, or assessment date are recorded in the extracted canonical model.

    File:     The file contains multiple passages addressing client circumstances relevant to vulnerability assessment under FG21/1. P9 describes the client as having limited investment experience ('although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'), earning £300 per week net as an HGV driver with state pension of £230 per week, and needing to work to support household spending (total net monthly income £1,430 against expenditure £1,718, producing a monthly deficit of £288). P9 states the client confirmed that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living'. P8 records marital status as Married with spouse age 65. P20 shows the couple's projected life events including illness and death at age 100. P6 shows liquid savings of £6,000. The Fact Find records no accessibility requirements, communication adjustments, or support needs flagged, and no third-party involvement is documented.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report records hasVulnerability = No, but the file evidences multiple FG21/1 drivers: limited financial experience (P9), age 70 with short retirement horizon (P20), earned income of £300/week as HGV driver (P9), household cashflow deficit of £288/month (P7), and modest liquid savings of £6,000 (P6). These are characteristics that under FG21/1 ('low capability' and 'low resilience') would ordinarily trigger vulnerability assessment.

      - The report does not record any explicit vulnerability assessment process, rationale, or conclusion in the extracted model, despite the case carrying identifiable FG21/1 drivers.

    The comparison can be performed. The canonical model records hasVulnerability = No with no supporting categories, characteristics, or assessment date. The file contains sufficient detail to identify potential vulnerability drivers under FCA guidance FG21/1. The client is aged 70, in employment earning modest weekly income (£300 net as HGV driver), with household expenditure (£1,718/month) exceeding net income (£1,430/month), creating a monthly deficit of £288. The client has limited investment experience (acknowledged in P9 as limited to workplace pension). Liquid savings are modest at £6,000. The client's spouse is aged 65. Retirement is projected within 2 years. These circumstances—low income resilience, high expenditure relative to income, age, limited investment experience, and short-term life transitions—align with FG21/1 characteristics that warrant vulnerability assessment. The file records no explicit assessment of vulnerability, no documented decision to rate the client as non-vulnerable, and no recorded rationale for such a conclusion. The fact that the client confirmed underperformance would not impact day-to-day living (P9) does not eliminate vulnerability; rather, it documents one aspect of the client's stated resilience, but does not substitute for a recorded assessment. The report's assertion of No vulnerability is not supported by evidence of a completed assessment process, and the file's documented circumstances suggest the question was not properly considered or recorded. This is a material gap: where clear FG21/1 drivers exist, absence of a recorded assessment constitutes a Potential Concern under the decision logic.
